 function initializeReadingPreferences() {
 let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
 if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
 if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
 saved_theme = "dark";
 }
 }
 if (saved_theme == "dark") {
 document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
 } else {
 document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
 }

 const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
 if (tocDisplay) {
 document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
 }
 const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
 if (readingMode) {
 document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
 }
 }

 Conditional contraction coefficients and their applications to quantum networks 
 
 
 Christoph Hirche

 August 21, 2026 

 Abstract 
 
 Contraction coefficients quantify the loss of distinguishability induced by a channel and provide a strong form of the data-processing inequality. While standard contraction coefficients ignore auxiliary quantum systems, existing extensions based on complete contraction coefficients require the compared states to have identical reference marginals. In this work, we introduce conditional contraction coefficients, a novel family that incorporates arbitrary quantum reference systems by subtracting the distinguishability already present in the reference system. We develop a general framework for contraction coefficients with such quantum side information, including the corresponding strong-data-processing-inequality (SDPI) constants, expansion coefficients, and relative contraction coefficients. For the trace distance, we show that the optimization can be restricted to orthogonal input states. For the quantum relative entropy, we prove that its conditional contraction coefficient is exactly equal to the contraction coefficient of the conditional mutual information, extending the classical correspondence between relative-entropy contraction and mutual-information contraction to the setting with quantum side information. More generally, we identify structural properties of divergences required for these results and discuss extensions beyond the relative entropy. These results establish a unified framework for analyzing information contraction in quantum network settings, where quantum side information and distributed correlations are intrinsic features of the information-processing task. Applications include an extension of the Polyanskiy–Wu bounds on mutual information contraction, new perspectives on mixing times, and fundamental limits on quantum memories.

 1 Introduction 

 
 1.1 Background and motivation 

 
 Contraction coefficients give a strengthening of the data-processing inequality for divergences and are fundamental to our understanding of how systems behave under noise. Given a quantum channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to B}, they provide a universal bound on the loss of distinguishability of 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A})\text{ versus }\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A}) 
 
 (1.1) 
 
 for all quantum states \rho_{A} and \sigma_{A}. Due to their importance, they have been thoroughly investigated in the literature  [ 66 , 57 , 48 , 65 , 33 , 37 , 38 , 31 , 22 ] . Applications range from quantum computing  [ 25 ] , error mitigation  [ 72 , 61 ] , differential privacy  [ 17 , 55 ] , quantum capacities  [ 4 ] , and mixing times  [ 31 ] . For a given divergence \mathbb{D}, the contraction coefficient is formally defined as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{A}\neq\sigma_{A}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}\|\sigma_{A})}. 
 
 (1.2) 
 
 
 
 What is not covered by this setting is the presence of quantum correlations beyond the input system of the channel. We are often interested in the loss of distinguishability of 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle(\operatorname{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\text{ versus }(\operatorname{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}) 
 
 (1.3) 
 
 for all quantum states \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA}. However, one can easily find two different states, for example \rho_{RA}=\rho_{R}\otimes\tau_{A} and \sigma_{RA}=\sigma_{R}\otimes\tau_{A}, such that their distinguishability is not affected by the channel. The idea here is simply that all available distinguishability is due entirely to the additional reference system. As a result, a naive extension of contraction coefficients to include reference systems, simply by considering \mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N}, generally leads to a trivial bound  [ 37 ] . 
 
 
 A remedy is to restrict the states to be identical on the reference system. The result is the complete contraction coefficient  [ 30 , Sec. 6] , motivated by related complete/tensor-stable relative-entropy inequalities developed in  [ 27 , 28 ] , defined as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})}. 
 
 (1.4) 
 
 Note that the optimization here is over a potentially unbounded reference system, making this quantity more challenging to work with. 
 
 
 Interestingly, the need for such quantities is a purely quantum phenomenon for certain divergences.
Indeed, if we assume that R is a classical system and that \mathbb{D} has the extended direct-sum property, then the complete contraction coefficient and the usual one are equal (see Appendix   A ). Nevertheless, in the quantum setting, such complete contraction coefficients provide a way of handling reference systems. However, the limitation to equal marginals is constraining and often not reflected in practical applications. 
 
 
 In what follows, we will introduce and discuss an alternative notion of contraction coefficients that takes into account reference systems without the restriction of equal marginals. 
 
 
 
 1.2 Summary of contributions 

 
 In this paper, we propose an alternative notion of a non-trivial contraction coefficient that circumvents the restrictions discussed above. To this end, we define the conditional contraction coefficient of a quantum channel \mathcal{N} as follows: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}. 
 
 (1.5) 
 
 Here, the reference system is not artificially restricted, and instead we appropriately take into account the distinguishability that it provides. This concept is somewhat similar to the idea of amortized channel divergences  [ 80 ] , in which a reference system can aid the distinguishability. The difference of divergences in the numerator and denominator of ( 1.5 ) also appeared under the name “conditional relative entropy” in  [ 14 ] . If we again restrict the optimization to states with equal marginals, we recover the complete contraction coefficient, thus implying the bound \eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 
 Similar to the complete contraction coefficient, the need for reference systems in the conditional contraction coefficient is a purely quantum phenomena for certain divergences. Indeed, if the reference system is restricted to be classical and the divergence obeys the extended direct-sum property, then the usual contraction coefficient and the conditional one are equal (see Appendix   A ). 
 
 
 A different notion of contraction coefficient involves correlation measures instead of divergences, in particular, the mutual information. Such contraction coefficients are closely related to the usual contraction coefficients for the relative entropy. In particular, it is known that  [ 78 , 37 ] 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N})=\sup_{\rho_{UA}}\frac{I(U:B)}{I(U:A)}, 
 
 (1.6) 
 
 where we chose \mathbb{D} to be the quantum relative entropy  [ 75 ] , the latter denoted by D.
Here U is a classical system, the channel \mathcal{N} has input system A and output system B, and the supremum is over every classical-quantum state \rho_{UA}. The notation I(U:B) denotes the mutual information of the output state and I(U:A) the mutual information of the input state. Hence, the contraction of the mutual information is identical to that of the relative entropy. 
 
 
 Since the contraction coefficient in ( 1.6 ) does not capture quantum correlations, one might rather consider fully quantum states or even the conditional mutual information (CMI), incorporating another quantum reference system. Such contractions have been considered, for example, in  [ 16 ] .
One of our main results states that the CMI contraction is indeed the information counterpart to the conditional contraction coefficient \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}); concretely, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})=\sup_{\rho_{RCA}}\frac{I(C:B|R)}{I(C:A|R)}. 
 
 (1.7) 
 
 Interestingly, for this result, it does not matter whether C is a quantum system, a classical system, or even restricted to be a binary classical system. 
 
 
 In addition to the equality in ( 1.7 ), we extensively discuss these and other relations between divergence and mutual information based contraction. For a summary of these results, refer to  Fig.   1 and  Corollary   3 . We then also extend these results to divergences beyond the relative entropy. 
 
 \eta_{D} \eta_{D}^{p} \eta^{c}_{D} \eta^{\Delta}_{D} no reference product reference \rho_{R}=\sigma_{R} arbitrary \rho_{R},\sigma_{R} \leq \leq \leq \eta_{\mathrm{cqMI}} \eta_{\mathrm{MI}} \eta_{\mathrm{MIR}} \eta_{\mathrm{CMI}} [ 37 , Prop. 6.2] Lem.  5 Cor.  2 Cor.  1 
 Figure 1: 
Relations between the contraction coefficients considered in this paper
for the relative entropy. Arrows denote inequalities and double lines
denote equalities. References indicate the corresponding results in the
paper. The depicted inequalities are summarized in  Corollary   3 .
 
 
 
 Many of the results described above also hold for the strong-data-processing-inequality (SDPI) constants, formally defined in  Definition   1 , where the second state is fixed and not subject to optimization. A general SDPI constant is denoted by \eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma), where \mathcal{N} is a channel and \sigma is a fixed state. One question that is of particular interest for SDPI constants is whether the tensorization property holds, namely whether 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})\overset{?}{=}\max\{\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\} 
 
 (1.8) 
 
 holds for channels \mathcal{N}_{1} and \mathcal{N}_{2} and states \sigma_{1} and \sigma_{2}. This is true for many classical divergences  [ 63 ] , but is not in general in the quantum setting  [ 13 ] . In  Section   4 , we consider the special case of relative entropy SDPI constants and give an approximate tensorization result that leads to exact tensorization for several special cases, including the generalized depolarizing channel with respect to its fixed point. 
 
 
 Often it is sensible to consider tensorization in the presence of reference systems. In fact, Ref.  [ 27 ] showed that tensorization indeed holds for a particular definition of relative entropy contraction; we refer to  Section   4 for details. We show here that a similar result is equivalent to tensorization of the mutual information SDPI constant, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})=\max\{\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{\operatorname{MI}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}. 
 
 (1.9) 
 
 Even more generally, using a conditional SDPI constant, we show that tensorization also holds for the conditional mutual information, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{CMI}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})=\max\{\eta_{\operatorname{CMI}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{\operatorname{CMI}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}. 
 
 (1.10) 
 
 Thus, our paper significantly extends the understanding of tensorization of SDPI constants beyond the quantities usually considered in the literature. 
 
 
 Another relevant divergence in the context of contraction coefficients is the trace distance. In particular, its contraction has the favorable property that it is optimized by orthogonal states. We show that this indeed holds also in the presence of reference systems. That is, for the conditional contraction coefficient, the following equality holds: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{RA}\perp\bar{\tau}_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\bar{\tau}_{RA}))-E_{1}(\tau_{R}\|\bar{\tau}_{R})}{1-E_{1}(\tau_{R}\|\bar{\tau}_{R})}. 
 
 (1.11) 
 
 Considering instead the more general hockey-stick divergence we give an example demonstrating that the conditional contraction coefficient can be strictly larger than the complete contraction coefficient, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{E_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{N})>\eta^{c}_{E_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{N}), 
 
 (1.12) 
 
 even in the classical setting. 
 
 
 Next, we consider applications of our extended contraction framework.
First, we discuss contraction in network settings. As our main result in this context, we generalize a bound from  [ 60 ] to the quantum setting. For a network channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to BE}=\mathcal{R}_{D\to E}\circ\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD}, we prove the following inequality: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{A\to BE}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD})+(1-\eta_{\mathcal{R}})\cdot\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to B}). 
 
 (1.13) 
 
 We follow up with implications of this inequality, including novel bounds on the contraction coefficients of tensor-product channels.
The second main application is the introduction of replacing time , as a generalization of mixing time. It is defined by 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle t_{\operatorname{rep}}^{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\delta)\coloneqq\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\begin{matrix}\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\leq\delta\,,\\
\forall R\,,\,\forall\rho_{RA},\sigma_{RA}\in\mathcal{D}(R\otimes A)\end{matrix}\right\} 
 
 (1.14) 
 
 and we give bounds in terms of the conditional contraction coefficient and show that replacing is asymptotically equivalent to mixing. 
 
 
 Finally, we give some applications to bounding storage times in quantum memories and limitations in quantum machine learning. 
 
 
 
 1.3 Paper organization 

 
 The remainder of our paper is organized as follows. First, we establish various definitions and prove some general properties in  Section   2 . Here we also discuss expansion coefficients and relative contraction coefficients. In  Section   3 , we focus on the relative entropy and, in particular, its relation to the mutual information and the CMI. We also extend the results to divergences that are structurally similar to the relative entropy, such as f-divergences. In  Section   4 , we continue with the relative entropy and discuss tensorization of SDPI constants, providing our exact and approximate tensorization results.
In  Section   5 , we specialize the investigation to the trace distance and, more generally, the hockey-stick divergence.
We study the applications of our new contraction coefficients in quantum networks, discrete-time quantum Markov chains, and quantum machine learning, in  Section   6 ,  Section   7 , and  Section   8 , respectively.
Finally, we conclude in  Section   9 and briefly discuss some open problems. 
 
 
 
 
 2 Contraction with reference systems 

 
 In this section, we begin by recalling quantum divergences and their properties ( Section   2.1 ). After that, we introduce generalized SDPI constants and contraction coefficients, including our new definition of conditional contraction coefficients and restricted coefficients with a product constraint on the reference system ( Section   2.2 ). Section   2.3 then establishes various properties of these contraction coefficients. In  Section   2.5 , we introduce expansion coefficients, and finally, we introduce relative contraction and expansion coefficients, in which two channels are compared ( Section   2.6 ). 
 
 
 2.1 Quantum divergences 

 
 First, we recall some basic definitions for quantum divergences.
We define a quantum divergence \mathbb{D} to be a function that maps two quantum states to a real number and satisfies the data-processing inequality: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\rho\|\sigma)\geq\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho)\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma)). 
 
 (2.1) 
 
 Sometimes we need additional properties beyond this basic statement. 
 
 
 A divergence \mathbb{D} is non-negative if 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\rho\|\sigma)\geq 0 
 
 (2.2) 
 
 for all states \rho and \sigma, and we say that a non-negative divergence is faithful if 
 

 
 
 \mathbb{D}(\rho\|\sigma)=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\rho=\sigma. 
 
 (2.3) 
 
 
 
 A divergence \mathbb{D} is additive if 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}\|\sigma_{A}\otimes\sigma_{B})=\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}\|\sigma_{A})+\mathbb{D}(\rho_{B}\|\sigma_{B}), 
 
 (2.4) 
 
 for all states \rho_{A}, \rho_{B}, \sigma_{A}, and \sigma_{B}. 
 
 
 We say that \mathbb{D} has the direct-sum property if the following equality holds for all classical-quantum states \rho_{XA}=\sum_{x}p(x)|x\rangle\!\langle x|_{X}\otimes\rho^{x}_{A} and \sigma_{XA}=\sum_{x}p(x)|x\rangle\!\langle x|_{X}\otimes\sigma^{x}_{A}: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\rho_{XA}\|\sigma_{XA})=\sum_{x}p(x)\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}^{x}\|\sigma_{A}^{x}). 
 
 (2.5) 
 
 
 
 A divergence \mathbb{D} is jointly convex if the following inequality holds for all \lambda\in[0,1] and states \rho_{0}, \rho_{1}, \sigma_{0}, and \sigma_{1}: 
 

 
 
 (1-\lambda)\mathbb{D}(\rho_{0}\|\sigma_{0})+\lambda\mathbb{D}(\rho_{1}\|\sigma_{1})\geq\mathbb{D}((1-\lambda)\rho_{0}+\lambda\rho_{1}\|(1-\lambda)\sigma_{0}+\lambda\sigma_{1}). 
 
 (2.6) 
 
 A divergence that obeys the direct-sum property is jointly convex, as a consequence of ( 2.5 ) and data processing under partial trace over the classical register X. 
 
 
 We will introduce further more specific properties where needed. 
 
 
 
 2.2 Contraction coefficients 

 
 We now introduce various notions of contraction coefficient. Here we distinguish between different settings, depending on which states are known. If we want an improvement in the data-processing inequality for all states in the first argument, but fixed second argument, then we consider SDPI constants of the form 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{A}\neq\sigma_{A}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}\|\sigma_{A})}. 
 
 (2.7) 
 
 If we want an improvement to hold when both inputs are arbitrary, we consider contraction coefficients, as defined in ( 1.2 ). They can be phrased as optimized SDPI constants: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})=\sup_{\sigma}\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{A}\neq\sigma_{A}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}\|\sigma_{A})}. 
 
 (2.8) 
 
 
 
 We now define versions of these that explicitly include reference systems, as initiated in  [ 28 ] for the relative entropy. Let us begin with the SDPI constants. 
 
 
 Definition 1 (Generalized SDPI constants) . 
 
 For a quantum channel \mathcal{N}, a state \sigma_{RA}, and a generalized divergence \mathbb{D}, we define the SDPI constant with product reference as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A})}, 
 
 (2.9) 
 
 the complete SDPI constant as  [ 30 ] 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})}, 
 
 (2.10) 
 
 and the conditional SDPI constant as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}. 
 
 (2.11) 
 
 
 
 
 Similarly, we define contraction coefficients with reference systems. 
 
 
 Definition 2 (Generalized contraction coefficient) . 
 
 For a quantum channel \mathcal{N} and a generalized divergence \mathbb{D}, we define the contraction coefficient with product reference as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA},\,\sigma_{A}:\\
\rho_{RA}\neq\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A})}, 
 
 (2.12) 
 
 the complete contraction coefficient as  [ 30 ] 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})}, 
 
 (2.13) 
 
 and the conditional contraction coefficient as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}. 
 
 (2.14) 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 1 . 
 
 Extending the notion of conditional relative entropy from  [ 14 ] , we can define the generalized conditional relative entropy of states \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA} as follows: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{A}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})\coloneqq\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}). 
 
 (2.15) 
 
 Using this notation, the conditional contraction coefficient can alternatively be expressed as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}_{B}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))}{\mathbb{D}_{A}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})}. 
 
 (2.16) 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 2 . 
 
 We note here that the notation used above for \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) and \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}) is incomplete. The optimization is should be over states such that \infty>\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})>0, which is a strictly stronger constraint than just \rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA}. However, for notational brevity, we will keep using the latter notation. As a general rule, in this paper, all ratio optimizations are over strictly positive and finite denominators. If no admissible state with strictly positive finite denominator exists, the corresponding contraction coefficient is defined to be zero. 
 
 
 
 
 2.3 Properties of contraction coefficients 

 
 In this section, we will first discuss some general properties of the definitions from Section   2.2 for arbitrary divergences, before specializing to particular divergences in the following sections. 
 
 
 The following inequalities hold for every non-negative divergence, as defined in ( 2.2 ), 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle 0\leq\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq 1. 
 
 (2.17) 
 
 Furthermore, the following bounds hold for a concatenation of channels. 
 
 
 Lemma 1 . 
 
 For two quantum channels \mathcal{N} and \mathcal{M} and a state \sigma_{RA}, the following hold for the SDPI constants, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{N},\sigma) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}(\sigma))\;\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma), 
 
 (2.18) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{N},\sigma) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}(\sigma))\;\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma), 
 
 (2.19) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{N},\sigma) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}(\sigma))\;\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma), 
 
 (2.20) 
 
 where \mathcal{N}(\sigma)\equiv(\operatorname{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}).
Hence the following hold for the contraction coefficients: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M})\;\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}), 
 
 (2.21) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M})\;\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}), 
 
 (2.22) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M})\;\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (2.23) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We show the second inequality. Consider that 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes(\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{N}))(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes(\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{N}))(\sigma_{RA})) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}(\sigma))\;\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA})) 
 
 (2.24) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}(\sigma))\;\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma)\;\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA}), 
 
 (2.25) 
 
 where both inequalities hold by the definition of the SDPI constant. The other two claims for SDPI constants follows from a similar proof. Those for contraction coefficients then follow by optimizing over \sigma_{RA}.
∎ 
 
 
 
 If the underlying divergence is also additive, then the behavior of the contraction coefficients under tensor products is similar to that found previously  [ 37 , Lemma 3.8] . 
 
 
 Lemma 2 . 
 
 For quantum channels \mathcal{N} and \mathcal{M}, states \sigma_{R_{1}A_{1}} and \sigma_{R_{2}A_{2}}, and a generalized divergence \mathbb{D} that is additive on tensor-product states, the following hold for the SDPI constants: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{A_{2}}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\max\{\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\sigma_{A_{1}}),\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A_{2}})\}, 
 
 (2.26) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N},\sigma_{R_{1}A_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{R_{2}A_{2}}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\max\{\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\sigma_{R_{1}A_{1}}),\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{R_{2}A_{2}})\}, 
 
 (2.27) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N},\sigma_{R_{1}A_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{R_{2}A_{2}}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\max\{\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\sigma_{R_{1}A_{1}}),\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{R_{2}A_{2}})\}, 
 
 (2.28) 
 
 and the following hold for the contraction coefficients: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\max\{\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}),\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\}, 
 
 (2.29) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\max\{\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}),\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\}, 
 
 (2.30) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\max\{\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M}),\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\}. 
 
 (2.31) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For the complete SDPI constant, the proof follows by restricting the supremum over \rho_{R_{1}A_{1}R_{2}A_{2}} to states of the form either \rho_{R_{1}A_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{R_{2}A_{2}} or \sigma_{R_{1}A_{1}}\otimes\rho_{R_{2}A_{2}}, and similar for the conditional case and that with product reference.
Here we use that \mathbb{D}(\rho\otimes\tau\|\sigma\otimes\tau)=\mathbb{D}(\rho\|\sigma), which is also a consequence of data processing; see, for example  [ 81 , Eq. (9)] .
For the contraction coefficients, the claim follows by restricting both states in the optimization to product states where one channel input gets assigned a fixed input state in both cases.
∎ 
 
 
 
 While these tensorization inequalities for contraction coefficients are usually strict, in the case of SDPI constants achievability depends on the exact definition used. We discuss this further in  Section   4 . 
 
 
 The contraction coefficients are also convex if the divergence is jointly convex (see ( 2.6 )). 
 
 
 Lemma 3 . 
 
 For a jointly convex divergence \mathbb{D}, \lambda\in[0,1], channels \mathcal{N}_{1} and \mathcal{N}_{2}, and a channel \mathcal{N}_{\lambda}\equiv\lambda\mathcal{N}_{1}+(1-\lambda)\mathcal{N}_{0}, the following inequalities hold: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\lambda\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1})+(1-\lambda)\eta^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{0}), 
 
 (2.32) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\lambda\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1})+(1-\lambda)\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{0}), 
 
 (2.33) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\lambda\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1})+(1-\lambda)\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{0}). 
 
 (2.34) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The properties follow directly from the joint convexity of the divergence.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 2.4 Quantum Doeblin coefficient upper bounds on contraction coefficients 

 
 Contraction coefficients are generally difficult to compute, and so upper bounds are also of interest. For this purpose, Refs.  [ 39 , 30 ] employed quantum Doeblin coefficients, extending the original development in [ 85 , Theorem 8.17 and Eq. (8.86)] . Here we use the following definition: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup\{\epsilon\in[0,1]:\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}\succeq_{\operatorname{deg}}\mathcal{N}\}, 
 
 (2.35) 
 
 where \mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\rho)\coloneqq(1-\epsilon)\rho+\epsilon|e\rangle\!\langle e| is the quantum erasure channel and \mathcal{N} can be degraded from \mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}, denoted \mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}\succeq_{\operatorname{deg}}\mathcal{N}, if there exists a quantum channel \mathcal{D} such that \mathcal{N}=\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}. Note that the Doeblin coefficient is a semidefinite program (SDP)  [ 39 ] .
Additionally, we will later use the following expression. 
 
 
 Proposition 1 ( [ 39 , Proposition III.3] ) . 
 
 For a quantum channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to B}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N})=\max\{\epsilon:\exists\mathcal{D}\in\operatorname{CPTP}(A,B)\text{ s.t. }\mathcal{N}=(1-\epsilon)\mathcal{D}+\epsilon\mathcal{R}_{\tau}\}\ , 
 
 (2.36) 
 
 where \mathcal{R}_{\tau}(X)=\tau\operatorname{Tr}[X] and \tau is a quantum state. 
 
 
 
 We then have the following result. 
 
 
 Proposition 2 . 
 
 For a jointly convex divergence \mathbb{D}, the following inequality holds: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (2.37) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By  Proposition   1 , we have that \mathcal{N}=(1-\epsilon)\mathcal{D}+\epsilon\mathcal{R}_{\tau} with \epsilon=\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}). Hence, we get the following, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq(1-\epsilon)\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{D})+\epsilon\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{R}_{\tau})\leq 1-\epsilon=1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}), 
 
 (2.38) 
 
 where the first inequality follows from  Lemma   3 and the second uses \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{D})\leq 1 and \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{R}_{\tau})=0, which completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The bound in  Proposition   2 above represents an improvement over the previous bound from [ 30 , Proposition 31 and Corollary 7] . Indeed, there we proved, for some choices of \mathbb{D}, that \eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}). However, since \eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}), it then follows that \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}) is a tighter lower bound on 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}) than \eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}). Additionally, we have proven the claim rather generally for every conditional contraction coefficient based on a divergence that is jointly convex. 
 
 
 
 2.5 Expansion coefficients 

 
 In contrast to contraction coefficients, one may also ask if a channel preserves at least a fixed amount of information. To that end, expansion coefficients were introduced  [ 64 , 39 , 46 ] and defined as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{A}\neq\sigma_{A}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}\|\sigma_{A})}. 
 
 (2.39) 
 
 A natural extension is to introduce a reference system also here. Hence, we define the following generalized expansion coefficients. 
 
 
 Definition 3 (Generalized expansion coefficient) . 
 
 For a quantum channel \mathcal{N} and a generalized divergence \mathbb{D}, we define the expansion coefficient with product reference as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA},\sigma_{A}:\\
\rho_{RA}\neq\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A})}, 
 
 (2.40) 
 
 the complete expansion coefficient as  [ 30 ] 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})}, 
 
 (2.41) 
 
 and the conditional expansion coefficient as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}. 
 
 (2.42) 
 
 
 
 
 Also here, one could additionally define SDPI-like versions by keeping the second state fixed. 
 
 
 An important note to make here is that the usefulness of these coefficients depends strongly on the choice of divergence. It was found that even the standard expansion coefficient is often trivial (i.e., equal to zero), for many divergences  [ 39 , 46 , 4 , 40 ] . Opposite to ( 2.17 ), the following inequalities hold: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\check{\eta}^{p}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\geq\check{\eta}^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\geq\check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\geq 0, 
 
 (2.43) 
 
 implying that our generalized expansion coefficients with reference systems are equal to zero for the scenarios considered in  [ 39 , 46 , 4 , 40 ] . One common case where such expansion coefficients remain generally interesting is when they are defined in terms of the trace distance  [ 39 ] . 
 
 
 
 2.6 Relative contraction and expansion coefficients 

 
 The goal of relative contraction coefficients is to compare the data-processing strength of different channels. Such quantities appeared classically as early as  [ 50 , Eq. (43)] . The idea was then picked up in the quantum setting in  [ 37 ] , but only investigated in much more detail in  [ 4 ] ; see also  [ 40 ] . Related recent work in the classical setting can also be found in  [ 71 ] . 
 
 
 We generalize this concept to the setting with reference systems here. 
 
 
 Definition 4 (Generalized relative contraction coefficient) . 
 
 For quantum channels \mathcal{N} and \mathcal{M} and a generalized divergence \mathbb{D}, we define the relative complete contraction coefficient as follows: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))}{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\sigma_{RA}))}, 
 
 (2.44) 
 
 and the relative conditional contraction coefficient as follows: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}. 
 
 (2.45) 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 5 (Generalized relative expansion coefficient) . 
 
 For quantum channels \mathcal{N} and \mathcal{M} and a generalized divergence \mathbb{D}, we define the relative complete expansion coefficient as follows: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))}{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\sigma_{RA}))}, 
 
 (2.46) 
 
 and the relative conditional expansion coefficient as follows: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}. 
 
 (2.47) 
 
 
 
 
 Similar to what was observed in [ 4 , after Eq. (4)] , relative expansion and relative contraction coefficients are related by 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) 
 \displaystyle=\left(\check{\eta}^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\right)^{-1}, 
 
 (2.48) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) 
 \displaystyle=\left(\check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N})\right)^{-1}. 
 
 (2.49) 
 
 Furthermore, such coefficients have proven useful in the study of quantum capacities  [ 4 ] . 
 
 
 In the next sections, we will dive deeper into investigating these quantities for specific divergences. 
 
 
 
 
 3 Relative entropy-like divergences 

 
 In this section, we will discuss contraction of primarily the relative entropy and the connection to contraction of related quantities such as the mutual information and conditional mutual information. However, we will also see that we only need very few properties of the relative entropy for most of the results, and we will later discuss similar divergences that possess the same properties. 
 
 
 3.1 (Conditional) mutual information contraction 

 
 Mutual information and conditional mutual information are correlation measures that have numerous applications in information theory and can be written in terms of the relative entropy. It is hence natural that the data-processing properties of mutual information and relative entropy are tightly connected. Without reference systems, several results in this direction are known  [ 37 ] . Here, we extend the results to the setting with reference systems, which in particular allows us to incorporate the conditional mutual information. Unless stated otherwise, we adopt the shorthand convention 
 

 
 
 \mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\equiv(\operatorname{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{A\to B})(\rho_{RA}) 
 
 (3.1) 
 
 in all that follows. 
 
 
 The quantum relative entropy of states \rho and \sigma is defined as [ 75 ] 
 

 
 
 D(\rho\|\sigma)\coloneqq\operatorname{Tr}[\rho(\log\rho-\log\sigma)] 
 
 (3.2) 
 
 when \operatorname{supp}(\rho)\subseteq\operatorname{supp}(\sigma) and it is equal to +\infty otherwise.
The mutual information and the conditional mutual information are respectively defined for a tripartite state \rho_{ABC} as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(A:B) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq D(\rho_{AB}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}), 
 
 (3.3) 
 
 
 \displaystyle I(A:B|C) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq I(A:BC)-I(A:C) 
 
 (3.4) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=D(\rho_{ABC}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{BC})-D(\rho_{AC}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{C}). 
 
 (3.5) 
 
 There are many ways to define meaningful contraction coefficients for the mutual information, some of which explicitly choose certain subsystems as classical. We list several of these possibilities below. 
 
 
 Definition 6 . 
 
 Given a channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to B}, we define the following mutual-information and conditional-mutual-information contraction coefficients: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{CA}}\frac{I(C:B)}{I(C:A)}, 
 
 (3.6) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{UA}}\frac{I(U:B)}{I(U:A)}, 
 
 (3.7) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{2cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{UA},\\
|U|=2\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:B)}{I(U:A)}, 
 
 (3.8) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{RCA}}\frac{I(C:B|R)}{I(C:A|R)}, 
 
 (3.9) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{RUA}}\frac{I(U:B|R)}{I(U:A|R)}, 
 
 (3.10) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{2cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
|U|=2\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:B|R)}{I(U:A|R)}, 
 
 (3.11) 
 
 where \rho_{CBR}\coloneqq\mathcal{N}_{A\to B}(\rho_{RCA}) and U is always a classical system, meaning that \rho_{UA} and \rho_{RUA} are classical-quantum states. 
 
 
 
 It was shown in  [ 37 , Proposition 6.2] that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (3.12) 
 
 However, upon reexamination, the proof there actually shows something stronger, namely, that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{2cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (3.13) 
 
 This is a, perhaps surprising, simplification of the contraction coefficient, which was previously remarked upon in the classical literature; see  [ 1 , Footnote 5] . We refrain from giving a proof here because it is the same as that given for  [ 37 , Proposition 6.2] , while noting that |U|=2 always suffices for one direction of the proof. 
 
 
 Instead, we consider a more novel setting with the goal of extending previous results to the conditional mutual information and connect that to the conditional contraction coefficient. 
 
 
 Theorem 1 . 
 
 Let \mathcal{M}\colon A\to B and \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B^{\prime} be quantum channels, \sigma_{RA} a quantum state, and \eta\geq 0. Then the following are equivalent: 
 
 (i) 
 
 For all c-q states \rho_{RUA} with marginal \sigma_{RA} , where U is a classical system of dimension |U|=2 , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta I(U:B|R)\geq I(U:B^{\prime}|R). 
 
 (3.14) 
 
 
 (ii) 
 
 For all c-q states \rho_{RUA} with marginal \sigma_{RA} , where U is an arbitrary classical system, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta I(U:B|R)\geq I(U:B^{\prime}|R). 
 
 (3.15) 
 
 
 (iii) 
 
 For every quantum state \rho_{RA} satisfying \operatorname{supp}(\rho_{RA})\subseteq\operatorname{supp}(\sigma_{RA}) , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta\left[D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\right]\geq D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}). 
 
 (3.16) 
 
 
 
 In ( 3.14 )–( 3.15 ), the conditional mutual informations on the left-hand side are evaluated with respect to \mathcal{M}(\rho) and those on the right-hand side with respect to \mathcal{N}(\rho). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof for (ii)\Rightarrow(i) is obvious, given that the set of states in (ii) contains those in (i).
The direction (iii)\Rightarrow(ii) follows easily by the direct-sum property. 
 
 
 We now focus on the proof of (i)\Rightarrow(iii). Without loss of generality we assume that \sigma_{RA} is a full-rank state. Then, for every \rho_{RA}, and 0\leq\lambda\leq\epsilon, where \epsilon is small enough such that \sigma_{RA}-\epsilon\rho_{RA}\geq 0, we let U\equiv U_{\lambda} be a binary random variable with distribution P_{U_{\lambda}}(0)=\lambda and P_{U_{\lambda}}(1)=1-\lambda, and we define states \rho_{RA}^{0}\coloneqq\rho_{RA} and \rho_{RA}^{1}(\lambda)\coloneqq(1-\lambda)^{-1}(\sigma_{RA}-\lambda\rho_{RA}). Defining 
 

 
 
 \rho_{URA}\coloneqq\lambda|0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{U}\otimes\rho^{0}_{RA}+(1-\lambda)|1\rangle\!\langle 1|_{U}\otimes\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda), 
 
 (3.17) 
 
 we conclude that \mathop{\operatorname{Tr}}\nolimits_{U}(\rho_{RUA})=\sigma_{RA}. Then, under the assumption that (i) holds and using the direct-sum property of quantum relative entropy, define 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\varphi(\lambda) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\eta I(U:B|R)-I(U:B^{\prime}|R) 
 
 (3.18) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta\left[I(U:BR)-I(U:R)\right]-\left[I(U:B^{\prime}R)-I(U:R)\right] 
 
 (3.19) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta\left[D(\rho_{UBR}\|\rho_{U}\otimes\rho_{BR})-D(\rho_{UR}\|\rho_{U}\otimes\rho_{R})\right] 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\qquad-\left[D(\rho_{UB^{\prime}R}\|\rho_{U}\otimes\rho_{B^{\prime}R})-D(\rho_{UR}\|\rho_{U}\otimes\rho_{R})\right] 
 
 (3.20) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta\left[\lambda D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))+(1-\lambda)D(\mathcal{M}(\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda))\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))\right. 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\qquad\left.-\lambda D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})-(1-\lambda)D(\rho^{1}_{R}(\lambda)\|\sigma_{R})\right] 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\quad-\left[\lambda D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))+(1-\lambda)D(\mathcal{N}(\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda))\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))\right. 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\qquad\left.-\lambda D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})-(1-\lambda)D(\rho^{1}_{R}(\lambda)\|\sigma_{R})\right]. 
 
 (3.21) 
 
 At \lambda=0, the equality \rho^{1}_{RA}(0)=\sigma_{RA} holds, and we conclude that \varphi(0)=0. Moreover, (i) implies that \varphi(\lambda)\geq 0 for all \epsilon\geq\lambda\geq 0. Hence, it must be the case that \varphi^{\prime}(0)\geq 0. The desired result follows from computing the derivative \varphi^{\prime}(0): 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(0) 
 \displaystyle=\eta\left[D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\right]-D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))+D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\quad+\Bigg((1-\lambda)\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\left[\eta D(\mathcal{M}(\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda))\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-\eta D(\rho^{1}_{R}(\lambda)\|\sigma_{R})\right. 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-D(\mathcal{N}(\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda))\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))+D(\rho^{1}_{R}(\lambda)\|\sigma_{R})\right]\Bigg)\Bigg|_{\lambda=0} 
 
 (3.22) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta\left[D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\right]-D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))+D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}), 
 
 (3.23) 
 
 where the second equality is a well known property of the relative entropy by which each individual one of the remaining derivatives vanishes at \lambda=0; see  [ 57 , Section 4] and later discussions in  [ 37 , 38 , 3 ] . For convenience, we provide a brief proof of the needed equality in  Appendix   B .
∎ 
 
 
 
 A direct consequence of Theorem   1 is the following result regarding our previously defined contraction coefficients. 
 
 
 Corollary 1 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{\operatorname{2cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (3.24) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The first two equalities follow immediately from Theorem   1 by setting \mathcal{M}=\mathrm{id} and fixing \eta to be the relevant contraction coefficient.
We only need to show the final equality. Clearly \eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}). We conclude the claim by showing \eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}).
Observe that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:B^{\prime}|R^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle=D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{R^{\prime}CA})\|\rho_{C}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\rho_{R^{\prime}A}))-D(\rho_{CR^{\prime}}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R^{\prime}}) 
 
 (3.25) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta\left[D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{R^{\prime}CA})\|\rho_{C}\otimes\mathcal{M}(\rho_{R^{\prime}A}))-D(\rho_{CR^{\prime}}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R^{\prime}})\right] 
 
 (3.26) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta I(C:B|R^{\prime}), 
 
 (3.27) 
 
 where we identify R^{\prime}C\leftrightarrow R, \rho_{R^{\prime}CA}\leftrightarrow\rho_{RA}, and \rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R^{\prime}A}\leftrightarrow\sigma_{RA}.
This implies the desired inequality.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3 . 
 
 We can combine  Proposition   2 and  Corollary   1 to conclude the following inequality: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}), 
 
 (3.28) 
 
 thus reproducing the finding of [ 16 , Proposition III.2] after taking Proposition   1 into account. 
 
 
 
 
 3.2 Mutual information with quantum reference systems 

 
 The next question that we address is as follows: What is the mutual information counterpart of the complete contraction coefficient?
We first check its relationship with the fully quantum mutual information contraction coefficient. 
 
 
 Lemma 4 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, the following inequality holds: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}), 
 
 (3.29) 
 
 where \eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}) is defined in ( 2.13 ) and \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}) in ( 3.6 ). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Consider that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})=\sup_{\rho_{CA}}\frac{I(C:B)}{I(C:A)} 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\rho_{CA}}\frac{D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{CA})\|\rho_{C}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A}))}{D(\rho_{CA}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{A})} 
 
 (3.30) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N})\sup_{\rho_{CA}}\frac{D(\rho_{CA}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{A})}{D(\rho_{CA}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{A})} 
 
 (3.31) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}), 
 
 (3.32) 
 
 which implies the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 It is not currently known whether the inequality in  Lemma   4 is indeed an equality.
We have not made use of the product nature of the state in the second argument in the above proof. In order to find mutual-information-based contraction coefficients that do equal the complete contraction coefficient, we define the following quantities. 
 
 
 Definition 7 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, we define the following mutual-information-based contraction coefficients: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RCA},\\
\rho_{CR}=\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(C:BR)}{I(C:AR)}, 
 
 (3.33) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
\rho^{u}_{R}=\rho^{v}_{R}\,\forall u,v\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:BR)}{I(U:AR)}, 
 
 (3.34) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{2cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
\rho^{0}_{R}=\rho^{1}_{R},\\
|U|=2\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:BR)}{I(U:AR)}, 
 
 (3.35) 
 
 where the optimization is now over classical-quantum states of the form 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{URA}=\sum_{u}p(u)|u\rangle\!\langle u|_{U}\otimes\rho^{u}_{RA}, 
 
 (3.36) 
 
 defined by an ensemble \left((p(u),\rho^{u}_{RA})\right)_{u} in which \rho^{u}_{R}=\rho^{v}_{R} for all u,v, or in the second case, where there are only two states, those have to be equal. 
 
 
 
 We refer to the above contraction coefficients as MIR (“MI with Reference”) contraction coefficients. The MIR contraction coefficients can be expressed in alternative ways, as captured in the following proposition: 
 
 
 Proposition 3 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, the following equalities hold: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RCA},\\
\rho_{CR}=\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(C:B|R)}{I(C:A|R)} 
 
 (3.37) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RCA},\\
\rho_{CR}=\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(C:B:R)-I(B:R)}{I(C:A:R)-I(A:R)\ }, 
 
 (3.38) 
 
 where the multipartite mutual information of a tripartite state \rho_{ABC} is defined as 
 

 
 
 I(A:B:C)\coloneqq D(\rho_{ABC}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}\otimes\rho_{C}). 
 
 (3.39) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The equality in ( 3.37 ) follows from ( 3.4 ) and the fact that I(C:R)=0 because \rho_{CR}=\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R}. 
 
 
 Using the relative entropy chain rule in ( 3.53 ), we find that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:BR) 
 \displaystyle=D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{CAR})\|\rho_{C}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\rho_{AR})) 
 
 (3.40) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{CAR})\|\rho_{C}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A})\otimes\rho_{R})-D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{AR})\|\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A})\otimes\rho_{R}) 
 
 (3.41) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I(C:B:R)-I(B:R)\ , 
 
 (3.42) 
 
 thus establishing ( 3.38 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 We now establish a result similar to  Theorem   1 . 
 
 
 Theorem 2 . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B^{\prime} and \mathcal{M}\colon A\to B be quantum channels, \sigma_{RA} a quantum state, and \eta\geq 0. Then the following are equivalent: 
 
 (i) 
 
 For all c-q states \rho_{RUA} with marginal \sigma_{RA} , where U is a classical system of size |U|=2 , and \rho_{R}^{0}=\rho_{R}^{1} , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta I(U:BR)\geq I(U:B^{\prime}R). 
 
 (3.43) 
 
 
 (ii) 
 
 For all c-q states \rho_{RUA} with marginal \sigma_{RA} , where U is an arbitrary classical system, and \rho_{R}^{u}=\rho^{v}_{R} for all u,v , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta I(U:BR)\geq I(U:B^{\prime}R). 
 
 (3.44) 
 
 
 (iii) 
 
 For all quantum states \rho_{RA} with \operatorname{supp}(\rho_{RA})\subseteq\operatorname{supp}(\sigma_{RA}) and \rho_{R}=\sigma_{R} , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))\geq D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})). 
 
 (3.45) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is structurally similar to that of  Theorem   1 , so we keep it brief.
The proof for (ii)\Rightarrow(i) is again obvious, given that the set of states in (ii) contain those in (i).
Also (iii)\Rightarrow(ii) follows immediately by the direct-sum property. 
 
 
 It remains to show (i)\Rightarrow(iii): Without loss of generality we assume that \sigma_{RA} is a full-rank state. Then, for every \rho_{RA} such that \rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}, and 0\leq\lambda\leq\epsilon, where \epsilon is small enough such that \sigma_{RA}-\epsilon\rho_{RA}\geq 0, we let U\equiv U_{\lambda} be a binary random variable of distribution P_{U_{\lambda}}(0)=\lambda and P_{U_{\lambda}}(1)=1-\lambda, and states \rho_{RA}^{0}=\rho_{RA} and \rho_{RA}^{1}(\lambda)=(1-\lambda)^{-1}(\sigma_{RA}-\lambda\rho_{RA}). After defining \rho_{URA} as in ( 3.17 ), clearly we have that \mathop{\operatorname{Tr}}\nolimits_{U}(\rho_{RUA})=\sigma_{RA}. The main difference with the proof of  Theorem   1 is that we now have \rho_{R}=\rho_{R}^{0}=\rho_{R}^{1}=\sigma_{R}. Then, define 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\varphi(\lambda) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\eta I(U:BR)-I(U:B^{\prime}R) 
 
 (3.46) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta D(\rho_{UBR}\|\rho_{U}\otimes\rho_{BR})-D(\rho_{UB^{\prime}R}\|\rho_{U}\otimes\rho_{B^{\prime}R}) 
 
 (3.47) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta\left[\lambda D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))+(1-\lambda)D(\mathcal{M}(\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda))\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))\right] 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\quad-\left[\lambda D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))+(1-\lambda)D(\mathcal{N}(\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda))\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))\right]. 
 
 (3.48) 
 
 Since \rho^{1}_{RA}(0)=\sigma_{RA}, we have that \varphi(0)=0. Moreover, (i) implies that \varphi(\lambda)\geq 0 for all \lambda\in[0,\epsilon]. Hence, it must be the case that \varphi^{\prime}(0)\geq 0. The result follows from computing the derivative \varphi^{\prime}(0): 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(0) 
 \displaystyle=\eta D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\quad+\left((1-\lambda)\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\left[\eta D(\mathcal{M}(\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda))\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\mathcal{N}(\rho^{1}_{RA}(\lambda))\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))\right]\right)\right|_{\lambda=0} 
 
 (3.49) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})), 
 
 (3.50) 
 
 where the second equality follows again from  Lemma   14 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Theorem   2 implies the following result. 
 
 
 Corollary 2 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, the following equalities hold: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{\operatorname{2cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{\operatorname{cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (3.51) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Also here, the first two equalities follow immediately from  Theorem   2 .
The third equality follows after noting that the CR system in the mutual information acts as the reference system and is indeed given by the same state \rho_{CR}=\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R} in both arguments.
∎ 
 
 
 
 With this, we have found equivalences for almost all of our contraction coefficients. The last one that does not have a relation to the relative entropy contraction coefficient is the mutual information contraction coefficient \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}). Given the results in  Theorem   1 and  Theorem   2 , it is somewhat remarkable that it does not seem to fit in with the usual relative entropy contraction coefficient. In Section   3.3 , we will see that those coefficients are indeed different. To complete the map, we show that \eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}) and \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}) are equal. 
 
 
 Lemma 5 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, the following equality holds: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (3.52) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By the definition of mutual information, we immediately have \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}). We will now prove the opposite direction. To that end, recall the chain rule for relative entropy that follows from direct calculation: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D(\rho_{AB}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\sigma_{B})=D(\rho_{AB}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B})+D(\rho_{B}\|\sigma_{B})\ . 
 
 (3.53) 
 
 Note that, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A})) 
 \displaystyle=D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A}))+D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{A})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A})) 
 
 (3.54) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})D(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\rho_{A})+\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N})D(\rho_{A}\|\sigma_{A}) 
 
 (3.55) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\max\{\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N})\}\left[D(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\rho_{A})+D(\rho_{A}\|\sigma_{A})\right] 
 
 (3.56) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\max\{\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N})\}D(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A}) 
 
 (3.57) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})D(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A}), 
 
 (3.58) 
 
 where in the last equality we have made use of \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})\geq\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}), which follows directly from ( 3.13 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Having found equivalent expressions for all quantities we defined initially, we summarize our results in the following corollary. 
 
 
 Corollary 3 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{2cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}) 
 
 (3.59) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}) 
 
 (3.60) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\operatorname{2cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{\operatorname{cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}) 
 
 (3.61) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\operatorname{2cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (3.62) 
 
 
 
 
 The first inequality can be strict; see the next section. 
 
 
 
 3.3 Separations between the coefficients 

 
 We know that for qubit depolarizing channels (see, e.g.,  [ 37 , Eq. (3.54)] ), 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p})=(1-p)^{2}, 
 
 (3.63) 
 
 where \mathcal{D}_{p}(\rho)\coloneqq(1-p)\rho+pI/d.
This allows us to find examples for which the other contraction coefficients are strictly larger. Consider first the mutual information contraction coefficient for the depolarizing channel at p=0.5 and lower bound it by choosing \rho_{CA}=0.5\,\Phi^{+}+0.5\,\frac{I}{2}\otimes\frac{I}{2}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{D}_{p})\geq\frac{I(C:B)}{I(C:A)}>0.265>0.25=\eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p}). 
 
 (3.64) 
 
 
 
 Generally, separating the larger contraction coefficients is more challenging, because they include optimizations over unbounded reference systems. We provide one additional example.
Choosing \rho_{RA}=\Phi^{+} and \sigma_{RA}=\frac{3}{4}\Phi^{-}+\frac{1}{4}\Phi^{+}, then one can numerically calculate that for p=0.5, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p})\geq\frac{D((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{p})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{p})(\sigma_{RA}))}{D(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})}>0.2881>0.25=\eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p}). 
 
 (3.65) 
 
 
 
 In a numerical search, we did not find any states achieving a comparable value with the mutual information contraction coefficient, leading us to conjecture that the complete contraction coefficient is indeed larger. Nevertheless, this does not imply that such a state does not exist. 
 
 
 Especially interesting would be if any of the following can be shown 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p})\overset{?}{>}\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p})\overset{?}{>}\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{D}_{p}). 
 
 (3.66) 
 
 All three quantities here suffer from a potentially unbounded reference system, making them challenging to compute.
For now, it remains an open problem to conclusively show whether these three coefficients are different or indeed the same. 
 
 
 
 3.4 Partial orders 

 
 Partial orders quantify the data-processing decrease of information relative to a second channel. Such orders have been investigated in the quantum setting since the introduction of degradability in the context of quantum capacities  [ 23 ] , while they have a much longer history classically  [ 45 ] . Later, quantum generalizations of the less noisy and more capable orders were also introduced  [ 78 ] . Since then, they have noticeably shaped the discussion around capacities  [ 73 , 35 , 47 , 70 ] and have also been studied in their own right  [ 11 , 4 ] . 
 
 
 While degradability inherently takes into account reference systems, entropy-based partial orders such as less noisy do not in general.
One suggestion, made in  [ 37 ] , was to define the completely less noisy order : Denote by \mathcal{N}\geq_{\operatorname{cln}}\mathcal{M} that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(U:BR) 
 \displaystyle\geq I(U:B^{\prime}R) 
 \displaystyle\forall\rho_{RUA} 
 
 (3.67) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})) 
 \displaystyle\geq D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA})) 
 \displaystyle\forall\rho_{RA},\sigma_{RA}, 
 
 (3.68) 
 
 where U is a classical system and the equivalence between the two conditions was shown in  [ 37 , Proposition 2.3] . Although usually the less noisy order is closely related to relative entropy contraction, it was understood that building a contraction coefficient naively from these quantities leads to a trivial result. 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\sup_{\rho_{RUA}}\frac{I(U:BR)}{I(U:AR)}=\sup_{\rho_{RA},\sigma_{RA}}\frac{D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))}{D(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})}=1. 
 
 (3.69) 
 
 
 
 However, Theorem   1 suggests that the contraction coefficient corresponding to the completely less noisy ordering should instead be constructed from conditional relative entropies. To see this, note that it immediately follows from ( 3.68 ) that an equivalent condition for the completely less noisy partial order is 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\geq D(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\;\forall\rho_{RA},\sigma_{RA}. 
 
 (3.70) 
 
 Given item (iii) of Theorem   1 , it seems clear that this represents a better connection of the completely less noisy partial order to a contraction coefficient. This identification also motivates the following result, which is the analogue of the relation between the less noisy partial order and the relative entropy contraction coefficient [ 37 , Proposition 2.5] . 
 
 
 Lemma 6 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, the following equality holds: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})=1-\sup\left\{\epsilon\in[0,1]\mid\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}\geq_{\operatorname{cln}}\mathcal{N}\right\}, 
 
 (3.71) 
 
 where \mathcal{E}_{\epsilon} is the erasure channel with erasure probability \epsilon. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 To begin with, let us note that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{RA})\right)=(1-\epsilon)D(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})+\epsilon D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}), 
 
 (3.72) 
 
 implying that 
 

 
 
 \frac{D\!\left(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{RA})\right)-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{D(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}=1-\epsilon. 
 
 (3.73) 
 
 for every pair of states \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA} such that D(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})>0.
Hence, by definition, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon})=1-\epsilon. 
 
 (3.74) 
 
 
 
 We first prove the \leq direction in ( 3.71 ). For an \epsilon^{\star} that optimizes the right-hand side, we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon^{\star}})=1-\epsilon^{\star}, 
 
 (3.75) 
 
 where the inequality follows from ( 3.68 ) and the assumption that \mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}\geq_{\operatorname{cln}}\mathcal{N}. 
 
 
 We now show the other direction in ( 3.71 ). Assume \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})=1-\epsilon. Then for all \rho_{RA}, \sigma_{RA} we have, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\leq(1-\epsilon)\left[D(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\right], 
 
 (3.76) 
 
 which is equivalent to 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA})) 
 \displaystyle\leq(1-\epsilon)D(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})+\epsilon D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 (3.77) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=D(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{RA})). 
 
 (3.78) 
 
 This in turn implies that \mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}\geq_{\operatorname{cln}}\mathcal{N} and hence \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})\geq 1-\sup\{\epsilon\in[0,1]\mid\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}\geq_{\operatorname{cln}}\mathcal{N}\}.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma   6 might also provide some intuition regarding the conditional contraction coefficient in the sense that it answers the question of how much information is erased by the channel. Consider the erasure channel with erasure probability \epsilon and any divergence \mathbb{D} satisfying the direct-sum property. Then, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{RA}))=(1-\epsilon)\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})+\epsilon\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}). 
 
 (3.79) 
 
 Setting the erasure probability \epsilon=1-\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}), the conditional contraction coefficient quantifies the decrease of information at the output system, while maintaining that in the reference system; i.e., 
 

 
 
 \mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))\leq\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{RA})). 
 
 (3.80) 
 
 This follows because 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})) 
 \displaystyle=\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})+\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 (3.81) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})(\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}))+\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 (3.82) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})+(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}))\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 (3.83) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{RA})). 
 
 (3.84) 
 
 
 
 From  Lemma   6 we also obtain a direct connection to the Doeblin coefficient \alpha_{+}(\cdot) as defined in ( 2.35 ), showing, as a special case of Proposition   2 , that 
 

 
 
 \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}), 
 
 (3.85) 
 
 because degradability always implies completely less noisy (see [ 39 , Eq. (I.4) and Prop. III.3] in this context). 
 
 
 Remark 4 . 
 
 Note that one could also define a variant of the completely less noisy partial order where \rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}. This would then allow for connections similar to the above relating the partial order to the complete contraction coefficient. 
 
 
 
 Remark 5 . 
 
 Another direct consequence of  Theorem   1 is that in the (conditional) mutual information definition of completely less noisy, it is sufficient to consider a classical system U with |U|=2. 
 
 
 
 
 3.5 SDPI constants 

 
 So far, we have mostly discussed contraction coefficients. However, it is worth noting that much of the above also holds for the generalized SDPI constants as defined in  Definition   1 , with regards to the relative entropy. To make that explicit, define the conditional mutual information SDPI constants as follows: 
 
 
 Definition 8 . 
 
 Given a channel \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B and a state \sigma_{RA}, we define 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RCA},\\
\rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(C:B|R)}{I(C:A|R)}, 
 
 (3.86) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
\rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:B|R)}{I(U:A|R)}, 
 
 (3.87) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{2cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
\rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA},\\
|U|=2\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:B|R)}{I(U:A|R)}. 
 
 (3.88) 
 
 
 
 
 We then immediately have the following result connecting most of the established SDPI constants. 
 
 
 Corollary 4 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B, the following equalities hold: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\eta_{\operatorname{2cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.89) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof follows from  Theorem   1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 However, in the fully quantum CMI case one actually needs to be a bit more careful in generalizing the equivalence. To that end we introduce stabilized versions of the complete and conditional SDPI constants. 
 
 
 Definition 9 . 
 
 For a generalized divergence \mathbb{D}, a quantum channel \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B and a quantum state \sigma_{RA}, define the stabilized complete SDPI constant by 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}^{\mathrm{c,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,\,\tau_{S}\end{subarray}}\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA}), 
 
 (3.90) 
 
 where S ranges over all finite-dimensional quantum systems and
RS is regarded as the reference system on the right-hand side. We also define the stabilized conditional SDPI constant, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,\,\tau_{S}\end{subarray}}\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.91) 
 
 
 
 
 Note that for the relative entropy Eq.   3.91 can be simplified by restricting the optimization to the maximally mixed state as a consequence of the chain rule for the relative entropy, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\sup_{S}\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA}), 
 
 (3.92) 
 
 where \pi_{S}\coloneqq I_{S}/|S| is the maximally mixed state of dimension |S|.
This makes it purely an optimization over the size of the system S.
We are now in a position to prove the remaining missing equalities. 
 
 
 Proposition 4 (CMI SDPI as a stabilized conditional SDPI) . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B be a quantum channel, and let \sigma_{RA} be a
quantum state.
Then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.93) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First, suppose that no extension \rho_{RCA} of \sigma_{RA} has
I(C:A|R)_{\rho}>0. Then \eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=0.
Moreover, \eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=0:
otherwise, the fixed-state cqCMI characterization would give some
\omega_{URSA} with \omega_{RSA}=\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA} and
I(U:A|RS)_{\omega}>0, and hence 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(US:A|R)_{\omega}=I(S:A|R)_{\omega}+I(U:A|RS)_{\omega}=I(U:A|RS)_{\omega}>0, 
 
 (3.94) 
 
 contradicting the assumption. We may therefore assume that an extension
with strictly positive conditional mutual information exists. 
 
 
 We continue by proving the \leq direction.
First, consider an
arbitrary state \rho_{RCA} satisfying 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA}\qquad\text{and}\qquad I(C:A|R)_{\rho}>0. 
 
 (3.95) 
 
 The relative-entropy chain rule ( 3.53 ) gives 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle D\!\left(\rho_{RCA}\middle\|\pi_{C}\otimes\sigma_{RA}\right)-D\!\left(\rho_{RC}\middle\|\pi_{C}\otimes\sigma_{R}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\qquad=D\!\left(\rho_{RCA}\middle\|\rho_{C}\otimes\sigma_{RA}\right)-D\!\left(\rho_{RC}\middle\|\rho_{C}\otimes\sigma_{R}\right) 
 
 (3.96) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\qquad=I(C:A|R)_{\rho}. 
 
 (3.97) 
 
 Similarly, after applying \mathcal{N} to A, 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle D\!\left(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RCA})\middle\|\pi_{C}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})\right)-D\!\left(\rho_{RC}\middle\|\pi_{C}\otimes\sigma_{R}\right)=I(C:B|R)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}. 
 
 (3.98) 
 
 Therefore, by the definition of the conditional SDPI constant with
fixed second state \pi_{C}\otimes\sigma_{RA}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:B|R)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\pi_{C}\otimes\sigma_{RA})I(C:A|R)_{\rho}. 
 
 (3.99) 
 
 Consequently, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{I(C:B|R)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}}{I(C:A|R)_{\rho}} 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\pi_{C}\otimes\sigma_{RA})\leq\eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.100) 
 
 Taking the supremum over C and over all admissible states
\rho_{RCA} proves 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})\leq\eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.101) 
 
 
 
 For the converse inequality, fix an arbitrary system S. By applying Corollary   4 with the joint space RS acting as the reference system and expanding the definition of \eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}, we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA})=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega_{URSA},\\
U\ \mathrm{classical},\\
\omega_{RSA}=\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA},\\
I(U:A|RS)_{\omega}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:B|RS)_{\mathcal{N}(\omega)}}{I(U:A|RS)_{\omega}}. 
 
 (3.102) 
 
 The classical system U may, in fact, be restricted to be binary. 
 
 
 Consider an arbitrary feasible state \omega_{URSA} in ( 3.102 ), for which I\!\left(S:A|R\right)_{\omega}=0 by the product restriction.
Using the chain rule for conditional mutual information ( 7.47 ), we obtain 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(US:A|R)_{\omega} 
 \displaystyle=I(S:A|R)_{\omega}+I(U:A|RS)_{\omega} 
 
 (3.103) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I(U:A|RS)_{\omega}. 
 
 (3.104) 
 
 Moreover, we have \omega_{RA}=\sigma_{RA}.
Thus, the joint system US is an admissible quantum auxiliary system
in the optimization defining
\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). It follows that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{I(U:B|RS)_{\mathcal{N}(\omega)}}{I(U:A|RS)_{\omega}} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{I(US:B|R)_{\mathcal{N}(\omega)}}{I(US:A|R)_{\omega}}\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.105) 
 
 Taking the supremum over the states in
( 3.102 ) gives 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.106) 
 
 Since this holds for every finite-dimensional system S, taking the
supremum over S yields 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.107) 
 
 Combining ( 3.101 )
and ( 3.107 ) proves
( 3.93 ).
∎
