 Similarly, we can define the MIR (mutual information with reference) SDPI constants, 
 
 
 Definition 10 . 
 
 Given a channel \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B and a state \sigma_{RA}, we define 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RCA},\\
\rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{CR}=\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(C:BR)}{I(C:AR)}, 
 
 (3.108) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
\rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho^{x}_{R}=\rho^{y}_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:BR)}{I(U:AR)}, 
 
 (3.109) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{2cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
\rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho^{0}_{R}=\rho^{1}_{R},\\
|U|=2\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:BR)}{I(U:AR)}. 
 
 (3.110) 
 
 
 
 
 With this we can show the following. 
 
 
 Corollary 5 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, the following equalities hold: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\eta_{\operatorname{2cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\eta_{\operatorname{cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.111) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof follows just like the one of  Corollary   2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Similar to the CMI case, considering the fully quantum setting requires a modification. 
 
 
 Proposition 5 (MIR SDPI as a stabilized complete SDPI) . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B be a quantum channel and let \sigma_{RA} be a
quantum state.
Then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\eta_{D}^{\mathrm{c,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.112) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We follow the same strategy as in
 Proposition   4 , highlighting only the differences
caused by the equal-reference-marginal constraint. The case where no admissible state with strictly positive denominator exists is treated the same way as in Proposition   4 . 
 
 
 First, let \rho_{RCA} be admissible in the definition of
\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}), so that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA},\qquad\rho_{CR}=\rho_{C}\otimes\sigma_{R}. 
 
 (3.113) 
 
 The two states \rho_{RCA} and \rho_{C}\otimes\sigma_{RA} have the same marginal \rho_{C}\otimes\sigma_{R} on the reference
system CR. Moreover, as \rho_{RA}=\sigma_{RA}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D(\rho_{RCA}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle=I(C:AR)_{\rho}, 
 
 (3.114) 
 
 
 \displaystyle D\!\left(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RCA})\middle\|\rho_{C}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})\right) 
 \displaystyle=I(C:BR)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}. 
 
 (3.115) 
 
 It follows from the definition of the complete SDPI constant that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{I(C:BR)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}}{I(C:AR)_{\rho}} 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\rho_{C}\otimes\sigma_{RA}) 
 
 (3.116) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{D}^{\mathrm{c,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.117) 
 
 Taking the supremum over all admissible \rho_{RCA} gives 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})\leq\eta_{D}^{\mathrm{c,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.118) 
 
 
 
 For the converse, fix a system S and a state
\tau_{S}. By the fixed-state cqMIR characterization of the complete
SDPI constant, applied with reference system RS and fixed state
\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA}, we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA})=\sup_{\omega_{URSA}}\frac{I(U:BRS)_{\mathcal{N}(\omega)}}{I(U:ARS)_{\omega}}, 
 
 (3.119) 
 
 where U is classical and the supremum is over c-q states 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{URSA}=\sum_{u}p(u)|u\rangle\!\langle u|_{U}\otimes\omega^{u}_{RSA} 
 
 (3.120) 
 
 such that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{RSA} 
 \displaystyle=\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA}, 
 
 (3.121) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\omega^{u}_{RS} 
 \displaystyle=\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{R}\qquad\forall u, 
 
 (3.122) 
 
 and with I(U:ARS)_{\omega}>0. The classical system U may be
restricted to be binary.
These marginal conditions imply 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{USR} 
 \displaystyle=\omega_{U}\otimes\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{R}. 
 
 (3.123) 
 
 In particular, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(U:S)_{\omega}=0,\qquad I(S:AR)_{\omega}=0. 
 
 (3.124) 
 
 Using these equalities and the mutual-information chain rule therefore gives 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(U:ARS)_{\omega} 
 \displaystyle=I(U:S)_{\omega}+I(U:AR|S)_{\omega} 
 
 (3.125) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I(U:AR|S)_{\omega} 
 
 (3.126) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I(S:AR)_{\omega}+I(U:AR|S)_{\omega} 
 
 (3.127) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I(US:AR)_{\omega}, 
 
 (3.128) 
 
 and similarly, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(U:BRS)_{\mathcal{N}(\omega)}=I(US:BR)_{\mathcal{N}(\omega)}. 
 
 (3.129) 
 
 
 
 Moreover, \omega_{RA}=\sigma_{RA} and
\omega_{USR}=\omega_{US}\otimes\sigma_{R}, so the joint system US
is an admissible quantum auxiliary system in the definition of
\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). Consequently, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{I(U:BRS)_{\mathcal{N}(\omega)}}{I(U:ARS)_{\omega}} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{I(US:BR)_{\mathcal{N}(\omega)}}{I(US:AR)_{\omega}} 
 
 (3.130) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.131) 
 
 Taking the supremum in
( 3.119 ), followed by the
supremum over S and \tau_{S}, yields 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}^{\mathrm{c,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})\leq\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (3.132) 
 
 Combining
( 3.118 )
and
( 3.132 )
proves the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 These findings illustrate that, while many results transfer directly to the case of SDPI constants, others need further care. Intuitively, the stabilization considered here is necessary because fixing \sigma_{RA} also fixes the size of the reference system, removing much of the flexibility present in the contraction coefficient case. 
 
 
 We are now left with proving that the MI SDPI constant and \eta^{p}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A}), as defined in ( 2.9 ), are equal. 
 
 
 Lemma 7 (MI SDPI and product-reference SDPI) . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B be a quantum channel, and let \sigma_{A} be a
quantum state. Define the mutual-information SDPI constant at
\sigma_{A} as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}C,\,\rho_{CA},\\
\rho_{A}=\sigma_{A},\\
I(C:A)_{\rho}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{I(C:B)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}}{I(C:A)_{\rho}}, 
 
 (3.133) 
 
 where the supremum is over every finite-dimensional quantum system
C. Then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A})=\eta_{D}^{p}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A}). 
 
 (3.134) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from the stabilized CMI characterization in
 Proposition   4 by taking the original reference
system to be trivial. Indeed, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A})=\sup_{S}\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{A}), 
 
 (3.135) 
 
 where \pi_{S}\coloneqq I_{S}/|S| and S is regarded as the reference
system on the right-hand side. 
 
 
 For a fixed system S and an arbitrary state \rho_{SA}, the
relative-entropy chain rule gives 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D(\rho_{SA}\|\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{A})=D(\rho_{SA}\|\rho_{S}\otimes\sigma_{A})+D(\rho_{S}\|\pi_{S}). 
 
 (3.136) 
 
 Consequently, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D(\rho_{SA}\|\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{A})-D(\rho_{S}\|\pi_{S})=D(\rho_{SA}\|\rho_{S}\otimes\sigma_{A}). 
 
 (3.137) 
 
 It follows that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\pi_{S}\otimes\sigma_{A})=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{SA},\\
0<D(\rho_{SA}\|\rho_{S}\otimes\sigma_{A})<\infty\end{subarray}}\frac{D\!\left((\mathrm{id}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{SA})\middle\|\rho_{S}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{A})\right)}{D(\rho_{SA}\|\rho_{S}\otimes\sigma_{A})}. 
 
 (3.138) 
 
 Taking the supremum over all finite-dimensional systems S, the
right-hand side becomes precisely the product-reference SDPI constant
\eta_{D}^{p}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A}). Combining this observation with
( 3.135 ) proves
( 3.134 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 This completes our exploration of SDPI constants. 
 
 
 
 3.6 Expansion coefficients and relative contraction coefficients 

 
 Essentially every contraction coefficient above has an associated expansion coefficient by simply replacing the supremum with an infimum. Also, most of the results above would then transfer to this setting, with all inequalities changing direction, for example in  Corollary   3 . Note however, that this would make \check{\eta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}) the largest such coefficient, and Ref.  [ 4 ] showed that, whenever the output dimension is not larger than the input dimension, either \mathcal{N} is a unitary channel or the contraction coefficient is equal to zero. Hence, for this very large class of channels, expansion coefficients defined using the relative entropy are generally not useful, with or without reference systems. The result from  [ 4 ] was later generalized to all monotone f-divergences in  [ 40 ] . We will discuss such divergences in the next section. 
 
 
 More interesting in this context are the relative contraction coefficients. 
 
 
 Definition 11 . 
 
 For two quantum channels \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B^{\prime} and \mathcal{M}\colon A\to B, define 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{RCA}}\frac{I(C:B^{\prime}|R)}{I(C:B|R)}, 
 
 (3.139) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{RUA}}\frac{I(U:B^{\prime}|R)}{I(U:B|R)}, 
 
 (3.140) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{2cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
|U|=2\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:B^{\prime}|R)}{I(U:B|R)}. 
 
 (3.141) 
 
 
 
 
 Then we have the following. 
 
 
 Corollary 6 . 
 
 For channels \mathcal{N} and \mathcal{M}, we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{2cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})=\eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})=\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})=\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}). 
 
 (3.142) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is identical to that of  Corollary   1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Similarly, consider the following. 
 
 
 Definition 12 . 
 
 For quantum channels \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B^{\prime} and \mathcal{M}\colon A\to B, define 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RCA},\\
\rho_{CR}=\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(C:B^{\prime}R)}{I(C:BR)}, 
 
 (3.143) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
\rho^{x}_{R}=\rho^{y}_{R}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:B^{\prime}R)}{I(U:BR)}, 
 
 (3.144) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{2cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
\rho^{0}_{R}=\rho^{1}_{R},\\
|U|=2\end{subarray}}\frac{I(U:B^{\prime}R)}{I(U:BR)}. 
 
 (3.145) 
 
 
 
 
 Then the following holds. 
 
 
 Corollary 7 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}, we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{2cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})=\eta_{\operatorname{cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})=\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})=\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}). 
 
 (3.146) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof follows just like the one of  Corollary   2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 All these results then naturally transfer to relative expansion coefficients via ( 2.48 ) and ( 2.49 ). 
 
 
 
 3.7 Beyond the relative entropy 

 
 As mentioned before, while we have only discussed the relative entropy in this section so far, many of the results above actually hold for much larger classes of divergences. Reexamining the proofs of  Theorem   1 and  Theorem   2 , we note that they really only require the following two properties, 
 
 1. 
 
 (Direct-sum property) For a classical-quantum state \rho_{UA}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\rho_{UA}\|\rho_{U}\otimes\rho_{A})=\sum_{x}p(x)\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}^{x}\|\rho_{A}), 
 
 (3.147) 
 
 
 2. 
 
 (Vanishing first derivative) For two quantum states \rho and \sigma, such that \mathrm{supp}(\rho)\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\sigma), 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\mathbb{D}(\lambda\rho+(1-\lambda)\sigma\|\sigma)\right|_{\lambda=0^{+}}=0. 
 
 (3.148) 
 
 
 
 Particular classes of divergences that follow these properties include several families of f-divergences, including the Petz f-divergence  [ 58 , 59 ] : 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\bar{D}_{f}(\rho\|\sigma)\coloneqq\mathop{\operatorname{Tr}}\nolimits\!\left[\sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}f(\Delta_{\rho,\sigma})(\sigma^{\frac{1}{2}})\right], 
 
 (3.149) 
 
 where \Delta_{\rho,\sigma}(X)\coloneqq\rho X\sigma^{-1} is the relative modular operator, the Hirche–Tomamichel f-divergence  [ 38 ] : 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D_{f}(\rho\|\sigma)\coloneqq\int_{1}^{\infty}f^{\prime\prime}(\gamma)E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma)+\frac{1}{\gamma^{3}}f^{\prime\prime}(\gamma^{-1})E_{\gamma}(\sigma\|\rho)\operatorname{d}\gamma, 
 
 (3.150) 
 
 and the maximal f-divergence  [ 51 ] : 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\hat{D}_{f}(\rho\|\sigma)\coloneqq\mathop{\operatorname{Tr}}\nolimits\!\left[\sigma f(\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\rho\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}})\right]. 
 
 (3.151) 
 
 In all cases the proof of the direct-sum property given in ( 3.147 ) is rather direct, using that \rho_{UA} is block-diagonal. The second property given in ( 3.148 ) was proven for the first and third divergences above in  [ 37 , Proof of Proposition 6.2] and for the second divergence above in  [ 38 , Proof of Theorem 2.8] ; for more details see also  [ 3 , Theorem 2.2] . It was also noted in  [ 37 , Remark 6.3] that the properties also hold for the optimized f-divergence of  [ 84 ] by extending the result from the Petz f-divergence. Lemma   15 in  Appendix   B clarifies that the second property above implies the property used in the proofs of Theorem   1 and Theorem   2 . 
 
 
 For the remainder of this section, we will only consider divergences that satisfy these two properties.
Noteworthy divergences that fall into this class include essentially all \alpha-Hellinger divergences (see, e.g., [ 38 , Section 3] ),
but the Rényi relative entropies do not. 
 
 
 We can define mutual information-like quantities as follows: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{I}(A:B) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{D}(\rho_{AB}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}), 
 
 (3.152) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{I}(A:B|C) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{I}(A:BC)-\mathbb{I}(A:C) 
 
 (3.153) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\mathbb{D}(\rho_{ABC}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{BC})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{AC}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{C}). 
 
 (3.154) 
 
 With these, we can define contraction coefficients similarly to all of what we discussed above and then many results discussed earlier in this section translate directly. As our main example of this, we provide a generalization of  Theorem   1 . 
 
 
 Theorem 3 . 
 
 Let \mathbb{D} be a divergence that satisfies ( 3.147 ) and ( 3.148 ), let \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B^{\prime} and \mathcal{M}\colon A\to B be quantum channels, \sigma_{RA} a quantum state, and \eta\geq 0. Then the following are equivalent: 
 
 (i) 
 
 For all c-q states \rho_{RUA} with marginal \sigma_{RA} , where U is a classical system of size |U|=2 , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta\mathbb{I}(U:B|R)\geq\mathbb{I}(U:B^{\prime}|R). 
 
 (3.155) 
 
 
 (ii) 
 
 For all c-q states \rho_{RUA} with marginal \sigma_{RA} , where U is an arbitrary classical system, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta\mathbb{I}(U:B|R)\geq\mathbb{I}(U:B^{\prime}|R). 
 
 (3.156) 
 
 
 (iii) 
 
 For all quantum states \rho_{RA} with \operatorname{supp}(\rho_{RA})\subseteq\operatorname{supp}(\sigma_{RA}) , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta\left[\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\right]\geq\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}). 
 
 (3.157) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is the same as that for  Theorem   1 , while taking into account  Lemma   15 in  Appendix   B .
∎ 
 
 
 
 As a result, whenever \mathbb{D} satisfies ( 3.147 ) and ( 3.148 ), we can give alternative expressions for the contraction coefficients akin to those for the relative entropy. 
 
 
 Corollary 8 . 
 
 Let \mathbb{D} be a divergence satisfying
( 3.147 ) and
( 3.148 ). Then, for every quantum channel
\mathcal{N}\colon A\to B, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
U\ \mathrm{classical},\ |U|=2\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{I}(U:B|R)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}}{\mathbb{I}(U:A|R)_{\rho}} 
 
 (3.158) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RUA},\\
U\ \mathrm{classical}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{I}(U:B|R)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}}{\mathbb{I}(U:A|R)_{\rho}} 
 
 (3.159) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\rho_{RCA}}\frac{\mathbb{I}(C:B|R)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}}{\mathbb{I}(C:A|R)_{\rho}}, 
 
 (3.160) 
 
 where all suprema are restricted to states for which the denominator
is strictly positive and finite. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The first two equalities follow from
 Theorem   3 after optimizing over the fixed state
\sigma_{RA}. 
 
 
 Since a classical system is a special case of a quantum system, the
last supremum is at least \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}). To prove the converse,
consider an arbitrary state \rho_{RCA} and regard RC as the
reference system in the definition of \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}). Choose the
two states 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{RCA}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{RA}. 
 
 (3.161) 
 
 Then 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RCA}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RC}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R})=\mathbb{I}(C:A|R)_{\rho}, 
 
 (3.162) 
 
 while, after applying the channel, 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}\!\left(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RCA})\middle\|\rho_{C}\otimes\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\right)-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RC}\|\rho_{C}\otimes\rho_{R})=\mathbb{I}(C:B|R)_{\mathcal{N}(\rho)}. 
 
 (3.163) 
 
 Thus every ratio in
( 3.160 ) is bounded from above by
\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}), which proves the last equality.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Analogous results hold for the expansion coefficient and the relative contraction coefficients. Similarly, generalizations of  Theorem   2 and its consequences for the complete contraction coefficient hold as well. 
 
 
 
 
 4 On tensorization of SDPI constants 

 
 As is standard in the literature  [ 63 , 12 ] , tensorization of an SDPI constant for \mathbb{D} refers to the following property: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})\overset{?}{=}\max\{\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}. 
 
 (4.1) 
 
 To motivate this question, we can consider mixing times, as discussed further in Section   7.1 , and take \sigma to be the fixed point of the channel. Usually, considering multiple systems requires an SDPI constant to be evaluated on a tensor product of quantum channels and states. However, tensorization implies that, if all systems are affected by the same noise, the exponential contraction rate does not deteriorate with the number of tensor factors. 
 
 
 Classically, this holds, e.g., for many f-divergences  [ 63 , Theorem 3.9] , including the relative entropy; see also  [ 1 ] . In the quantum setting without reference systems, it holds for one very particular definition of the \chi^{2}-divergence  [ 12 ] , but not for another definition of the \chi^{2} nor the relative entropy  [ 13 ] .
In general, it is still an open problem to determine exactly when tensorization holds. Even for the relative entropy, it holds for two erasure channels  [ 37 , Lemma 3.11] . In the following, we will give conditions under which tensorization does hold for the relative entropy, and we also provide several concrete examples. 
 
 
 4.1 Without reference systems 

 
 Let us first consider the case without reference systems. One direction is easy to see, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})\geq\max\{\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}, 
 
 (4.2) 
 
 following the same line of reasoning given in the proof of Lemma  2 .
To establish the other direction, we introduce the following mutual-information correlation coefficient of a bipartite channel \mathcal{N}_{A_{1}A_{2}\rightarrow B_{1}B_{2}}: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\zeta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{A_{1}A_{2}\rightarrow B_{1}B_{2}})\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{A_{1}A_{2}}\not\in\,\operatorname{PROD}}\frac{I(B_{1}:B_{2})}{I(A_{1}:A_{2})}, 
 
 (4.3) 
 
 where PROD denotes the set of product states.
In what follows, we will only be interested in the setting of a product channel. For that, we collect a few properties here. 
 
 
 Lemma 8 . 
 
 For a product channel \mathcal{N}_{A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{A_{2}\rightarrow B_{2}}, the following properties hold for the mutual-information correlation coefficient: 
 
 1. 
 
 The coefficient is bounded as follows: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle 0\leq\zeta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{A_{1}\rightarrow B_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{A_{2}\rightarrow B_{2}})\leq 1. 
 
 (4.4) 
 
 Note that the upper bound does not generally hold for non-product channels. 
 
 2. 
 
 Additionally, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\zeta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2}). 
 
 (4.5) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For property 1, the lower bound follows from non-negativity of the mutual information and the upper bound from data processing. For non-product channels, the upper bound is violated for example by a replacer channel that prepares a maximally entangled state. Property 2 follows by noting that, for a product channel \mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(B_{1}:B_{2})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1})I(A_{1}:B_{2})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2})I(A_{1}:A_{2}). 
 
 (4.6) 
 
 Since the bound holds for every possible input state \rho_{A_{1}A_{2}}\not\in\operatorname{PROD}, we conclude ( 4.5 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 For the relative entropy SDPI constant, the following approximate tensorization result holds. 
 
 
 Proposition 6 . 
 
 For channels \mathcal{N}_{1} and \mathcal{N}_{2} and states \sigma_{1} and \sigma_{2}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\max\{\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\} 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}) 
 
 (4.7) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\max\{\zeta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}. 
 
 (4.8) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The first inequality is a special case of ( 4.2 ). For the second, by using the chain rule in ( 3.53 ), consider that 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle D(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}(\rho_{A_{1}A_{2}})\|\mathcal{N}_{1}(\sigma_{A_{1}})\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}(\sigma_{A_{2}})) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=D(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}(\rho_{A_{1}A_{2}})\|\mathcal{N}_{1}(\rho_{A_{1}})\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}(\rho_{A_{2}}))+D(\mathcal{N}_{1}(\rho_{A_{1}})\|\mathcal{N}_{1}(\sigma_{A_{1}}))+D(\mathcal{N}_{2}(\rho_{A_{2}})\|\mathcal{N}_{2}(\sigma_{A_{2}})) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\zeta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2})D(\rho_{A_{1}A_{2}}\|\rho_{A_{1}}\otimes\rho_{A_{2}})+\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1})D(\rho_{A_{1}}\|\sigma_{A_{1}})+\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})D(\rho_{A_{2}}\|\sigma_{A_{2}}) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\max\{\zeta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\} 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\qquad\times\left[D(\rho_{A_{1}A_{2}}\|\rho_{A_{1}}\otimes\rho_{A_{2}})+D(\rho_{A_{1}}\|\sigma_{A_{1}})+D(\rho_{A_{2}}\|\sigma_{A_{2}})\right] 
 
 (4.9) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\max\{\zeta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}D(\rho_{A_{1}A_{2}}\|\sigma_{A_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{A_{2}}), 
 
 (4.10) 
 
 from which the claim follows.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Note that related approximate tensorization questions with respect to conditional expectations were also studied in  [ 2 , 28 ] .
Now, Proposition   6 leads to the following corollary. 
 
 
 Corollary 9 . 
 
 For channels \mathcal{N}_{1} and \mathcal{N}_{2} and states \sigma_{1} and \sigma_{2}, if 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2})\leq\max\{\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}, 
 
 (4.11) 
 
 then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}) 
 \displaystyle=\max\{\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}. 
 
 (4.12) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows by using property 2 of  Lemma   8 in  Proposition   6 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 This gives the following special cases. 
 
 
 Corollary 10 . 
 
 The relative entropy SDPI constant tensorizes, i.e. 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}) 
 \displaystyle=\max\{\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}, 
 
 (4.13) 
 
 in the following special cases: 
 
 1. 
 
 If \mathcal{N}_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon} is an erasure channel and \mathcal{N}_{2} is an arbitrary channel, or vice versa. 
 
 2. 
 
 If \mathcal{N}_{1}=\mathcal{N}_{2}=\mathcal{D}_{p,\tau} are generalized depolarizing channels, defined as 
 

 
 
 \mathcal{D}_{p,\tau}(\rho)\coloneqq(1-p)\rho+p\tau, 
 
 (4.14) 
 
 and \sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=\tau are their full-rank fixed points. 
 
 3. 
 
 If \mathcal{N}_{1} is a quantum-to-classical channel and if \sigma_{2} is such that \eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})=\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2}) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 (1) For the erasure channel, all relevant contraction coefficients are equal, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2})=(1-\epsilon)\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2})\leq 1-\epsilon=\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}), 
 
 (4.15) 
 
 from which the claim follows from  Corollary   9 . 
 (2) By  Proposition   2 and  Corollary   3 , we have, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{D}_{p,\tau})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p,\tau})\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{D}_{p,\tau})=1-p. 
 
 (4.16) 
 
 With that, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{D}_{p,\tau})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{D}_{p,\tau})\leq(1-p)^{2}\leq\eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p,\tau},\tau), 
 
 (4.17) 
 
 where the second inequality is from  [ 52 , Theorem 3.1] and is tight for dimension equal to two. The claim then follows from  Corollary   9 .
 (3) Let \sigma_{2} be such that \eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})=\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2}). Because we assume B_{1} to be classical, we have, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(B_{1}:B_{2})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2})I(B_{1}:A_{2}), 
 
 (4.18) 
 
 and hence 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\zeta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1})\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2})=\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})=\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2}), 
 
 (4.19) 
 
 where the second-to-last inequality follows from our initial assumption. This concludes the proof by  Proposition   6 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Ref.  [ 29 ] recently provided derivations of tight large-index estimates for the relative-entropy contraction of generalized depolarizing dynamics, together with tensor-stable estimates under arbitrary ancillary extensions. For replacer dynamics, the latter correspond to the product-reference SDPI constant \eta_{D}^{p} considered here. Combined with the tensorization result in item 2 of  Corollary   10 , their single-system estimates in the replacer setting also yield system-size-independent bounds for arbitrary tensor powers.
In the next part, we study tensorization in settings with reference systems. 
 
 
 
 4.2 SDPIs with reference systems 

 
 In what follows, we discuss variants of the above results that directly address complete and conditional contraction coefficients. First, we note the particular role that \eta_{D}^{p}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A}), as defined in ( 2.9 ), has in this context.
It turns out that this quantity indeed tensorizes. Closely related complete SDPI tensorization results were established in  [ 27 , Proposition 2.9] ; see also  [ 28 , Proposition 4.2] . For completeness, we state and prove here the product-reference form needed in our framework. 
 
 
 Theorem 4 . 
 
 For channels \mathcal{N}_{1} and \mathcal{N}_{2} and states \sigma_{1} and \sigma_{2}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})=\max\{\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}. 
 
 (4.20) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The \geq direction is clear by choosing appropriate product states. For the \leq direction, observe 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle D(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}(\rho_{RA_{1}A_{2}})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{1}(\sigma_{A_{1}})\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}(\sigma_{A_{2}})) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=D(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}(\rho_{RA_{1}A_{2}})\|\mathcal{N}_{1}(\rho_{RA_{1}})\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}(\sigma_{A_{2}}))+D(\mathcal{N}_{1}(\rho_{RA_{1}})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{1}(\sigma_{A_{1}})) 
 
 (4.21) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})D(\mathcal{N}_{1}(\rho_{RA_{1}A_{2}})\|\mathcal{N}_{1}(\rho_{RA_{1}})\otimes\sigma_{A_{2}})+\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1})D(\rho_{RA_{1}}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A_{1}}) 
 
 (4.22) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})D(\rho_{RA_{1}A_{2}}\|\rho_{RA_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{A_{2}})+\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1})D(\rho_{RA_{1}}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A_{1}}) 
 
 (4.23) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\max\{\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}\left[D(\rho_{RA_{1}A_{2}}\|\rho_{RA_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{A_{2}})+D(\rho_{RA_{1}}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A_{1}})\right] 
 
 (4.24) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\max\{\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}D(\rho_{RA_{1}A_{2}}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\sigma_{A_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{A_{2}}), 
 
 (4.25) 
 
 where the equalities follow from the relative entropy chain rule in ( 3.53 ), the first inequality follows by applying the SDPI constants, the second follows from data processing, and the third is a direct upper bound.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Note that by  Lemma   7 , this immediately implies that the MI SDPI constant tensorizes, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}) 
 \displaystyle=\max\{\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}, 
 
 (4.26) 
 
 while the counterexample of [ 13 ] implies that the same does not hold for \eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}. 
 
 
 We now turn our attention to the complete and conditional SDPI constants.
Again, it is clear that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\max\{\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}, 
 
 (4.27) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}) 
 \displaystyle\geq\max\{\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}, 
 
 (4.28) 
 
 by following the same line of reasoning given in the proof of Lemma  2 .
We will examine the converse direction closer for the case of the relative entropy. For that we need, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\zeta^{c}_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{A_{1}A_{2}\rightarrow B_{1}B_{2}}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{A_{1}A_{2}R_{1}R_{2}},\\
\rho_{R_{1}R_{2}}=\rho_{R_{1}}\otimes\rho_{R_{2}}\end{subarray}}\frac{I(R_{1}B_{1}:B_{2}R_{2})}{I(R_{1}A_{1}:A_{2}R_{2})}, 
 
 (4.29) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\zeta^{\Delta}_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{A_{1}A_{2}\rightarrow B_{1}B_{2}}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{A_{1}A_{2}R_{1}R_{2}}}\frac{I(R_{1}B_{1}:B_{2}R_{2})-I(R_{1}:R_{2})}{I(R_{1}A_{1}:A_{2}R_{2})-I(R_{1}:R_{2})}. 
 
 (4.30) 
 
 Similar to the case without reference systems, we can conclude the following approximate tensorization result. 
 
 
 Proposition 7 . 
 
 For channels \mathcal{N}_{1} and \mathcal{N}_{2} and states \sigma_{1} and \sigma_{2}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\max\{\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\} 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}) 
 
 (4.31) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\max\{\zeta^{c}_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}),\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}, 
 
 (4.32) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\max\{\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\} 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2}) 
 
 (4.33) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\max\{\zeta^{\Delta}_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2}),\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma_{1}),\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma_{2})\}. 
 
 (4.34) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is essentially identical to that for  Proposition   6 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Note however that, by the counterexample of [ 13 ] , exact tensorization cannot hold in general also for these quantities because the choice of \sigma_{RA} fixes the reference system and could potentially be chosen to be trivial. 
 
 
 Corollary 11 (Failure of tensorization for complete
and conditional SDPIs) . 
 
 There exist quantum channels
\mathcal{N},\overline{\mathcal{N}}\colon A\to B and a full-rank state
\widehat{\sigma}_{A} such that 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{D}\!\left(\mathcal{N}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{N}},\widehat{\sigma}_{A}\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{A}\right)>\max\left\{\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\widehat{\sigma}_{A}),\eta^{c}_{D}(\overline{\mathcal{N}},\widehat{\sigma}_{A})\right\}, 
 
 (4.35) 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{D}\!\left(\mathcal{N}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{N}},\widehat{\sigma}_{A}\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{A}\right)>\max\left\{\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\widehat{\sigma}_{A}),\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\overline{\mathcal{N}},\widehat{\sigma}_{A})\right\}. 
 
 (4.36) 
 
 Here, the fixed states are understood
as states with one-dimensional reference systems. Consequently,
neither the complete nor the conditional
relative-entropy SDPI constant tensorizes in general. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Ref.  [ 13 , Proposition 4] showed that there
exist quantum channels \mathcal{N},\overline{\mathcal{N}} and a full-rank state
\widehat{\sigma}_{A} such that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}\!\left(\mathcal{N}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{N}},\widehat{\sigma}_{A}\otimes\widehat{\sigma}_{A}\right)>\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N},\widehat{\sigma}_{A})=\eta_{D}(\overline{\mathcal{N}},\widehat{\sigma}_{A}). 
 
 (4.37) 
 
 
 
 Now take the reference system R to be one-dimensional. In this case,
the equal-reference-marginal condition in the definition of the
complete SDPI constant is vacuous, while 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})=0. 
 
 (4.38) 
 
 Therefore, for every channel \mathcal{M} and every state \sigma_{A}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{M},\sigma_{A})=\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{M},\sigma_{A})=\eta_{D}(\mathcal{M},\sigma_{A}), 
 
 (4.39) 
 
 where \sigma_{A} is identified with the state on the joint system
RA for a one-dimensional R. 
 
 
 Applying ( 4.39 ) to
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}, \mathcal{M}=\overline{\mathcal{N}}, and
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{N}} in
( 4.37 ) proves both
( 4.35 ) and
( 4.36 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Interestingly, the approximate tensorization results in Proposition   7 are in contrast to the exact tensorization of \eta_{D}^{p}. We have seen earlier, in  Lemma   7 , that adding an additional reference system akin to \eta^{\Delta}_{D} it can require additional stabilization to recover favorable properties. In the following, we show that \eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}} indeed tensorizes. 
 
 
 Theorem 5 (Tensorization of stabilized conditional SDPI constants) . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N}_{i}\colon A_{i}\to B_{i} be quantum channels and let
\sigma^{(i)}_{R_{i}A_{i}} be quantum states for i\in\{1,2\}. Then 
 

 
 
 \eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}\!\left(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma^{(1)}_{R_{1}A_{1}}\otimes\sigma^{(2)}_{R_{2}A_{2}}\right)=\max\left\{\eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma^{(1)}_{R_{1}A_{1}}),\eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma^{(2)}_{R_{2}A_{2}})\right\}. 
 
 (4.40) 
 
 Equivalently, by
 Proposition   4 , the CMI SDPI constant tensorizes: 
 

 
 
 \eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}\!\left(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma^{(1)}_{R_{1}A_{1}}\otimes\sigma^{(2)}_{R_{2}A_{2}}\right)=\max\left\{\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\sigma^{(1)}_{R_{1}A_{1}}),\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma^{(2)}_{R_{2}A_{2}})\right\}. 
 
 (4.41) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By  Proposition   4 , it suffices to prove
( 4.41 ). Set 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{i}\coloneqq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{i},\sigma^{(i)}_{R_{i}A_{i}}),\qquad\eta\coloneqq\max\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}. 
 
 (4.42) 
 
 
 
 Let \rho_{CR_{1}R_{2}A_{1}A_{2}} be an arbitrary state satisfying 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{R_{1}R_{2}A_{1}A_{2}}=\sigma^{(1)}_{R_{1}A_{1}}\otimes\sigma^{(2)}_{R_{2}A_{2}}. 
 
 (4.43) 
 
 Denote by 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{CR_{1}R_{2}B_{1}A_{2}} 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{N}_{1}(\rho_{CR_{1}R_{2}A_{1}A_{2}}), 
 
 (4.44) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\nu_{CR_{1}R_{2}B_{1}B_{2}} 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2})(\rho_{CR_{1}R_{2}A_{1}A_{2}}), 
 
 (4.45) 
 
 i.e.,
the states after the first channel and after both channels,
respectively.
By the chain rule for conditional mutual information, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:B_{1}B_{2}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\nu} 
 \displaystyle=I(C:B_{1}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\omega}+I(C:B_{2}|R_{1}R_{2}B_{1})_{\nu}. 
 
 (4.46) 
 
 The product condition
( 4.43 ) implies 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(R_{2}:A_{1}|R_{1})_{\rho}=0,\qquad I(R_{2}:B_{1}|R_{1})_{\omega}=0. 
 
 (4.47) 
 
 Consequently, using the conditional mutual information chain rule ( 7.47 ) twice, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:B_{1}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\omega} 
 \displaystyle=I(CR_{2}:B_{1}|R_{1})_{\omega} 
 
 (4.48) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{1}I(CR_{2}:A_{1}|R_{1})_{\rho} 
 
 (4.49) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta_{1}I(C:A_{1}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\rho}. 
 
 (4.50) 
 
 For the second term, regard CR_{1}B_{1} as the auxiliary system and
R_{2} as the reference system for \mathcal{N}_{2}. Again,
( 4.43 ) implies 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(R_{1}B_{1}:A_{2}|R_{2})_{\omega}=0,\qquad I(R_{1}B_{1}:B_{2}|R_{2})_{\nu}=0. 
 
 (4.51) 
 
 It follows that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:B_{2}|R_{1}R_{2}B_{1})_{\nu} 
 \displaystyle=I(CR_{1}B_{1}:B_{2}|R_{2})_{\nu} 
 
 (4.52) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{2}I(CR_{1}B_{1}:A_{2}|R_{2})_{\omega} 
 
 (4.53) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta_{2}I(C:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2}B_{1})_{\omega}. 
 
 (4.54) 
 
 The remaining CMI can be related to the original input state. Indeed,
the product marginal in
( 4.43 ) gives 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(A_{1}:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\rho}=0,\qquad I(B_{1}:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\omega}=0. 
 
 (4.55) 
 
 Using the chain rule and data processing under
\mathcal{N}_{1}\colon A_{1}\to B_{1}, we obtain 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2}B_{1})_{\omega} 
 \displaystyle=I(CB_{1}:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\omega} 
 
 (4.56) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq I(CA_{1}:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\rho} 
 
 (4.57) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I(C:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2}A_{1})_{\rho}. 
 
 (4.58) 
 
 In summary, we find 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:B_{1}B_{2}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\nu} 
 \displaystyle=I(C:B_{1}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\omega}+I(C:B_{2}|R_{1}R_{2}B_{1})_{\nu} 
 
 (4.59) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{1}I(C:A_{1}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\rho}+\eta_{2}I(C:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2}A_{1})_{\rho} 
 
 (4.60) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta\left[I(C:A_{1}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\rho}+I(C:A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2}A_{1})_{\rho}\right] 
 
 (4.61) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta I(C:A_{1}A_{2}|R_{1}R_{2})_{\rho}, 
 
 (4.62) 
 
 where the first equality follows from the chain rule in ( 4.46 ), the first inequality follows from combining
( 4.50 ),
( 4.54 ), and
( 4.58 ), and the final equality again follows from the chain rule.
Since \rho_{CR_{1}R_{2}A_{1}A_{2}} is arbitrary, this proves 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}\!\left(\mathcal{N}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{2},\sigma^{(1)}_{R_{1}A_{1}}\otimes\sigma^{(2)}_{R_{2}A_{2}}\right)\leq\max\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\}. 
 
 (4.63) 
 
 
 
 The converse inequality follows by the same arguments given in the proof of Lemma   2 , which involve choosing appropriate product states to satisfy the lower bound.
Together with ( 4.63 ), this proves
( 4.41 ). The stabilized
conditional-SDPI identity
( 4.40 ) then follows from
 Proposition   4 .
∎ 
 
 
 Non-stabilized SDPI Stabilized SDPI 
 
 
 \eta_{D}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A}) 
 =\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A}) 
 Does not tensorize in general 
 
 
 
 \eta_{D}^{p}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A}) 
 =\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{A}) 
 Exact tensorization 
 
 
 
 \eta^{c}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 =\eta_{\operatorname{cqMIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 Does not tensorize in general 
 
 
 
 \eta_{D}^{\mathrm{c,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 =\eta_{\operatorname{MIR}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 Does not tensorize in general 
 
 
 
 \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 =\eta_{\operatorname{cqCMI}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 Does not tensorize in general 
 
 
 
 \eta_{D}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 =\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}) 
 Exact tensorization 
 \leq \leq \leq 
 Figure 2: 
Status of tensorization of the relative-entropy SDPI constants considered
in this work. Stabilization cannot decrease the corresponding
coefficient. Green entries tensorize exactly for fixed product states and
red entries do not tensorize in general. The ordinary SDPI nevertheless
tensorizes under the sufficient conditions established in
 Corollary   9 . Appendix   C features a proof that the stabilized complete SDPI does not tensorize in general. For all quantities on the left side, we have an approximate tensorization result.
 
 
 
 Finally, the stabilized complete SDPI constant also does not tensorize
in general. We provide an explicit counterexample in
  Appendix   C . We summarize the resulting
tensorization picture in  Fig.   2 . 
 
 
 
 
 5 Trace distance and hockey-stick divergences 

 
 In this section, we focus on the quantum hockey-stick divergence and, in particular, the trace distance as a special case. The hockey-stick divergence is defined as  [ 67 ] 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\mathop{\operatorname{Tr}}\nolimits(\rho-\gamma\sigma)_{+} 
 
 (5.1) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{0\leq M\leq I}\mathop{\operatorname{Tr}}\nolimits[M(\rho-\gamma\sigma)], 
 
 (5.2) 
 
 where (A)_{+} denotes the positive part of a Hermitian matrix A.
The trace distance then corresponds to the above definition for \gamma=1. 
 
 
 One feature that makes the hockey-stick divergence particularly interesting here is that, for \gamma\geq 1, its contraction coefficient simplifies to an optimization over orthogonal pure states  [ 66 , 36 ] , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\gamma}(\mathcal{N})=\sup_{\Phi_{1}\perp\Phi_{2}}E_{\gamma}(\mathcal{N}(\Phi_{1})\|\mathcal{N}(\Phi_{2})), 
 
 (5.3) 
 
 where \Phi_{1} and \Phi_{2} are pure states.
It will be instructive for what follows to provide a brief proof of this result. In fact, the following proof is significantly simpler than the previous proof in  [ 36 , Theorem II.2] and is a direct consequence of the following auxiliary lemma. 
 
 
 Lemma 9 . 
 
 For all quantum states \rho and \sigma and \gamma\geq 1, there exist orthogonal states \tau_{+} and \tau_{-}, such that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho-\gamma\sigma\leq\lambda_{+}(\tau_{+}-\gamma\tau_{-}), 
 
 (5.4) 
 
 where \lambda_{+}=E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The case \lambda_{+}=0 is immediate, so suppose \lambda_{+}>0. We can use the Jordan–Hahn decomposition 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho-\gamma\sigma 
 \displaystyle=X_{+}-X_{-} 
 
 (5.5) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\lambda_{+}\tau_{+}-\lambda_{-}\tau_{-}, 
 
 (5.6) 
 
 where \lambda_{+}=\operatorname{Tr}X_{+}=E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma), \lambda_{-}=\operatorname{Tr}X_{-}, \tau_{\pm}=\frac{X_{\pm}}{\lambda_{\pm}}, and X_{+}X_{-}=0.
The crucial step here is the following observation: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle 1-\gamma 
 \displaystyle=\lambda_{+}-\lambda_{-} 
 
 (5.7) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\qquad\frac{\lambda_{-}}{\lambda_{+}} 
 \displaystyle=1-\frac{1-\gamma}{\lambda_{+}}\geq\gamma 
 
 (5.8) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad\lambda_{-} 
 \displaystyle\geq\gamma\lambda_{+}, 
 
 (5.9) 
 
 where the inequality in the second line follows because \gamma\geq 1 and \lambda_{+}\leq 1.
This directly implies that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho-\gamma\sigma 
 \displaystyle=\lambda_{+}\left(\tau_{+}-\frac{\lambda_{-}}{\lambda_{+}}\tau_{-}\right)\leq\lambda_{+}\left(\tau_{+}-\gamma\tau_{-}\right), 
 
 (5.10) 
 
 thus concluding the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma   9 has a number of implications. First, let \rho and \sigma be pure states; then, as a special case, we recover  [ 55 , Lemma 2] .
Next, it implies the following inequality for all \gamma\geq 1: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{\gamma}(\mathcal{N}(\rho)\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma))\leq E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma)\sup_{\tau_{1}\perp\tau_{2}}E_{\gamma}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{1})\|\mathcal{N}(\tau_{2})), 
 
 (5.11) 
 
 which follows because 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho-\gamma\sigma 
 \displaystyle\leq E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma)(\tau_{+}-\gamma\tau_{-}) 
 
 (5.12) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\implies\quad\mathcal{N}(\rho)-\gamma\mathcal{N}(\sigma) 
 \displaystyle\leq E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma)(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{+})-\gamma\mathcal{N}(\tau_{-})) 
 
 (5.13) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\implies\quad\operatorname{Tr}[M(\mathcal{N}(\rho)-\gamma\mathcal{N}(\sigma)] 
 \displaystyle\leq E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma)\operatorname{Tr}[M(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{+})-\gamma\mathcal{N}(\tau_{-}))]\quad\forall\ 0\leq M\leq I 
 
 (5.14) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\implies E_{\gamma}(\mathcal{N}(\rho)\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma)) 
 \displaystyle\leq E_{\gamma}(\rho\|\sigma)\sup_{\tau_{1}\perp\tau_{2}}E_{\gamma}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{1})\|\mathcal{N}(\tau_{2})). 
 
 (5.15) 
 
 Together with convexity, Eq.   5.11 
recovers the known result in ( 5.3 ). 
 
 
 It turns out that, in the setting with reference systems, this approach does not quite work for general \gamma. However, for \gamma=1, the approach remains useful. Before presenting the results, we prove the following meta-theorem. 
 
 
 Theorem 6 . 
 
 For every pair of states \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA} satisfying \rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}, and for every pair of channels \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B and \mathcal{M}\colon A\to B^{\prime}, there exists a pair of orthogonal states \tau_{RA} and \bar{\tau}_{RA} with \tau_{R}=\bar{\tau}_{R}, such that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))}{E_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))}=\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\bar{\tau}_{RA}))}{E_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\bar{\tau}_{RA}))}, 
 
 (5.16) 
 
 and, similarly, for every pair of states \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA}, there exists a pair of orthogonal states \tau_{RA} and \bar{\tau}_{RA}, such that 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))-E_{1}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{E_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-E_{1}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}=\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\bar{\tau}_{RA}))-E_{1}(\tau_{R}\|\bar{\tau}_{R})}{E_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\bar{\tau}_{RA}))-E_{1}(\tau_{R}\|\bar{\tau}_{R})}. 
 
 (5.17) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The definitions of \lambda_{\pm} for ( 5.6 ) imply for \gamma=1 that \lambda_{+}=\lambda_{-}. In this case, Eq.   5.6 simplifies to 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA}=\lambda_{+}(\tau_{+}-\tau_{-})\ , 
 
 (5.18) 
 
 which we will use to prove both identities.
We start by proving ( 5.17 ).
Because of the above, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{R}-\sigma_{R} 
 \displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}_{A}(\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA})=\lambda_{+}\operatorname{Tr}_{A}[\tau_{+}-\tau_{-}]=\lambda_{+}\left(\tau_{+,R}-\tau_{-,R}\right), 
 
 (5.19) 
 
 where \tau_{\pm,R}\coloneqq\operatorname{Tr}_{A}[\tau_{\pm}].
With this, we have, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}))-E_{1}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{E_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\rho_{RA})\|\mathcal{M}(\sigma_{RA}))-E_{1}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{+}E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{+})\|\mathcal{N}(\tau_{-}))-\lambda_{+}E_{1}(\tau_{+,R}\|\tau_{-,R})}{\lambda_{+}E_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\tau_{+})\|\mathcal{M}(\tau_{-}))-\lambda_{+}E_{1}(\tau_{+,R}\|\tau_{-,R})} 
 
 (5.20) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{+})\|\mathcal{N}(\tau_{-}))-E_{1}(\tau_{+,R}\|\tau_{-,R})}{E_{1}(\mathcal{M}(\tau_{+})\|\mathcal{M}(\tau_{-}))-E_{1}(\tau_{+,R}\|\tau_{-,R})}, 
 
 (5.21) 
 
 where, in the first equality, we have used ( 5.18 ) and ( 5.19 ) and the second equality follows by reducing the fraction to remove \lambda_{+}. This proves ( 5.17 ). 
 
 
 Next, we show ( 5.16 ). Note that, if \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA} have the same marginals, then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle 0=\operatorname{Tr}_{A}(\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA})=\lambda_{+}\operatorname{Tr}_{A}(\tau_{+}-\tau_{-}), 
 
 (5.22) 
 
 which implies that \operatorname{Tr}_{A}\tau_{+}=\operatorname{Tr}_{A}\tau_{-} and hence E_{1}(\tau_{+,R}\|\tau_{-,R})=0. Plugging this into ( 5.17 ) completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 With this result, it is now straightforward to prove simplified expressions for the trace distance contraction coefficients. 
 
 
 Proposition 8 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B, the coefficients \eta^{c}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}) and \eta^{\Delta}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}) are optimized by orthogonal states, so that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{RA}\perp\bar{\tau}_{RA},\\
\tau_{R}=\bar{\tau}_{R}\end{subarray}}E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\bar{\tau}_{RA})), 
 
 (5.23) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{RA}\perp\bar{\tau}_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\bar{\tau}_{RA}))-E_{1}(\tau_{R}\|\bar{\tau}_{R})}{1-E_{1}(\tau_{R}\|\bar{\tau}_{R})}. 
 
 (5.24) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Both claims directly follow by one of the results in  Theorem   6 each, by choosing \mathcal{M}=\mathrm{id}, taking the supremum over all states on the right side, and then on the left.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Notably, the optimization here is over mixed orthogonal states, in contrast to pure orthogonal states as in ( 5.3 ). It is not clear if the optimizations can be further simplified to be over pure orthogonal states. 
 
 
 We next prove that similar results hold for expansion coefficients. 
 
 
 Proposition 9 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}^{c}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{RA}\perp\bar{\tau}_{RA},\\
\tau_{R}=\bar{\tau}_{R}\end{subarray}}E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\bar{\tau}_{RA})), 
 
 (5.25) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{RA}\perp\bar{\tau}_{RA}\end{subarray}}\frac{E_{1}(\mathcal{N}(\tau_{RA})\|\mathcal{N}(\bar{\tau}_{RA}))-E_{1}(\tau_{R}\|\bar{\tau}_{R})}{1-E_{1}(\tau_{R}\|\bar{\tau}_{R})}. 
 
 (5.26) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Both claims directly follow by one of the results in  Theorem   6 each, by choosing \mathcal{M}=\mathrm{id}, taking the infimum over all states on the right side and then on the left.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Note that also the complete trace distance expansion coefficient is often non-trivial, because it can be lower bounded by a so-called reverse Doeblin coefficient as shown in  [ 30 , Proposition 33] . This quantity is an SDP and can easily be checked for numerous examples. However, whether the same bound holds for the conditional expansion coefficient is currently unknown. 
 
 
 Remark 6 . 
 
 In the setting without reference systems, the restriction to orthogonal states in the optimization for all \gamma\geq 1 has the useful consequence that one can easily prove that \eta_{\gamma}(\mathcal{N}) is non-increasing in \gamma. The absence of a similar result in the settings with reference systems raises the question whether the monotonicity does still hold here. We leave this question open for now, but note that some preliminary numerical evidence suggests that monotonicity in \gamma might not hold in the presence of reference systems. 
 
 
 
 Finally, we remark that, by  Theorem   6 , it also suffices to optimize over orthogonal states for the relative contraction coefficient and the relative expansion coefficient. However, this observation does not simplify the expression by much because the channel \mathcal{M} is generally arbitrary. 
 
 
 5.1 Separating complete and conditional contraction coefficients 

 
 In this subsection, we explore the general question of whether \eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}) is strictly smaller than \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}) by presenting an affirmative answer when the divergence is the hockey-stick divergence. 
 
 
 Proposition 10 . 
 
 For the hockey-stick divergence with \gamma>1, there exists a classical channel \mathcal{N}\equiv W_{Y|X} such that \eta^{\Delta}_{E_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{N})>\eta^{c}_{E_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let \mathcal{N} be a binary symmetric channel with flipping probability p\in[0,1/2) such that (1-p)/p=\gamma. We deduce the above claim by showing that 0=\eta^{c}_{E_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{N})<1-2p\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{E_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 
 We first show that \eta^{c}_{E_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{N})=0 even when the reference
system is quantum for the chosen classical channel \mathcal{N}. Let 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{RX} 
 \displaystyle=q\,\rho_{R}^{0}\otimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|+(1-q)\,\rho_{R}^{1}\otimes|1\rangle\!\langle 1|, 
 
 (5.27) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\sigma_{RX} 
 \displaystyle=r\,\sigma_{R}^{0}\otimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|+(1-r)\,\sigma_{R}^{1}\otimes|1\rangle\!\langle 1| 
 
 (5.28) 
 
 be arbitrary classical-quantum states, where \rho_{R}^{0}, \rho_{R}^{1}, \sigma_{R}^{0}, and \sigma_{R}^{1}
are normalized quantum states, satisfying 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}\eqqcolon\omega_{R}. 
 
 (5.29) 
 
 Thus, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle q\rho_{R}^{0}+(1-q)\rho_{R}^{1}=r\sigma_{R}^{0}+(1-r)\sigma_{R}^{1}=\omega_{R}. 
 
 (5.30) 
 
 Denote the two output blocks of (\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RX}) by
\widetilde{\rho}_{R}^{0} and \widetilde{\rho}_{R}^{1}. For the binary symmetric
channel with crossover probability p, they satisfy 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{R}^{0} 
 \displaystyle=(1-p)q\rho_{R}^{0}+p(1-q)\rho_{R}^{1}=p\omega_{R}+(1-2p)q\rho_{R}^{0}\leq(1-p)\omega_{R}, 
 
 (5.31) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{R}^{1} 
 \displaystyle=pq\rho_{R}^{0}+(1-p)(1-q)\rho_{R}^{1}=p\omega_{R}+(1-2p)(1-q)\rho_{R}^{1}\leq(1-p)\omega_{R}, 
 
 (5.32) 
 
 where we used q\rho_{R}^{0}\leq\omega_{R} and (1-q)\rho_{R}^{1}\leq\omega_{R}.
Analogously, for the output blocks
\widetilde{\sigma}_{R}^{0},\widetilde{\sigma}_{R}^{1} of
(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RX}), we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{R}^{0}\geq p\omega_{R},\qquad\widetilde{\sigma}_{R}^{1}\geq p\omega_{R}. 
 
 (5.33) 
 
 Since \gamma=(1-p)/p, it follows for x\in\{0,1\} that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{R}^{x}\leq(1-p)\omega_{R}=\gamma p\omega_{R}\leq\gamma\widetilde{\sigma}_{R}^{x}. 
 
 (5.34) 
 
 Consequently, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RX})\leq\gamma(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RX}), 
 
 (5.35) 
 
 and hence 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{\gamma}\!\left((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RX})\middle\|(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RX})\right)=0. 
 
 (5.36) 
 
 As this holds for every quantum reference system R and every pair
with \rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}, we conclude that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{E_{\gamma}}(\mathcal{N})=0. 
 
 (5.37) 
 
 
 
 To obtain a lower bound on the conditional contraction coefficient, we consider the following setting by choosing the reference system R to be classical: for the following input joint distributions for r,x\in\{0,1\}, with r a random variable for the reference system and x for the channel input, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle P_{RX}(r,x) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbf{1}\{(r,x)=(1,1)\}, 
 
 (5.38) 
 
 
 \displaystyle Q_{RX}(r,x) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\left(1-\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\mathbf{1}\{(r,x)=(0,1)\}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\mathbf{1}\{(r,x)=(1,0)\}, 
 
 (5.39) 
 
 where \mathbf{1} is the indicator function.
With that, along with the notation P_{RY}\equiv W_{Y|X}(P_{RX}) and Q_{RY}\equiv W_{Y|X}(Q_{RX}), we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{\gamma}(P_{R}\|Q_{R}) 
 \displaystyle=(0-\gamma(1-1/\gamma))_{+}+(1-\gamma/\gamma)_{+}=0, 
 
 (5.40) 
 
 
 \displaystyle E_{\gamma}(P_{RX}\|Q_{RX}) 
 \displaystyle=1, 
 
 (5.41) 
 
 
 \displaystyle E_{\gamma}(W_{Y|X}(P_{RX})\|W_{Y|X}(Q_{RX})) 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}(P_{RY}(1,y)-\gamma Q_{RY}(1,y))_{+}+0 
 
 (5.42) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}}\left(W_{Y|X}(y|1)-\gamma\frac{1}{\gamma}W_{Y|X}(y|0)\right)_{+} 
 
 (5.43) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=(1-2p). 
 
 (5.44) 
 
 Since p>0 for \gamma>1, it follows that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{RX},Q_{RX}\end{subarray}}\frac{E_{\gamma}(W_{Y|X}(P_{RX})\|W_{Y|X}(Q_{RX})-E_{\gamma}(P_{R}\|Q_{R})}{E_{\gamma}(P_{RX}\|Q_{RX})-E_{\gamma}(P_{R}\|Q_{R})} 
 \displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-2p)-0}{1-0} 
 
 (5.45) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=1-2p 
 
 (5.46) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle>0 
 
 (5.47) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta^{c}_{E_{\gamma}}(W_{Y|X}), 
 
 (5.48) 
 
 thus proving the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 A crucial ingredient in this example is that the hockey-stick divergence is not faithful. It remains an interesting open question to determine whether similar examples exist for faithful divergences. 
 
 
 
 
 6 Applications to quantum networks 

 
 6.1 Contraction of mutual information in networks 

 
 One of the most natural motivations for considering contraction with reference systems is to improve our understanding of data processing in network settings. This can ultimately lead to a better understanding of what information is preserved in a network and how information should flow through the network.
Ref.  [ 60 ] considered the contraction of mutual information in classical networks.
Our goal in what follows is to find a suitable generalization of their result that applies to quantum networks. 
 
 
 Consider the setting of a quantum channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to BE}=\mathcal{R}_{D\to E}\circ\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD} that is applied to an input state \rho_{CA}, depicted diagrammatically as follows: 
 
 A C B D E \rho_{CA} \;\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD} \mathcal{R}_{D\to E} 
 
 
 In what follows, we use the notation \mathcal{M}_{A\to B}\equiv\mathop{\operatorname{Tr}}\nolimits_{D}\circ\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD}. 
 
 
 We are interested in bounding the contraction of the channel \mathcal{N}, based on the contraction of \mathcal{M} and \mathcal{R}. The system C here plays the role of a reference system for the input, and we are interested in the mutual information between system C and different outputs of the quantum network. We first establish the main result of this section. 
 
 
 Proposition 11 . 
 
 With the above definitions, the following inequalities hold: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{A\to BE}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD})+(1-\eta_{\mathcal{R}})\cdot\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to B}), 
 
 (6.1) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{A\to BE}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\mathcal{R}}\cdot\eta_{D}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD})+(1-\eta_{\mathcal{R}})\cdot\eta_{D}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to B}), 
 
 (6.2) 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathcal{R}}\equiv\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{R}_{D\to E}). 
 
 (6.3) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We start by proving the first inequality in ( 6.1 ). First note the following: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(C:BE) 
 \displaystyle=I(C:BE)-I(C:B)+I(C:B) 
 
 (6.4) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{R}_{D\to E})\left[I(C:BD)-I(C:B)\right]+I(C:B) 
 
 (6.5) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{R}_{D\to E})I(C:BD)+(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{R}_{D\to E}))I(C:B), 
 
 (6.6) 
 
 where the inequality follows from ( 3.4 ) and Corollary   1 .
This implies, using the definition of \eta_{\mathcal{R}}, that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{I(C:BE)}{I(C:A)} 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\mathcal{R}}\frac{I(C:BD)}{I(C:A)}+(1-\eta_{\mathcal{R}})\frac{I(C:B)}{I(C:A)} 
 
 (6.7) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\mathcal{R}}\,\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD})+(1-\eta_{\mathcal{R}})\,\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to B}). 
 
 (6.8) 
 
 Now taking the supremum over all \rho_{CA}\not\in\operatorname{PROD} on the left proves the claim. 
 
 
 The second inequality in ( 6.2 ) for the relative entropy contraction then follows in the same way, assuming that C is a classical system and recalling that \eta_{D}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Note that, in the classical setting, Eqs. ( 6.1 ) and ( 6.2 ) are equivalent; however, in the quantum case, they are different, as follows from ( 3.64 ).
While  Eq.   6.2 appears as the more direct generalization of the classical result, Eq.   6.1 is a fully quantum version that explicitly considers a quantum system C. Recall that we can state both results either as mutual information contraction or as relative entropy contraction, because we have seen previously that \eta_{D}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{{\operatorname{cqMI}}}(\mathcal{N}) and \eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N})=\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}) (recall Corollary   3 ). Interestingly, the proof is the same for classical and quantum C, because, from  Corollary   1 , we know that the conditional mutual information contraction coefficient is the same in both cases. 
 
 
 If we are interested in a computable bound for \eta_{\mathcal{R}} defined in ( 6.3 ), we can use the following variant. 
 
 
 Corollary 12 . 
 
 With the above definitions, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}_{A\to BE}) 
 \displaystyle\leq(1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{R}))\cdot\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD})+\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{R})\cdot\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to B}), 
 
 (6.9) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{A\to BE}) 
 \displaystyle\leq(1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{R}))\cdot\eta_{D}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to BD})+\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{R})\cdot\eta_{D}(\mathcal{M}_{A\to B}). 
 
 (6.10) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows as before, by using the bound \eta_{\mathcal{R}}\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{R}) after ( 6.5 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 At this point, it is worth remarking that the results in  [ 60 ] also cover scenarios that include classical feedback. In the fully quantum setting, the limitations in the topology of our networks compared to those in  [ 60 ] are akin to the no-cloning theorem, forbidding us to copy and reuse quantum information. However, our result is actually more general, in the sense that if we restrict some communication paths to be at least partially classical, we can recover the topology discussed in  [ 60 ] . Here we consider a network of the following form: 
 
 X C V D E \rho_{CX} \;\mathcal{C}_{X\to V} \;\mathcal{M}_{XV\to D} \mathcal{R}_{VD\to E} 
 
 
 In the above diagram, X and V are now classical systems, and maps denoted by double arrows are classical input channels, with \mathcal{C}_{X\to V} being a fully classical channel. Now, we see that this is indeed a special case of  Proposition   11 , by noting that we can make three copies of the output of the fully classical channel \mathcal{C}_{X\to V}, which results in the following diagram: 
 
 X C V D E \rho_{CX} \;\mathcal{C}_{X\to V} \;\mathcal{M}_{XV\to D} \mathcal{R}_{VD\to E} copies of V 
 
 
 and is the desired special case. 
 
 
 The result in  Proposition   11 also allows us to observe another interesting property of the mutual-information contraction coefficients. 
 
 
 Corollary 13 . 
 
 Let n\in\mathbb{N}, and let \mathcal{N} be a quantum channel. Then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes n})\leq 1-(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}))^{n}\leq n\cdot\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (6.11) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let us denote the input and output systems of \mathcal{N}^{\otimes n} by \hat{A}^{n}\equiv\hat{A}_{1}\cdots\hat{A}_{n} and \hat{B}^{n}\equiv\hat{B}_{1}\cdots\hat{B}_{n}, respectively. By identifying
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{N}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\hat{A}_{n}} and \mathcal{R}=\mathcal{N}_{\hat{A}_{n}\to\hat{B}_{n}} and applying  Proposition   11 , we have that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes n}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})\cdot\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}\!\left(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A_{n}}\right)+(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}))\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes(n-1)}) 
 
 (6.12) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})+(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}))\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes(n-1)}). 
 
 (6.13) 
 
 where the equality follows because \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathrm{id})=1.
Subtracting 1 on both sides, this becomes, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes n})-1 
 \displaystyle\leq(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}))\left(\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes(n-1)})-1\right) 
 
 (6.14) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}))^{n-1}\left(\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})-1\right) 
 
 (6.15) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}))^{n-1}\left(\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})-1\right) 
 
 (6.16) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=-(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}))^{n}, 
 
 (6.17) 
 
 where the second inequality iteratively applies the first and the third uses \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}). Adding back 1 on both sides proves the first claim. For the second claim we used that 1-x^{n}\leq n(1-x) for all n\in\mathbb{N} and x\in[0,1].
∎ 
 
 
 
 This can be compared to the Doeblin upper bound derived in  [ 30 , Corollary 9] , which is the middle inequality in the following, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes n})\leq\eta_{\mathop{\operatorname{Tr}}\nolimits}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes n})\leq 1-\alpha_{\text{\begin{CJK}{bsmi}王
\end{CJK}}}\!\!(\mathcal{N})^{n}\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N})^{n}. 
 
 (6.18) 
 
 (See [ 30 , Definition 6] for a definition of \alpha_{\text{\begin{CJK}{bsmi}王
\end{CJK}}}\!\!(\mathcal{N}).)
Although the two might not be generally comparable,  Corollary   13 gives a tighter bound whenever we are interested in the contraction of mutual information. A classical analog of this result was previously reported in  [ 60 , Corollary 6] . 
 
 
 Alternatively, if we are interested in the tensor product of different channels, we can conclude results along the following lines. 
 
 
 Corollary 14 . 
 
 For two quantum channels \mathcal{N} and \mathcal{M} and \star\in\{D,{\operatorname{MI}}\}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\star}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{M})\leq\min\begin{cases}\eta_{\star}(\mathcal{M})+\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})-\eta_{\star}(\mathcal{M})\cdot\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N})\\
\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{M})+\eta_{\star}(\mathcal{N})-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{M})\cdot\eta_{\star}(\mathcal{N})\end{cases}. 
 
 (6.19) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof follows directly from ( 6.13 ), applied once to remove \mathcal{N} and once to remove \mathcal{M}.
∎ 
 
 
 
 This gives some general upper bounds on the contraction coefficient for tensor-product channels, which were previously only known for special cases, such as when one channel is an erasure channel (see  [ 37 , Lemma 3.9] ). Concretely, considering the qubit depolarizing channel leads to the following bounds: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle(1-p)^{2}\leq\frac{2(1-p)^{2}}{1+(1-p)^{2}}\leq\eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes 2})\leq 1-2p^{2}+p^{3}\leq 1-p^{2}, 
 
 (6.20) 
 
 The inequality \eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes 2})\leq 1-2p^{2}+p^{3} follows from  Corollary   14 .
The final inequality 1-2p^{2}+p^{3}\leq 1-p^{2} gives, to our knowledge, the best previously known upper bound.
This follows from
\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p})\leq 1-p, based on the Doeblin upper bound. An improvement over this would also improve the upper bound in the above example.
The loose lower bound above is given by \eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p}) itself, while the tighter lower bound \frac{2(1-p)^{2}}{1+(1-p)^{2}}\leq\eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes 2}) can be derived by fixing input states \rho_{0}=a|00\rangle\!\langle 00|+(1-a)|11\rangle\!\langle 11| and \rho_{1}=(1-a)|00\rangle\!\langle 00|+a|11\rangle\!\langle 11| and taking the limit a\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}.
We illustrate the resulting bounds in  Fig.   3 . 
 
 
 Figure 3: Illustration of the bounds on \eta_{D}(\mathcal{D}_{p}^{\otimes 2}) as given in ( 6.20 ). The shaded area gives the remaining range of possible values for the contraction coefficient. 
 
 
 
 6.2 Limits on quantum memories for preserving resources even with post-processing 

 
 A quantum memory is a device that preserves a quantum system and its correlations with other systems over time. In other words, it preserves entanglement or a useful state such that it may be used for subsequent quantum information processing tasks. For this reason, it is thought that quantum memories will be a significant component of the future quantum internet [ 79 ] . 
 
 
 As near-term quantum memories will be noisy, it is important to determine the limits of quantum memories with noisy components. One method for determining these limits is to model the quantum memory as a discrete, time-homogeneous Markov chain \left(\mathcal{N}^{n}_{A\to A}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}} and study to what depth n the chain still serves as a ‘good’ quantum memory. One way of addressing this question involves studying the zero-error quantum capacity of \left(\mathcal{N}^{n}\right)_{n} over time [ 68 , 26 , 5 ] . However, as identified in previous works, e.g.,  [ 68 , 26 ] , a problem with considering the capacity is that there is only one quantum memory in practice, rather than many copies of the memory, as would be needed for the capacity to be relevant. Thus, one should consider the one-shot setting. A second problem that, to the best of our knowledge has neither been raised nor addressed, is that the users of the quantum memory can in principle post-process the state after it has undergone the noisy memory for the relevant task. For example, if the memory is to preserve entanglement between two parties, the parties could perform entanglement distillation on the state that comes out of their memories in order to obtain some entanglement, so long as the memories did not destroy all the distillable entanglement (see Fig.  4 for a visualization). 
 
 
 Figure 4: Depiction of the setting we consider in Section 6.2 . 
 
 
 In this subsection, we establish limits on the time n such that the quantum memories cannot be used for storing entanglement or quantum states for secret key, even under post-processing. All the proofs follow the same idea: initially Alice and Bob have some shared, possibly resourceful state \rho_{A_{1}B_{1}} loaded into their local quantum memories. These quantum memories are described by discrete, time-inhomogeneous Markov chains 
 

 
 
 \left(\mathcal{G}_{n}\coloneq\bigcirc_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{N}^{i}_{A_{i}\to A_{i+1}}\right)_{n},\qquad\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\coloneq\bigcirc_{i\in[n]}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i}_{A_{i}\to A_{i+1}}\right)_{n}, 
 
 (6.21) 
 
 defined by channels \mathcal{N}^{i}_{A_{i}\to A_{i+1}} and \widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i}_{B_{i}\to B_{i+1}}, respectively. At some time n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}, the users decide that they want the relevant resource, so they run resource distillation on the output state (\mathcal{G}_{n^{\prime}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n^{\prime}})(\rho). By bounding the one-shot rate of the relevant resource distillation task by mutual information or conditional mutual information and applying the appropriate contraction coefficient, we determine a time n\in\mathbb{N} after which no resource can be distilled from the output of the quantum memories, independent of what the initial state \rho_{A_{1}B_{1}} is. The correspondence between task, allowed post-processing operations, and used contraction coefficient is summarized in Table  1 . 
 
 
 An interesting point to note is that entanglement distillation with LOCC post-processing is controlled by \eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}} but entanglement distillation with non-entangling operations is controlled by \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}. This is interesting as \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}} while LOCC is a strict subset of non-entangling operations. Thus, it should be easier to distill entanglement with non-entangling operations, but our bounds contract at least as fast in this setting. Of course, the relevant constants in the theorems differ, so this could be what makes up for the difference in principle. In contrast to entanglement distillation, for key distillation, we see that one-way LOCC is limited by \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}, whereas general LOCC is limited by \eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}. In this case, the governing contraction coefficient and the power of the post-processing resource aligns appropriately. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Task 
 Allowed Post-Processing 
 Contraction Coefficient 
 
 
 
 Entanglement Distillation 
 Non-Entangling 
 \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}} ( Theorem   7 ) 
 
 LOCC 
 \eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}} ( Theorem   8 ) 
 
 Key Distillation 
 One-Way LOCC 
 \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}} ( Theorem   10 ) 
 
 LOCC 
 \eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}} ( Theorem   9 ) 
 
 
 Table 1: Summary of limits on quantum memories for achieving certain tasks with post-processing.
 
 
 
 In the remaining part of this subsection, we prove our bounds. 
 
 
 6.2.1 Limits on entanglement preservation with non-entangling map post-processing 

 
 We begin with entanglement distillation as measured in terms of singlet fraction, which for a d-dimensional state is the quantity 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle F(\rho,|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|)=\langle\Phi_{d}|\rho|\Phi_{d}\rangle\ , 
 
 (6.22) 
 
 where |\Phi_{d}\rangle\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{i}|i\rangle|i\rangle denotes the standard maximally entangled state vector of Schmidt rank d and F(P,Q)\coloneqq\left\|\sqrt{P}\sqrt{Q}\right\|_{1}^{2} is the Uhlmann fidelity of positive semidefinite matrices P and Q [ 74 ] .
For entanglement distillation under an allowed set of channels, \mathcal{S}, we denote the one-shot distillable entanglement by 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{D,\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}(\rho)\coloneq\max_{d\in\mathbb{N},\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{S}}\{\log d:\langle\Phi_{d}|\mathcal{N}(\rho)|\Phi_{d}\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon\}\ . 
 
 (6.23) 
 
 The first set of channels we will consider are non-entangling channels, which we denote by \operatorname{NE}. To establish bounds on the depth of quantum memories at which one is able to distill entanglement under non-entangling channels, we need to bound E^{\varepsilon}_{D,\operatorname{NE}} by a correlation measure for which we have a contraction coefficient. The following establishes such a bound in terms of mutual information. 
 
 
 Lemma 10 . 
 
 For \varepsilon\in[0,1] and a bipartite state \rho, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{D,\operatorname{NE}}^{(1),\varepsilon}(\rho)\leq\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}[I(A:B)_{\rho}+h(\varepsilon)], 
 
 (6.24) 
 
 where h(\varepsilon)\coloneqq-\varepsilon\log\varepsilon-(1-\varepsilon)\log(1-\varepsilon). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Consider that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{D,\operatorname{NE}}^{(1),\varepsilon}(\rho) 
 \displaystyle\leq\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\Lambda\leq I\\
\operatorname{Tr}[\rho\Lambda]\geq 1-\epsilon\end{subarray}}\min_{\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}}-\log\operatorname{Tr}[\Lambda\sigma] 
 
 (6.25) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\min_{\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\Lambda\leq I\\
\operatorname{Tr}[\rho\Lambda]\geq 1-\epsilon\end{subarray}}-\log\operatorname{Tr}[\Lambda\sigma] 
 
 (6.26) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\min_{\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}}D^{\varepsilon}_{h}(\rho\|\sigma) 
 
 (6.27) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq D_{h}^{\varepsilon}(\rho_{AB}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}) 
 
 (6.28) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}[I(A:B)+h(\varepsilon)]\ , 
 
 (6.29) 
 
 where the first inequality follows from [ 8 , Theorem 1] , the second from the max-min inequality, the equality from the definition of hypothesis testing relative entropy (see, e.g., [ 77 ] ), the third inequality from choosing \sigma=\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B}, which is manifestly separable, and the final inequality from [ 77 , Eq. (2)] and the definition of mutual information.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We now establish our limits on quantum memories. 
 
 
 Theorem 7 . 
 
 Let \varepsilon\in[0,1/2), and let \left(\mathcal{G}_{n}\right)_{n} and \left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\right)_{n} be inhomogeneous Markov chains defined by channels \mathcal{N}^{i}_{A_{i}\to A_{i+1}} and \widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i}_{B_{i}\to B_{i+1}}, respectively. For n\geq\hat{n} where 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\hat{n}\coloneq\min\!\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\left[\prod_{i\in[n]}\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]<\frac{1-\varepsilon-h(\varepsilon)}{2\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}}\right\}\ , 
 
 (6.30) 
 
 it is the case E^{\varepsilon}_{D,\operatorname{NE}}\left(\mathcal{G}_{n}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}(\rho_{A_{1}B_{1}})\right)<1 for all quantum states \rho_{A_{1}B_{1}}. That is, for every input quantum state to the quantum memories, after time \hat{n} the users cannot distill a state with singlet fraction greater than 1-\varepsilon using non-entangling post-processing. 
 
 
 In particular, for homogeneous Markov chains \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} and \mathcal{M}_{B\to B}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\hat{n}=\left\lceil\frac{\log\!\left(\frac{2\log(\min\{|A|,|B|\})}{1-\varepsilon-h(\varepsilon)}\right)}{\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M})}\right)}\right\rceil\ .
