 (6.31) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It suffices to show that the one-shot distillable entanglement is bounded from above by one, as this would show that one cannot distill any state with sufficient overlap with the maximally entangled state, i.e., not even a single ebit. By arithmetical manipulation, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}[I(A:B)+h(\varepsilon)]<1\iff I(A:B)<1-(\varepsilon+h(\varepsilon))\ . 
 
 (6.32) 
 
 By using the definition of the mutual information contraction coefficient, for every initial state \rho_{A_{1}B_{1}}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(A_{n}:B_{n})_{(\mathcal{G}_{n}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n})(\rho)} 
 \displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in[n]}\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})I(A_{1}:B_{n})_{(\mathrm{id}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n})(\rho)} 
 
 (6.33) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\left[\prod_{i\in[n]}\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]I(A_{1}:B_{1}) 
 
 (6.34) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\left[\prod_{i\in[n]}\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]2\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}\ . 
 
 (6.35) 
 
 Thus, by Lemma   10 , if ( 6.35 ) is strictly bounded from above by 1-(\varepsilon+h(\varepsilon)), the claim holds. 
 
 
 The special case of homogeneous Markov chains follows from arithmetic and the contraction coefficient on each system being the same at every time step.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 7 . 
 
 The fact that the interval does not include 1/2 is a consequence of there always existing a product state with singlet fraction 1/2. One may verify that Lemma   10 would require I(A:B)<0 in the case \varepsilon=1/2, which is consistent with this fact. 
 
 
 
 
 6.2.2 Limits on entanglement preservation with LOCC post-processing 

 
 While Theorem   7 is appealing in the sense that it is a very strong no-go theorem, as non-entangling channels represent a powerful class of free channels, there are at least two natural reservations against it. First, non-entangling channels are perhaps too powerful a class of channels to be relevant. Second, in ( 6.35 ) we relaxed the mutual information to twice the local dimension, which ought to be too loose of a starting quantity as one can only distill at most half of that— the minimum of the local dimensions. 
 
 
 The following development resolves both of these problems. First, we place limits on using LOCC post-processing, the set of channels implementable by the two users being able to implement any quantum channel to their local systems and being able to exchange classical communication between each other 1 1 
 1 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Of course, if the users can perform any local channel, one might expect they also could have a fault-tolerant quantum memory. Nonetheless, this only makes our no-go theorems more powerful. , which are considered more reasonable than non-entangling channels (see [ 18 ] and references therein). Second, to do this, we use bounds in terms of the squashed entanglement of a bipartite state \rho_{AB} [ 19 ] , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{\operatorname{sq}}(A:B)_{\rho}\coloneq\frac{1}{2}\inf_{\hat{\rho}_{ABE}}\left\{I(A:B|E)_{\hat{\rho}}:\hat{\rho}_{AB}=\rho_{AB}\right\}\ . 
 
 (6.36) 
 
 Due to the factor of 1/2, the squashed entanglement is always upper bounded by the logarithm of the minimum of the local dimensions, rather than twice that. 
 
 
 With the physical and technical motivation and methods addressed, we state and prove our theorem. 
 
 
 Theorem 8 . 
 
 Let \varepsilon\in(0,0.07), and let \left(\mathcal{G}_{n}\right)_{n} and \left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\right)_{n} be inhomogeneous Markov chains defined by channels \mathcal{N}^{i}_{A_{i}\to A_{i+1}} and \widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i}_{B_{i}\to B_{i+1}}, respectively. For n\geq\hat{n} where 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\hat{n}\coloneq\min\!\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\left[\prod_{i\in[n]}\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]<\frac{1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}-g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}}\right\}\ , 
 
 (6.37) 
 
 where g(x)\coloneqq(x+1)\log(x+1)-x\log x is the bosonic entropy function, it is the case E^{\varepsilon}_{D,\operatorname{LOCC}}\left(\mathcal{G}_{n}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}(\rho_{A_{1}B_{1}})\right)<1 for all quantum states \rho_{A_{1}B_{1}}. That is, for every input quantum state to the quantum memories, after time \hat{n} the users cannot distill a state with singlet fraction greater than 1-\varepsilon using LOCC post-processing. 
 
 
 In particular, for homogeneous Markov chains \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} and \mathcal{M}_{B\to B}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\hat{n} 
 \displaystyle=\left\lceil\frac{\log\left(\frac{\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}}{1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}-g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\right)}{\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{M})}\right)}\right\rceil\ . 
 
 (6.38) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For a state \rho_{AB} that is stored in the quantum memories, we have the sequence of inequalities 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle E_{D,{\operatorname{LOCC}}}^{\varepsilon}(\rho) 
 \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(E_{\operatorname{sq}}(A_{n}:B_{n})_{(\mathcal{G}_{n}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n})(\rho)}+g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\right) 
 
 (6.39) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1}{2}I(A_{n}:B_{n}|E)_{(\mathcal{G}_{n}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E})(\rho)}+g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\right) 
 
 (6.40) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left[\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]I(A_{1}:B_{1}|E)_{\rho}+g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\right) 
 
 (6.41) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left[\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]\log\left(\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\right)+g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})\right)\ , 
 
 (6.42) 
 
 where the first inequality follows from [ 44 , Theorem 13.9] ,
the second inequality from choosing an arbitrary extension of the initial \rho_{AB}, the fourth from the definition of the CMI contraction coeffficient, and the final inequality from the standard dimension bound on the conditional mutual information. Since the above inequality holds for an arbitrary initial state, it follows that if this final upper bound is strictly bounded above by 1, then no entanglement can be distilled using LOCC from the state shared between the quantum memories. As \frac{g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\geq 1 for \varepsilon\in(0.075,1], we restrict \varepsilon\in(0,0.07) in the theorem statement. Thus, bounding ( 6.42 ) from above by 1 and rearranging terms completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.2.3 Limits on preserving resources for secret key with LOCC post-processing 

 
 It is known that distilling key from quantum states can be easier than distilling entanglement. Thus, the previous theorems do not imply that the memory is useless for preserving states enough to perform key distillation. While obviously if the users knew at the start they wanted to distill secret key, they would do so immediately rather than storing the quantum resource in a memory, if the users stored the quantum state and later decided they wanted to distill key from it, then bounds on distillable key are relevant. This is our motivation for establishing such limits on key distillation. 
 
 
 We begin with our limits on key distillation when using local operations and public communication (LOPC). This will again make use of the squashed entanglement ( 6.36 ). This is because it is known to serve as an upper bound on the asymptotics of key distillation and an approximate upper bound in the one-shot setting [ 82 ] . 
 
 
 Theorem 9 . 
 
 Let \varepsilon\in(0,0.01), and let \left(\mathcal{G}_{n}\right)_{n} and \left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\right)_{n} be inhomogeneous Markov chains defined by channels \mathcal{N}^{i}_{A_{i}\to A_{i+1}} and \widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i}_{B_{i}\to B_{i+1}}, respectively. One cannot distill a state of fidelity >1-\varepsilon to a secret key using LOPC from any state stored in the memories for any time larger than or equal to 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\min\!\left\{n:\left[\prod_{i\in[n]}\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]<\frac{1-2\sqrt{\varepsilon}-2g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}}\right\}\ . 
 
 (6.43) 
 
 
 
 In particular, for homogeneous Markov chains \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} and \mathcal{M}_{B\to B}, one cannot distill a state of fidelity >1-\varepsilon to a secret key using LOCC from any state stored in the memories past the critical time 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle n_{c} 
 \displaystyle=\left\lceil\frac{\log\left(\frac{\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}}{1-2\sqrt{\varepsilon}-2g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\right)}{\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{M})}\right)}\right\rceil. 
 
 (6.44) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Following the notation of [ 82 ] , in the framework of key distillation, there exists a one-shot key distillation protocol for state \rho_{AB} that distills \lfloor\log_{2}|\widehat{A}|\rfloor bits of key to tolerated error \varepsilon\in(0,1) if there exists a quantum channel \mathcal{L}_{AB\to\widehat{A}A^{\prime}\widehat{B}B^{\prime}} implementable by two-way LOCC such that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle F(\mathcal{L}(\rho),\gamma_{\widehat{A}A^{\prime}\widehat{B}B^{\prime}})\geq 1-\varepsilon\ , 
 
 (6.45) 
 
 where \gamma_{\widehat{A}A^{\prime}\widehat{B}B^{\prime}} is a private state. 2 2 
 2 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 A private state is one such that there exist local operations that allow Alice and Bob to distill a key containing \log_{2}|\widehat{A}| bits. See [ 82 ] and references therein for more details. Thus our goal is to show that, for the tolerated error \varepsilon, it must be the case \log|\widehat{A}|<1 regardless of the state initially stored in the quantum memories. To do this, let \mathcal{L} be an LOCC channel that achieves the tolerated error \varepsilon for dimension of key, |\widehat{A}|. Then, the following sequence of inequalities holds: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\log|\widehat{A}| 
 \displaystyle\leq E_{\text{sq}}(\widehat{A}A^{\prime}:\widehat{B}B^{\prime})_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}_{n}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}(\rho))}+f_{1}(\sqrt{\varepsilon},|\widehat{A}|) 
 
 (6.46) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq E_{\text{sq}}(A_{n}:B_{n})_{\mathcal{G}_{n}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}(\rho)}+f_{1}(\sqrt{\varepsilon},|\widehat{A}|) 
 
 (6.47) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}I(A_{n}:B_{n}|E)_{\mathcal{G}_{n}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E}(\rho)}+f_{1}(\sqrt{\varepsilon},|\widehat{A}|) 
 
 (6.48) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left[\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]\frac{1}{2}I(A:B|E)+f_{1}(\sqrt{\varepsilon},|\widehat{A}|) 
 
 (6.49) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left[\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}+f_{1}(\sqrt{\varepsilon},|\widehat{A}|)\ , 
 
 (6.50) 
 
 where the first inequality follows from [ 82 , Theorem 2] with 
 

 
 
 f_{1}(\delta,K)\coloneq 2\delta\log(K)+2g(\delta), 
 
 (6.51) 
 
 the second because squashed entanglement monotonically decreases under LOCC
 [ 19 ] , the third from choosing an arbitrary extension of the initial state \rho_{AB}, the fourth from the definition of the CMI contraction coefficient, and the final inequality from the standard dimension bound on conditional mutual information. Next, using the definition of f_{1} in ( 6.51 )
and re-arranging terms, we rewrite the above inequality as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\log|\widehat{A}|\leq\frac{\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left[\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}+2g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{1-2\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\ . 
 
 (6.52) 
 
 It thus suffices to upper bound the right-hand side by 1. As \frac{2g(\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{1-2\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\geq 1 for \varepsilon\geq 0.010989, we restrict to \varepsilon\in(0,0.01) in the theorem statement. The rest of the proof is arithmetic.
∎ 
 
 
 
 While the operational relevance is less clear, we also prove bounds when the users are only willing to use one-way LOCC during post-processing. 
 
 
 Theorem 10 . 
 
 Let \varepsilon\in(0,0.14), and let \left(\mathcal{G}_{n}\right)_{n} and \left(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{n}\right)_{n} be inhomogeneous Markov chains defined by channels \mathcal{N}^{i}_{A_{i}\to A_{i+1}} and \widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i}_{B_{i}\to B_{i+1}}, respectively. One cannot distill a state of fidelity >1-\varepsilon to a secret key using one-way LOCC from any state stored in the memories once the time is 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\min\!\left\{n:\left[\prod_{i\in[n]}\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i})\right]<\frac{\ell(\varepsilon)}{2\log\min\{|A_{1}|,|B_{1}|\}}\right\}\ , 
 
 (6.53) 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\ell(\varepsilon)\coloneq(1-\varepsilon)\log(1/\kappa(\varepsilon))-h(\varepsilon)\text{ and }\kappa(\varepsilon)\coloneq\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{3\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)}}{2}\ . 
 
 (6.54) 
 
 
 
 In particular, for homogeneous Markov chains \mathcal{N}_{A\to A}, \mathcal{M}_{B\to B}, one cannot distill a state of fidelity >1-\varepsilon to a secret key using one-way LOCC from any state stored in the memories past the time 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\hat{n}=\left\lceil\frac{\log\!\left(\frac{2\log[\min\{|A|,|B|\}]}{\ell(\varepsilon)}\right)}{\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{M})}\right)}\right\rceil\ . 
 
 (6.55) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By [ 69 , Theorem 2] , for a state \rho_{AB} and Hilbert space B^{\prime}\cong B, if 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle 2^{-\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{ABB^{\prime}}:\\
\sigma_{AB}=\rho_{AB}\end{subarray}}D_{h}^{\varepsilon}(\rho\|\operatorname{Tr}_{B}[\sigma])}>\kappa(\varepsilon)\ , 
 
 (6.56) 
 
 then there does not exist a one-way LOCC protocol that can distill any key. By basic arithmetic, we can re-express this condition as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{ABB^{\prime}}:\\
\sigma_{AB}=\rho_{AB}\end{subarray}}D_{h}^{\varepsilon}(\rho\|\operatorname{Tr}_{B}[\sigma])<\log(1/\kappa(\varepsilon))\ . 
 
 (6.57) 
 
 Moreover, note 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{ABB^{\prime}}:\\
\sigma_{AB}=\rho_{AB}\end{subarray}}D_{h}^{\varepsilon}(\rho\|\operatorname{Tr}_{B}[\sigma])\leq D^{\varepsilon}_{h}(\rho_{AB}\|\rho_{A}\otimes\rho_{B})\leq\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\left[I(A:B)+h(\varepsilon)\right] 
 
 (6.58) 
 
 where the first inequality follows from considering \sigma_{ABB^{\prime}}\coloneq\rho_{AB}\otimes\rho_{B^{\prime}} and the second from [ 77 , Eq. (2)] . It thus suffices that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\left[I(A:B)+h(\varepsilon)\right]<\log(1/\kappa(\varepsilon))\iff I(A:B)<\ell(\varepsilon)\ 
 
 (6.59) 
 
 for there to be no one-way distillable key to the appropriate tolerated error \varepsilon. We remark that \ell(\varepsilon)>0 for \varepsilon\in(0,0.14), which selects for our parameter range. The rest of the proof is identical to that of Theorem   7 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.3 Limits on quantum memories for quantum secret sharing schemes 

 
 Figure 5: Depiction of model considered in Subsection 6.3 . 
 
 
 In the previous section, we established limits on quantum memories by their ability to preserve the resources we want quantum states to contain even with post-processing. A similar use of a quantum memory is to store a quantum state that acts as part of a cryptographic scheme. An example of such a state consists of the shares in a quantum secret sharing scheme. To see this, recall that a (t,m)-quantum secret sharing (QSS) scheme is a method for mapping a single quantum system into a multipartite state made up of m subsystems such that one can perfectly recover the original system by acting on only t of the subsystems, but cannot recover the initial state “much at all” with strictly less than t subsystems [ 34 , 32 ] . 
 
 
 Formally, consider Hilbert spaces A and \{B_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}, and let B^{m}\coloneq\bigotimes_{i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}B_{i}. Note that |B_{i}| need not be the same for each i. For every S\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}, define B_{S}\coloneq\bigotimes_{i\in S}B_{i}, let S^{c} denote the complement of S with respect to \{1,\ldots,m\}, and let \mathcal{S}_{t}\coloneq\{S\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}:|S|=t\}. A (t,m)-quantum secret sharing scheme is then specified by an encoder \mathcal{E}_{A\to B^{m}} and decoders \left\{\mathcal{D}_{B_{S}\to A}\right\}_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{t}}. One may then require that the secret be recoverable with t shares by demanding the channel fidelity of the encoding and decoding channels with the identity channel be large, i.e., 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle 1-\varepsilon\leq F(\mathcal{D}_{B_{S}\to A}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{S^{c}}}\circ\mathcal{E},\mathrm{id}_{A})\quad\forall S\in\mathcal{S}_{t}\ , 
 
 (6.60) 
 
 where F(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})\coloneq\inf_{\rho_{RA}}F\!\left((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho),(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{M})(\rho)\right). 
 
 
 The concern we consider is then the following. To implement a (t,m)-quantum secret sharing scheme, the encoding \mathcal{E} will be performed, then the m subsystems will be sent to the respective m parties, who then will need to store their local system in their local quantum memory until a later time during which at least t parties agree to gather their systems to decode the secret. One would expect that if the local quantum memories inject too much noise into the global state, the initial secret cannot be recovered. To capture this idea, one can ignore the intended decoders of the scheme and simply determine if decoders even exist . Aligning with  [ 56 ] , we identify this as \varepsilon-recoverability, which by itself does not actually require the channel to be a (t,m) QSS scheme. 
 
 
 Definition 13 . 
 
 Let \mathcal{E}_{A\to B^{m}} and \mathcal{N}_{B^{m}\to\widetilde{B}^{m}} be quantum channels. We say \mathcal{E} is \varepsilon-recoverable for (t,m)-sharing under noise model \mathcal{N} if 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle 1-\varepsilon\leq\min_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{t}}\max_{\mathcal{D}_{\widetilde{B}_{S}\to A}}F(\mathcal{D}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{B}_{S^{c}}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E},\mathrm{id}_{A})\ , 
 
 (6.61) 
 
 where the maximization is over all decoding channels from \widetilde{B}_{S}\coloneq\bigotimes_{i\in S}\widetilde{B}_{i} to A. 
 
 
 
 The following shows that the MI contraction coefficient of the noise on t memories controls the depth at which no secret sharing scheme will be \varepsilon-recoverable for (t,m)-sharing given noisy quantum memories. 
 
 
 Theorem 11 . 
 
 Consider m users, where user j has a quantum memory described by a sequence of channels \left(\mathcal{N}_{j}^{i}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}, and define \mathcal{N}^{i}_{S}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{j\in S}\mathcal{N}_{j}^{i}. Let d\in\mathbb{N}, \delta\in(0,\frac{d-1}{2d}), and \varepsilon\in(0,\frac{d-1}{2d}-\delta). Then, for all n\in\mathbb{N} larger than 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\min\!\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\exists S\in\mathcal{S}_{t}:\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i}_{S})\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{2\ln(2)\log(\min\{|B_{S}|,|A\otimes B_{S^{c}}|\})}\right\} 
 
 (6.62) 
 
 there does not exist an encoding channel that is \varepsilon-recoverable for (t,m) sharing under noise model \mathcal{N}^{n}\coloneq\bigcirc_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n}\}\bigotimes_{j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}\mathcal{N}^{i}_{j}. 
 
 
 
 A limitation of the practicality of computing the bound in Theorem   11 is that it is expressed in terms of the contraction coefficient of product channels, i.e., \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i}_{S})=\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\bigotimes_{j\in S}N_{j}^{i}). This however makes sense from a coding theory perspective. First, a (quantum) secret sharing scheme can be built from an error-correcting code. Second, one way of seeing that contraction coefficients should not tensorize is that one can design error correcting codes to preserve the distinguishability of inputs over product channels. As such, the encoding of the secret sharing scheme may act simultaneously as a secret sharing scheme and an error correcting code for the noise in the network of memories, and this is captured in Theorem   11 . Nonetheless, one can obtain computable bounds that only look at the channels individually by using the \alpha_{+} Doeblin coefficient. 
 
 
 Corollary 15 . 
 
 Consider m users where user j has a quantum memory described by a sequence of channels \left(\mathcal{N}_{j}^{i}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}} and define \mathcal{N}^{i}_{S}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{j\in S}\mathcal{N}_{j}^{i}. Let d\in\mathbb{N}, \delta\in\left(0,\frac{d-1}{2d}\right), and \varepsilon\in\left(0,\frac{d-1}{2d}-\delta\right). Then, for all n\in\mathbb{N} larger than 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\min\!\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\exists S\in\mathcal{S}_{t}:\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left(1-\prod_{j\in S}\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}^{i}_{j})\right)\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{2\ln(2)\log(\min\{|B_{S}|,|A\otimes B_{S^{c}}|\})}\right\} 
 
 (6.63) 
 
 there does not exist an encoding channel that is \varepsilon-recoverable for (t,m) sharing under noise model \mathcal{N}^{n}\coloneq\bigcirc_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\bigotimes_{j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}\mathcal{N}^{i}_{j}. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from Theorem   11 and 
 

 
 
 \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{M})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{M})\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{M})\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N})\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{M})\ , 
 
 (6.64) 
 
 where the last inequality follows from [ 39 , Item 2 of Lemma III.8] and so if we have the stated bound, we have bounded the contraction coefficients in Theorem   11 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 We now turn to proving Theorem   11 . To do so, we begin by recalling the main definitions and lemmas of which we will make use. A state \rho_{AB} is k-extendible on B if there exists \rho_{AB_{1}B_{2}\cdots B_{k}} where B_{i}\cong B for all i\in[k], and \rho_{AB_{i}}=\rho_{AB} for all i\in[k]. A k-symmetrically extendible state \rho_{A^{\prime}A} on A where A^{\prime}\cong A has bounded overlap with the maximally entangled state (also known as singlet fraction). 
 
 
 Proposition 12 . 
 
 Let \rho_{A^{\prime}A} be k-symmetrically extendible, and let A^{\prime}\cong A. Then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\langle\Phi_{d}|\rho_{A^{\prime}A}|\Phi_{d}\rangle\leq\frac{1}{d}\left(1+\frac{d-1}{k}\right)\ , 
 
 (6.65) 
 
 where d=|A|. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let \rho_{A^{\prime}A} be k-symmetrically extendible on A. Let \rho_{A^{\prime}A_{1}A_{2}\cdots A_{k}} be its extension. Note that by partial trace 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{A^{\prime}A_{1}A_{2}\cdots A_{k}}\coloneq\int dU\left(U\otimes\bigotimes_{i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}}\overline{U}\right)\rho\left(U\otimes\bigotimes_{i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}}\overline{U}\right)^{\ast} 
 
 (6.66) 
 
 is a k-symmetric extension on A of the state 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{A^{\prime}A}\coloneq\int dU(U\otimes\overline{U})\rho(U\otimes\overline{U})^{\ast}\eqqcolon\mathcal{T}(\rho_{A^{\prime}A})\ . 
 
 (6.67) 
 
 Thus, twirling preserves k-symmetric extendibility. Now, we may write 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\langle\Phi_{d}|\rho_{A^{\prime}A}|\Phi_{d}\rangle 
 \displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|\rho_{AA^{\prime}}] 
 
 (6.68) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}[|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|\mathcal{T}(\rho_{A^{\prime}A})] 
 
 (6.69) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{d}\left(1+\frac{d-1}{k}\right), 
 
 (6.70) 
 
 where we used the invariance of the maximally entangled state, that \mathcal{T}(\rho_{A^{\prime}A}) is an isotropic state t|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|+\frac{1-t}{d^{2}-1}(I-|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|) after twirling, and that an isotropic state has parameter t at most \frac{1}{d}(1+\frac{d-1}{k}) if it is k-symmetrically extendible  [ 41 , 43 ] .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Note that k-symmetric extendibility of \rho_{AB} on the B system means that each B_{i} is equally correlated with A.
Also observe that the CMI of any extension of \rho_{AB} is an upper bound on the squashed entanglement, as follows from ( 6.36 ). These ideas are related through the fact that the squashed entanglement of a state is related to its k-extendibility, as captured in the following lemma. 
 
 
 Lemma 11 (Theorem 1 of [ 49 ] ) . 
 
 Let \rho_{AB} be a quantum state such that E_{\operatorname{sq}}(A:B)_{\rho}\leq\varepsilon. Then for every k\in\mathbb{N}, there exists a k-extendible state \sigma_{AB} such that \left\|\rho-\sigma\right\|_{1}\leq(k-1)\sqrt{2\ln(2)}\sqrt{\varepsilon}. 
 
 
 
 Now we prove the theorem. 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem   11 . 
 
 We begin with a summary of the proof. First, we show that one can bound the \varepsilon-recoverability of the encoder \mathcal{E} under a noise model \mathcal{N} by the singlet fraction of the decoded state. Second, we show that if the system after the noise is \delta-close to a 2-symmetrically extendible state, then the state will still be \delta-close to a 2-symmetrically extendible state after decoding. Combining these with Proposition   12 , if the state after the noise is (0,\frac{d-1}{2d})\ni\delta-close to a 2-symmetrically extendible state, then it is impossible for the encoding channel to be (0,\frac{d-1}{2d}-\delta)\ni\varepsilon-recoverable. Finally, we use MI contraction coefficients to control the time it takes for a noise model \mathcal{N}^{n}\coloneq\bigcirc_{i\in[n]}\bigotimes_{j\in[m]}\mathcal{N}^{i}_{j} to make the state \delta-close to a symmetrically extendible state. 
 
 
 First, we bound the \varepsilon-recoverability of a channel \mathcal{E}_{A\to B^{m}} under a noise model \mathcal{N} in terms of the singlet fraction of the decoded subset. Fix subset \widehat{S}\subset\{1,\ldots,m\} of size t. Define the state 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}=\rho_{A^{\prime}B^{m}}\coloneq(\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E})(|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|_{A^{\prime}A})\ , 
 
 (6.71) 
 
 where M=|A|.
Then, 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\min_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{t}}\max_{\mathcal{D}_{B_{S}\to A}}F(\mathcal{D}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{S^{c}}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E},\mathrm{id}_{A}) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\max_{\mathcal{D}_{B_{\widehat{S}}\to A}}F(\mathcal{D}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E},\mathrm{id}_{A}) 
 
 (6.72) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\max_{\mathcal{D}_{B_{\widehat{S}}\to A}}\inf_{\rho_{RA}}F\left((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{D}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E})(\rho_{RA}),\rho_{RA}\right) 
 
 (6.73) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\inf_{\rho_{RA}}\max_{\mathcal{D}_{B_{\widehat{S}}\to A}}F\left((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{D}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E})(\rho_{RA}),\rho_{RA}\right) 
 
 (6.74) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\max_{\mathcal{D}_{B_{\widehat{S}}\to A}}F(\mathcal{D}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E}(|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|),|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|) 
 
 (6.75) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\langle\Phi_{d}|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E}(|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|)|\Phi_{d}\rangle 
 
 (6.76) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\langle\Phi_{d}|(\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}})(\rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}})|\Phi_{d}\rangle\ , 
 
 (6.77) 
 
 where the first inequality follows from choosing the partition, the second from the max-min inequality, the third by choosing the input state to be the maximally entangled state, and the second equality is a property of the fidelity when one argument is pure as well as letting \widehat{\mathcal{D}} represent a maximizing decoding channel. 
 
 
 To bound recoverability, we want to bound ( 6.77 ). To that end, we now show \rho_{A^{\prime}A}\coloneq(\mathcal{D}\circ\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}})(\rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}) is \delta-close from a two-symmetrically extendible state so long as \rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}} is. Assume there is \sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}} that is two-symmetrically extendible on the B_{\widehat{S}} space such that \left\|\rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}-\sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}\right\|_{1}\leq\delta. Denote the extension by \sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}B_{\widehat{S}}^{\prime}}. By definition of two-symmetric extendibility, \sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}}\cong\sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}^{\prime}}, so \sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}}^{\prime}} is itself a two-symmetric extension of \sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}}. Thus, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}}-\sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}}\right\|_{1} 
 \displaystyle=\left\|\operatorname{Tr}_{B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}[\rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}-\sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}]\right\|_{1}\leq\delta\ . 
 
 (6.78) 
 
 Thus, \rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}} is \delta-close from a state that is two-symmetrically extendible. Furthermore, \sigma_{A^{\prime}AA_{2}}\coloneq(\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B_{\widehat{S}}\to A}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{B_{\widehat{S}}^{\prime}\to A})(\sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}}^{\prime}}) satisfies \sigma_{A^{\prime}A}\cong\sigma_{A^{\prime}A_{2}}, so it is two-symmetrically extendible on the A space. Thus, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\rho_{A^{\prime}A}-\sigma_{A^{\prime}A}\right\|_{1}=\left\|\mathcal{D}_{B_{\widehat{S}}}[\rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}}-\sigma_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}}]\right\|_{1}\leq\delta\ . 
 
 (6.79) 
 
 It then follows by monotonicity of the Schatten 1-norm under CPTNI maps that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle|\langle\Phi_{d}|\rho_{A^{\prime}A}|\Phi_{d}\rangle-\langle\Phi_{d}|\sigma_{A^{\prime}A}|\Phi_{d}\rangle|\leq\delta\ . 
 
 (6.80) 
 
 Applying Proposition   12 , 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle|\langle\Phi_{d}|\rho_{A^{\prime}A}|\Phi_{d}\rangle|\leq\delta+\frac{1}{d}+\frac{d-1}{2d}\ . 
 
 (6.81) 
 
 Thus, if \rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}} is \delta-close to a two-symmetrically extendible state on the A system, then the \varepsilon-recoverability of \mathcal{E} is limited by 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle 1-\varepsilon\leq\delta+\frac{1}{d}+\frac{d-1}{2d}\iff\varepsilon\geq\frac{(d-1)}{2d}-\delta\ . 
 
 (6.82) 
 
 Thus, the bounds on \delta and \varepsilon that are specified in the theorem statement are chosen so that the right-hand side of the second inequality is positive and \varepsilon does not satisfy the inequality. 
 
 
 Given the above, it suffices to guarantee that \rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}} is \delta-close to a two-symmetrically extendible state. First, define the extension 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}}\otimes|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|_{E}=\rho_{A^{\prime}B^{m}E}\coloneq(\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{E})(|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|_{A^{\prime}A}\otimes|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|_{E})\ . 
 
 (6.83) 
 
 Note that at no point does the E system become correlated with the others. We use the shorthand \mathcal{N}^{i}_{S}\coloneq\bigotimes_{j\in S}\mathcal{N}^{i}_{j}. Then, using the independence of the E system and the definition of the MI contraction coefficient, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I(B_{\widehat{S}}:AB_{\widehat{S}^{c}}|E)_{\rho} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[I(B_{\widehat{S}}:A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}E)-I(B_{\widehat{S}}:E)\right] 
 
 (6.84) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}I(B_{\widehat{S}}:A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}) 
 
 (6.85) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left[\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i}_{\widehat{S}})\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{i}_{\widehat{S}^{c}})\right]\frac{1}{2}I(B_{\widehat{S}}:A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}})_{\mathcal{E}(|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|)} 
 
 (6.86) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}\left[\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i}_{\widehat{S}})\right]\log(\min\{|B_{\widehat{S}}|,|A\otimes B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}|\})\ , 
 
 (6.87) 
 
 where we used that \eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{N})=1. 
 
 
 It follows by Lemma   11 that if ( 6.87 ) is bounded from above by \delta^{2}/2\ln(2), then \rho_{A^{\prime}B_{\widehat{S}}B_{\widehat{S}^{c}}} is \delta-close to a two-symmetrically extendible state. None of this relied on a specific choice of \widehat{S}, so one may minimize over it. It also did not depend on the channel \mathcal{E}, so once the condition is satisfied, no encoding \mathcal{E} can exist.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 6.4 Quantum channel capacities 

 
 Contraction coefficients and partial orders are closely connected to the structure of channel capacities  [ 78 , 37 , 35 , 4 ] . Here we briefly discuss some connections of our work to the existing literature. 
 
 
 The quantum capacity is given by 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle Q(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}Q^{(1)}(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes n}), 
 
 (6.88) 
 
 
 \displaystyle Q^{(1)}(\mathcal{N}) 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{\rho}\left\{H(\mathcal{N}(\rho))-H(\mathcal{N}^{c}(\rho))\right\}, 
 
 (6.89) 
 
 where the supremum is over every channel input state \rho and \mathcal{N}^{c} denotes a complementary channel of \mathcal{N} (see, e.g., [ 23 ] or ( 6.90 ) below).
Due to the regularization, an important question is for which channels the quantum capacity is additive, meaning for which channels the regularization is unnecessary. The best known condition for additivity is if the channel \mathcal{N} is degradable [ 23 ] . A potentially more general set of channels for which the quantum capacity is additive is that of informationally degradable channels, as introduced in  [ 20 ] , sometimes also referred to as fully quantum less noisy. 
 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to B}, let V_{A\to BE} be an isometry such that \mathcal{N}_{A\to B}(\cdot)=\operatorname{Tr}_{E}[V(\cdot)V^{\dagger}]. Then a complementary channel of \mathcal{N} is given by 
 

 
 
 \mathcal{N}^{c}_{A\to E}(\cdot)\coloneq\operatorname{Tr}_{B}[V(\cdot)V^{\dagger}]. 
 
 (6.90) 
 
 A channel \mathcal{N} with complementary channel \mathcal{N}^{c} is called informationally degradable if, for every state \rho_{RA}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(R:B)\geq I(R:E), 
 
 (6.91) 
 
 or equivalently, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{{\operatorname{MI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{c},\mathcal{N})\leq 1. 
 
 (6.92) 
 
 A second criterion that guarantees additivity is if \mathcal{N} is regularized less noisy, which is the case if the inequality 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(U:B^{n})\geq I(U:E^{n}) 
 
 (6.93) 
 
 holds for all n\geq 1.
The guiding question is now whether there exists a channel that is informationally degradable or regularized less noisy but not degradable. Ref.  [ 4 ] recently proposed investigating this question using relative expansion and contraction coefficients, and they reported progress by constructing a channel that is less noisy but not degradable. 
 
 
 As identified in  [ 37 ] , there is a more restrictive criterion called completely less noisy, also discussed earlier in  Section   3.4 , that implies both informational degradability and regularized less noisy.
Here we briefly give a variant of the corresponding result in  [ 4 , Proposition 6.1] that constructs a candidate channel for completely less noisy channels. 
 
 
 Proposition 13 . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N} and \mathcal{M} be degradable channels. Then for all 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle p\in\left[\frac{1}{1+\check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M})\left(1-\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}^{c},\mathcal{N})\right)},1\right], 
 
 (6.94) 
 
 where \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}^{c},\mathcal{N}) and \check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}) are defined as in ( 2.45 ) and ( 2.47 ), the quantum channel 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\Psi_{p,\mathcal{N},\mathcal{M}}=p|0\rangle\!\langle 0|\otimes\mathcal{N}+(1-p)|1\rangle\!\langle 1|\otimes\mathcal{M}^{c} 
 
 (6.95) 
 
 is completely less noisy and hence also informationally degradable and regularized less noisy. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is essentially identical to that of  [ 4 , Proposition 6.1] .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Note that an example of a completely less noisy, but not degradable, channel has recently been provided in  [ 86 ] . The construction described here could be used for a guided search for further such examples. 
 
 
 
 
 7 Applications to discrete-time quantum Markov chains 

 
 7.1 On hierarchies of operational times 

 
 A canonical application of SDPI constants involves bounding mixing times. This application has been generalized to other contraction coefficients subsequently: Ref.  [ 30 ] showed that one can generalize the notion of mixing time to ‘decoupling times’ and upper bound this time with the complete SDPI constant, or in the case of a time-inhomogeneous Markov chain, the complete contraction coefficient. In a slightly different direction, Ref.  [ 40 ] used reverse contraction coefficients to place lower bounds on mixing times. 
 
 
 In this paper we have introduced various contraction coefficients, and in principle they all capture distinct alternative notions of mixing. The alternative notions of mixing that each constant captures is straightforward, and so we only focus on the definition that directly pertains to the conditional contraction coefficient and SDPI, which we call ‘replacing.’ In particular, we show that any notion of a time homogeneous Markov chain that implies mixing and is implied by being replacing is asymptotically equivalent ( Proposition   15 ). This result follows because it turns out that these are all different ways of expressing that the channel converges to a replacer channel. Motivated by this, we show that a channel is mixing if and only if there exists a finite number of channel applications at which the quantum Doeblin coefficient becomes non-trivial ( Corollary   17 ). 
 
 
 7.1.1 Replacing, mixing, and their equivalence 

 
 A quantum homogeneous Markov chain is a process where, at each time step, the same quantum channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} occurs; i.e., for all n\in\mathbb{N}, the total process is as follows: 
 

 
 
 \mathcal{N}^{n}\coloneq\bigcirc_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{N}=\underbrace{\mathcal{N}\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{N}}_{n\text{ times}}\ . 
 
 (7.1) 
 
 We thus will talk of a ‘homogeneous Markov chain \mathcal{N}_{A\to A},’ as this completely specifies the Markov chain. We say a homogeneous Markov chain \mathcal{N} is replacing if 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA})\right\|_{1}=\left\|\rho_{R}-\sigma_{R}\right\|_{1}\quad\forall\rho_{RA},\sigma_{RA}\ . 
 
 (7.2) 
 
 We note that this definition is similar to the notion of a homogeneous Markov chain with unique fixed point \omega being ‘mixing’ [ 10 , 9 , 30 ] : 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}(\rho_{A})-\omega_{A}\right\|_{1}=0\quad\forall\rho_{A}\ . 
 
 (7.3) 
 
 
 
 The distinction between these two definitions is two-fold. First, the Markov chain being replacing involves a reference system, whereas mixing does not. Second, the Markov chain being replacing does not explicitly include a fixed point, which might make it seem that replacing ought to be measured using the conditional contraction coefficient rather than a conditional SDPI constant. However, we will show that in fact both replacing and mixing identify the same property: \mathcal{N}^{n} converging to the replacer channel 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\omega}[X]\coloneqq\operatorname{Tr}[X]\omega\ , 
 
 (7.4) 
 
 where \omega is the unique fixed point of \mathcal{N}. 
 
 
 Norm equivalences for channels 

 
 To establish our asymptotic equivalence of replacing and mixing, we require a few notions of norms on channels and their equivalence in finite dimensions. For a Hermitian-preserving map \Phi_{A\to B} we recall the 1\to 1 norm and the diamond norm (completely bounded 1\to 1 norm) 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\Phi\right\|_{1} 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\max_{\rho_{A}\in\mathcal{D}(A)}\left\|\Phi(\rho)\right\|_{1}, 
 
 (7.5) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\Phi\right\|_{\diamond} 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{|R|,\,\rho_{RA}}\left\|(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\Phi)(\rho)\right\|_{1}=\max_{|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|_{A^{\prime}A}\in\mathcal{D}(A^{\prime}\otimes A)}\left\|(\mathrm{id}_{A^{\prime}}\otimes\Phi)(|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|)\right\|_{1}\ , 
 
 (7.6) 
 
 where the maximizations always come from the sets being optimized over being compact and A^{\prime}\cong A. We also consider the norm induced by the Schatten 1-norm on the Choi operator, \left\|\Gamma^{\Phi}\right\|_{1}, where the Choi operator \Gamma^{\Phi} is defined as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\Gamma^{\Phi}\coloneqq\ d_{A}(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Phi)(|\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|)\coloneq(\mathrm{id}\otimes\Phi)(|\Gamma\rangle\!\langle\Gamma|)\ , 
 
 (7.7) 
 
 i.e., the map acting on one share of the unnormalized maximally entangled state of dimension d=|A|. 
 
 
 Proposition 14 (Equivalence of norms on Hermitian-preserving maps) . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N}_{A\to B} and \mathcal{M}_{A\to B} be completely positive maps. Then, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{M}\right\|_{1} 
 \displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{M}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq 2d_{A}\left\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{M}\right\|_{1}, 
 
 (7.8) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{M}\right\|_{\diamond} 
 \displaystyle\leq\left\|\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}-\Gamma^{\mathcal{M}}\right\|_{1}\leq d_{A}\left\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{M}\right\|_{\diamond}. 
 
 (7.9) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof of ( 7.8 ) is identical to [ 6 , Lemma 6] , which need not make an assumption that the input and output linear operators act on the same dimension of Hilbert space (so long as they are finite). The proof of ( 7.9 ) may be done identically to [ 76 , Lemma 7] by noting that it need not require the input and output spaces be the same.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 The equivalence 

 
 Proposition 15 . 
 
 For a time-homogeneous Markov chain, being mixing and being replacing are equivalent. Moreover, this means that any notion of asymptotic behaviour of a homogeneous Markov chain that is implied by replacing and implies mixing, such as being decoupling 3 3 
 3 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Decoupling means that \lim_{n\to\infty}\|(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA}-\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})\|_{1}=0 [ 30 ] , is also equivalent. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 A homogeneous Markov chain \mathcal{N} that is replacing under trace distance implies it is mixing by choosing \rho_{RA}=\rho_{R}\otimes\rho_{A} and \sigma_{RA}=\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A}. It thus suffices to prove mixing implies replacing. By the definition of mixing, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\forall\delta>0,\exists n_{\delta}\in\mathbb{N}:\forall n\geq n_{\delta}\,,\,\left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}(\rho)-\omega\right\|_{1}\leq\delta\quad\forall\rho\in\mathcal{D}(A)\ . 
 
 (7.10) 
 
 We will use this to show that \mathcal{N}^{n} converges to the replacer channel \mathcal{R}^{\omega} in diamond norm. Note that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}(\rho)-\omega\right\|_{1}=\left\|(\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega})(\rho)\right\|_{1}\ . 
 
 (7.11) 
 
 Thus, Eq.   7.10 implies that, for all \delta>0, there exists n_{\delta}\in\mathbb{N} such that for all n\geq n_{\delta}, \left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega}\right\|_{1}\leq\delta for all n\geq n_{\delta}. By the equivalence of channel norms ( Proposition   14 ), the same claim holds for diamond distance; i.e., for all \delta>0, there exists n_{\delta}\in\mathbb{N} such that for all n\geq n_{\delta}, \left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\delta, so 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega}\right\|_{\diamond}=0\ . 
 
 (7.12) 
 
 Then for all \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA}, 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})-(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\sigma_{RA})\right\|_{1}-\left\|\rho_{R}-\sigma_{R}\right\|_{1} 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\left\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes(\mathcal{N}^{n}+\mathcal{R}^{\omega}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega}))(\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA})\right\|_{1}-\left\|\rho_{R}-\sigma_{R}\right\|_{1} 
 
 (7.13) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathrm{id}\otimes(\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega})(\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA})\right\|_{1}+\left\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{R}^{\omega})(\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA})\right\|_{1}-\left\|\rho_{R}-\sigma_{R}\right\|_{1} 
 
 (7.14) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\left\|\mathrm{id}\otimes(\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega})(\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA})\right\|_{1}+\left\|\rho_{R}\otimes\omega-\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega\right\|_{1}-\left\|\rho_{R}-\sigma_{R}\right\|_{1} 
 
 (7.15) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\left\|\mathrm{id}\otimes(\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega})(\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA})\right\|_{1} 
 
 (7.16) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\omega}\right\|_{\diamond}\left\|\rho_{RA}-\sigma_{RA}\right\|_{1} 
 
 (7.17) 
 
 where the first equality follows from grouping terms and adding and subtracting a channel, the first inequality from the triangle inequality, the second equality from the action of the replacer channel, the third equality from the Schatten 1-norm being multiplicative over tensor products, \left\|\omega\right\|_{1}=1, and canceling terms, and the final inequality from the definition of the completely bounded 1-norm. Combining ( 7.12 ) and ( 7.17 ), 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}[\left\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})-(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\sigma_{RA})\right\|_{1}-\left\|\rho_{R}-\sigma_{R}\right\|_{1}]=0\ . 
 
 (7.18) 
 
 Thus, by definition, the homogeneous Markov chain \mathcal{N} is replacing. 
 
 
 Finally, if some notion implies \mathcal{N} is mixing and is implied by replacing, then one concludes that it is equivalent as mixing implies replacing.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.1.2 Finite-time bounds 

 
 As shown, a homogeneous Markov chain \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} being replacing captures that the channel converges to the replacer channel. Thus, it is natural to measure the time it takes for the Markov chain to converge in terms of how different the output of \mathcal{N}^{n} is in relation to the fixed point. We call this the \mathbb{D}-replacement time and provide the pre-existing analogous definitions for clarity. 
 
 
 Definition 14 . 
 
 Given a divergence \mathbb{D}, a quantum channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} with unique fixed point \omega, and \delta\geq 0, we define 
 
 1. 
 
 \mathbb{D} -Mixing Time: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle t_{\operatorname{mix}}^{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\delta)\coloneq\inf\!\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\mathbb{D}\!\left(\mathcal{N}^{n}(\rho),\omega\right)\leq\delta\quad\forall\rho\in\mathcal{D}(A)\right\}\ , 
 
 (7.19) 
 
 
 2. 
 
 \mathbb{D} -Decoupling Time: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle t_{\operatorname{dec}}^{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\delta)\coloneqq\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\begin{matrix}\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})\leq\delta\,,\\
\forall R\,,\,\forall\rho_{RA}\in\mathcal{D}(R\otimes A)\end{matrix}\right\}\ , 
 
 (7.20) 
 
 
 3. 
 
 \mathbb{D} -Replacement Time: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle t_{\operatorname{rep}}^{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N},\delta)\coloneqq\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\begin{matrix}\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\leq\delta\,,\\
\forall R\,,\,\forall\rho_{RA}\in\mathcal{D}(R\otimes A),\sigma_{R}\in\mathcal{D}(R)\end{matrix}\right\}\ . 
 
 (7.21) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 We explicitly specify that \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} has a unique fixed point \omega, as otherwise the above times are trivially infinite by choosing \rho_{A} to be a different fixed point. 
 
 
 We then obtain a bound on the replacement time in terms of the conditional SDPI constant, which is analogous to previous bounds for other operational times (c.f.  [ 30 , 31 ] ). 
 
 
 Proposition 16 . 
 
 Let \mathbb{D} be a divergence. Consider a homogeneous Markov chain \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} with fixed point \omega_{A}. Then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle t^{\mathbb{D}}_{\operatorname{rep}}(\mathcal{N},\delta)\leq\log(C^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}/\delta)/\log(1/\eta_{\mathbb{D}}^{\Delta,\operatorname{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\omega)), 
 
 (7.22) 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 C_{\mathbb{D}}^{\Delta}\coloneqq\sup_{\rho_{RA},\sigma_{R}}\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}). 
 
 (7.23) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We have the sequence of inequalities 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n}(\omega_{A}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 (7.24) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\mathbb{D}}^{\operatorname{\Delta,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\omega_{A})\left[\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n-1})(\rho_{RA})\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\right] 
 
 (7.25) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\mathbb{D}}^{\operatorname{\Delta,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\omega_{A})^{n}\left[\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\right] 
 
 (7.26) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{\mathbb{D}}^{\operatorname{\Delta,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\omega_{A})^{n}C^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}\ , 
 
 (7.27) 
 
 where the equality follows because \omega_{A} is a fixed point of \mathcal{N}, the first inequality from ( 3.91 ) with the replacements S\to R, R\to\mathbb{C}, A\to A, the second inequality from doing the previous inequality n-1 times, and the final inequality from the definition of C^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}. By bounding the final term from above by \delta, taking the logarithm, and solving for n completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Here we recall that by  Theorem   5 , if we choose \mathbb{D} as the relative entropy, \eta_{D}^{\operatorname{\Delta,stab}} tensorizes, implying stability under tensor powers of the channel \mathcal{N}. 
 
 
 Finally, we observe that, for the relative entropy, decoupling and replacing time become equivalent. 
 
 
 Proposition 17 . 
 
 For every channel \mathcal{N} and all \delta\in(0,\infty), 
 

 
 
 t^{D}_{\operatorname{rep}}(\mathcal{N},\delta)=t^{D}_{\operatorname{dec}}(\mathcal{N},\delta). 
 
 (7.28) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By the relative entropy chain rule ( 3.53 ), 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle D((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=D((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})+D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 (7.29) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=D((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})\ . 
 
 (7.30) 
 
 Thus 
 

 
 
 D((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\sigma_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})-D(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\leq\delta 
 
 (7.31) 
 
 if and only if 
 

 
 
 D((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A})\leq\delta, 
 
 (7.32) 
 
 which are the conditions in the definitions.
∎ 
 
 
 
 An interesting corollary of this is that relative entropy replacement time is controlled by the product SDPI constant rather than the trivially stabilized conditional SDPI constant. 
 
 
 Corollary 16 . 
 
 For a channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} with fixed point \omega>0, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle t^{D}_{\operatorname{rep}}(\mathcal{N},\delta)=t^{D}_{\operatorname{dec}}(\mathcal{N},\delta)\leq\left\lceil\frac{\log(C^{p}_{D}/\delta)}{\log(1/\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\omega))}\right\rceil\ . 
 
 (7.33) 
 
 where C^{p}_{D}\coloneq\sup_{\rho_{RA}}D(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A}). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 As \mathcal{N}(\omega)=\omega and using the definition of the product SDPI constant (see ( 2.9 )) n times, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A}) 
 \displaystyle=D((\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n})(\rho_{RA})\|\rho_{R}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{n}(\omega_{A})) 
 
 (7.34) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\left[\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\omega)\right]^{n}D(\rho_{RA}\|\rho_{R}\otimes\omega_{A}) 
 
 (7.35) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\left[\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{N},\omega)\right]^{n}C^{p}_{D}\ . 
 
 (7.36) 
 
 Bounding the right-hand side by \delta and solving for n completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 8 (Time-Inhomogeneous Markov Chains) . 
 
 In [ 30 ] , finite time bounds on time inhomogeneous Markov chains were developed using contraction coefficients. The same ideas may be applied for replacing times. However, this is repetitive and does not lead to a new insight beyond that the Markov chain must converge to a sequence of replacer channels rather than a fixed replacer channel. Thus, we omit a more in-depth discussion. The reader can work out the ideas straightforwardly by combining the above proof methods with the notion of replacing and the definition of a time-inhomogeneous Markov chain from [ 30 ] . 
 
 
 
 
 7.1.3 Doeblin coefficient is eventually non-trivial for mixing channels 

 
 In [ 30 ] , the authors considered the quantum Doeblin coefficient: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\max\{\operatorname{Tr}[H]\colon I\otimes H\leq\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}},\ H\in\operatorname{Herm}\}\ . 
 
 (7.37) 
 
 This quantity is motivated as being an efficiently computable quantity used to upper bound the trace distance contraction coefficient, \eta_{\text{Tr}}(\mathcal{N})\leq 1-\alpha(\mathcal{N}). This is valuable as the trace distance contraction coefficient is NP-hard to calculate in general [ 22 ] , and the trace distance contraction coefficient can be used to bound the speed of mixing, i.e., to establish finite-time guarantees for mixing. 
 
 
 One of the undesired aspects of the quantum Doeblin coefficient is that for a channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} such that |A|>2, it can be the case \alpha(\mathcal{N})=0 even when \eta_{\text{Tr}}(\mathcal{N})<1; i.e., it is not faithful. Because the Choi operator of a replacer channel \mathcal{R}^{\omega} is I\otimes\omega, Eq.   7.37 in effect measures ‘how much’ of a replacer map that prepares a Hermitian operator is ‘contained’ in the Choi operator of the channel. In the previous section, we showed that all variations on mixing are asymptotically equivalent in finite dimensions because the (time-homogeneous) Markov chain converges to a replacer channel. Thus, one might hope that, for a determinable number n, one can guarantee \alpha(\mathcal{N}^{n})>0. In this section we show that this is indeed the case as long as the channel when applied for d^{2} iterations is sufficiently contractive. 
 
 
 Proposition 18 . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} be a quantum channel, d\coloneq|A|, and \varepsilon>0. Then either 
 
 1. 
 
 \eta_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}^{d^{2}})>1-\varepsilon or 
 
 2. 
 
 \alpha(\mathcal{N}^{m\cdot d^{2}})>0 for all m\geq\frac{\log\left(4d^{2}(d+1)\right)}{\log\left(\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\right)} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 We remark that the first condition is natural in the sense that it has been shown that if the channel were to be mixing, then its trace distance contraction coefficient would be strictly less than one after d^{2} iterations. 
 
 
 Proposition 19 . 
 
 ( [ 68 , Theorems A.11 and B.10] ) A channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} is mixing if and only if \eta_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}^{d^{2}})<1 where d=|A|. 
 
 
 
 In particular, this allows us to conclude the following. 
 
 
 Corollary 17 . 
 
 There exists n\in\mathbb{N} such that \alpha(\mathcal{N}^{n})>0 if and only if \mathcal{N} is mixing. 
 
 
 
 We now turn to proving Proposition   18 . First, we identify bounds on the Doeblin coefficient as a function of its distance from a replacer channel. 
 
 
 Lemma 12 . 
 
 Let \mathcal{N}_{A\to A} be a channel, and let \omega be a state. If \left\|\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}-I\otimes\omega\right\|_{\infty}\leq\delta, then \alpha(\mathcal{N})\geq 1-\delta|A|. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 As \delta\geq\left\|\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}-I\otimes\omega\right\|_{\infty}, \lambda_{\min}(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}-I\otimes\omega)\geq-\delta. It follows that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}-I\otimes\omega\geq-\delta I_{AB}\iff\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}} 
 \displaystyle\geq I\otimes\omega-\delta I_{AB} 
 
 (7.38) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I_{A}\otimes(\omega-\delta I_{A})\eqqcolon I_{A}\otimes\widehat{H}\ . 
 
 (7.39) 
 
 As \widehat{H} is Hermitian and \Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}\geq I_{A}\otimes H, by ( 7.37 ), \widehat{H} is a feasible point for \alpha(\mathcal{N}^{n}). Moreover, \operatorname{Tr}[H]=1-\delta|A|. As ( 7.37 ) is a maximization, this completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Now we prove the proposition. 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition   18 . 
 
 First observe that for a fixed point \omega of \mathcal{N} and all n\in\mathbb{N}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}^{n}}-I\otimes\omega\right\|_{\infty} 
 \displaystyle\leq\left\|\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}^{n}}-I\otimes\omega\right\|_{1} 
 
 (7.40) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq d\left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\sigma}\right\|_{\diamond} 
 
 (7.41) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq 2d^{2}\left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}-\mathcal{R}^{\sigma}\right\|_{1} 
 
 (7.42) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=2d^{2}\cdot\sup_{\rho}\left\|\mathcal{N}^{n}(\rho)-\omega\right\|_{1} 
 
 (7.43) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq 4d^{2}\cdot\eta_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}^{n})\ , 
 
 (7.44) 
 
 where we used the ordering of Schatten norms, Proposition   14 , the definition of the 1\to 1 norm, the fact that \omega is a fixed point of \mathcal{N}^{n} and then the fact that \eta_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneq\sup_{\rho\neq\sigma}\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathcal{N}(\rho-\sigma)\right\|_{1}. 
 
 
 Now assume that \eta_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}^{n})\leq 1-\varepsilon. By submultiplicativity of the contraction coefficient [ 37 ] , \eta_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}^{n\cdot m})\leq\eta_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}^{n})^{m}\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{m}. Combining ( 7.44 ) with this, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left\|\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}^{n}}-I\otimes\omega\right\|_{\infty}\leq 4d^{2}(1-\varepsilon)^{m}\ . 
 
 (7.45) 
 
 So long as 4d^{2}(1-\varepsilon)^{m}\leq\frac{1}{d+1}, we obtain \alpha(\mathcal{N}^{n\cdot m})>0 by Lemma   12 . Re-arranging to isolate m completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.2 Correlation decay across a 1D open chain 

 
 The authors of  [ 42 ] identified conditions on a quantum state \rho representing a 1D-chain to be approximated by the Gibbs state of a 2-local Hamiltonian: 
 
 
 Proposition 20 (Theorem 1 of [ 42 ] ) . 
 
 Let \varepsilon\geq 0, and let \rho_{A^{m}_{1}} be a quantum state on systems A_{1}\cdots A_{m} such that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(A_{1}^{i-1}:A_{i+1}^{m}|A_{i})\leq\varepsilon\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}\ . 
 
 (7.46) 
 
 Then there exist Hermitian operators h_{i}\in\operatorname{Herm}(A_{i}\otimes A_{i+1}) such that D(\rho\|\exp(-H)/Z)\leq\varepsilon m where H=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}h_{i} and Z is the corresponding partition function. 
 
 
 
 Motivated by this, the authors of  [ 16 ] showed that, given a broadcast channel \mathcal{N}_{C\to BC}, then for A\cong C and \rho_{AB^{n}C} generated by applying \mathcal{N} first to the \overline{A} system of a maximally entangled state |\Phi_{d}\rangle\!\langle\Phi_{d}|_{A\overline{A}} where d=|A| and then to the output C system the remaining times, it is the case that I(A:C|B^{\ell}_{1})=O(\eta^{\ell}) for some \eta<1 under certain conjectures including a conditional mutual information contraction coefficient being non-trivial (see [ 16 , Proposition III.4] ). That is, under certain conjectures, the correlation from one end of the chain to the other decays exponentially in the application of the channel. 
 
 
 Here we prove a similar result that does not require the input state to be a maximally entangled state. We also show that the exponential decay holds, so long as the sequence of channels have Choi operators with a minimum eigenvalue bounded from below by a number strictly greater than zero. 
 
 
 First we recall the following chain rule. 
 
 
 Proposition 21 . 
 
 For \rho_{ABCD}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(A:BC|D)=I(A:C|D)+I(A:B|CD) 
 
 (7.47) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 This follows from just using the chain rule I(A:B|C)=I(A:BC)-I(A:C) three times: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(A:B|CD)+I(A:C|D) 
 \displaystyle=I(A:BCD)-I(A:CD)+I(A:CD)-I(A:D) 
 
 (7.48) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I(A:BCD)-I(A:D) 
 
 (7.49) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=I(A:BC|D)\ , 
 
 (7.50) 
 
 thus concluding the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Now we prove the generic decay (see also the proof of [ 16 , Proposition III.4] ). 
 
 
 Proposition 22 . 
 
 Let \left(\mathcal{N}^{i}_{\overline{A}_{i}\to A_{i}\overline{A}_{i+1}}\right)_{i\in\{1,...,m-1\}} be a tuple of quantum channels, and let \mathcal{N}^{m}_{\overline{A}_{m}\to A_{m}} be a quantum channel. Define 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{A^{m}_{0}}\coloneq\bigcirc_{i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}}(\mathrm{id}_{A^{i-1}_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{i})(\rho_{A_{0}\overline{A}_{1}})\ . 
 
 (7.51) 
 
 Then for every k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(A_{0}:A_{k}|A_{1}^{k-1}) 
 \displaystyle\leq\left[\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}}\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\right]\cdot I(A_{0}:\overline{A}_{1}) 
 
 (7.52) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\left[\prod_{i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}}\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\right]2\log\min\{|A_{0}|,|\overline{A}_{1}|\}\ . 
 
 (7.53) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First, I(A_{0}:A_{m}|A_{1}^{m-1})\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{m})I(A_{0}:\overline{A}_{m}|A_{1}^{m-1}). Now to apply data processing again, we need the A^{m-1} register together with \overline{A}_{m}. By using ( 7.47 ) with A\to A_{0}, B\to\overline{A}_{m}, C\to A_{m-1}, and D\to A^{m-2}_{1}, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(A_{0}:\overline{A}_{m}|A^{m-1}_{1}) 
 \displaystyle=I(A_{0}:\overline{A}_{m}A_{m-1}|A^{m-2}_{1})-I(A_{0}:A_{m-1}|A^{m-2}_{1}) 
 
 (7.54) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq I(A_{0}:\overline{A}_{m}A_{m-1}|A^{m-2}_{1}) 
 
 (7.55) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{m-1})I(A_{0}:\overline{A}_{m-1}|A^{m-2}_{1})\ . 
 
 (7.56) 
 
 By applying the same argument iteratively, we obtain the mutual information bound. The subsequent bound follows from [ 83 , Exercise 11.7.9] . Lastly, we can start this argument for all k\leq m, which completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 To apply this decay, one needs some guarantee that \eta_{{\operatorname{CMI}}}(\mathcal{N}^{i}) is not equal to one. By Proposition   2 , it would suffice for \alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N}^{i}) to be non-zero. A sufficient condition for this is the following. 
 
 
 Proposition 23 . 
 
 For every quantum channel \mathcal{N}_{A\to B}, 
 

 
 
 \alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N})\geq|B|\lambda_{\min}(\Gamma^{\mathcal{N}}). 
 
 (7.57) 
 
 In particular, \alpha_{+}(\mathcal{N})>0 for every channel with a full rank Choi operator. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is identical to [ 30 , Proposition 38] .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Combining this with Proposition   22 allows us to conclude the following. 
 
 
 Proposition 24 . 
 
 Consider sequences of channels (\mathcal{N}^{i}_{\overline{A}_{i}\to A_{i}\overline{A}_{i+1}}) where \lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{N}^{i})\geq\epsilon>0 for all i. Define (\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{i}\coloneq\operatorname{Tr}_{\overline{A}_{i+1}}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}) and, for every m>1, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{A^{m}_{0}}\coloneq\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{m}\circ\bigcirc_{i\in\{1,\ldots,m-1\}}(\mathrm{id}_{A^{i-1}_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{i})(\rho_{A_{0}\overline{A}_{1}})\ . 
 
 (7.58) 
 
 If all of the channels are full rank, then I(A_{0}:A_{m}|A_{1}^{m-1}) decays exponentially fast in the depth. 
 
 
 
 We remark that, in comparison to [ 16 ] , our result does not require the initial state to be one share of an entangled state, nor do we require a unitality property of the channel. It does however require the channels to have full-rank Choi states. We also highlight that since \alpha_{+} is a semidefinite program, one can in principle compute a bound on the exponential decay. 
 
 
 
 
 8 Applications to quantum machine learning 

 
 In this section, we discuss how some of the results developed in this work provide insights into various performance indicators of quantum machine learning models, including stability, generalization, and training due to the application of noisy quantum channels and algorithms. 
 
 
 8.1 Stability of quantum learning algorithms 

 
 A learning algorithm is known to
be stable if its output does not depend too much on the input data  [ 7 , 62 ] . It is also known that stability and generalization of a classical learning algorithm to new inputs are closely related, where stability implies generalization using information-theoretic tools  [ 62 ] . 
 
 
 It is an interesting research question to explore whether noisy quantum learners also provide stability and generalization in quantum learning settings. In  [ 15 ] , it was shown that the generalization error of classical-quantum learners is bounded from above by a function of the mutual information between the input and output spaces, plus a Holevo information term.
In many noisy quantum learning algorithms, stability is primarily considered in differentially private (a form of noisy) quantum models  [ 55 , 21 , 54 ] . 
 
 
 Setup : We focus on the setup considered in  [ 21 , Section III] . Fix a finite classical dataset such that
s\coloneqq(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathcal{S}\coloneqq\mathcal{Z}^{n}, z_{i}\coloneqq(x_{i},y_{i}), and s is drawn from a data distribution
p_{S}. Each data sample z_{i}\in\mathcal{Z} is encoded into a quantum state as \rho_{z_{i}}\in\mathcal{D}(\hat{T_{1}}\otimes\hat{T_{2}}), where \hat{T_{1}} and \hat{T_{2}} are quantum systems. The aggregate state for n samples of data in s is 
 

 
 
 \rho_{s}\coloneq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{z_{i}}\in\mathcal{D}(T_{1}\otimes T_{2}), 
 
 (8.1) 
 
 where T_{1}\equiv\hat{T}_{1}^{\otimes n} and T_{2}\equiv\hat{T}_{2}^{\otimes n}. 
 
 
 The quantum algorithm is carried out by a party known as the Data Processor.
The Data Processor executes a learning algorithm modeled as a
collection of quantum channels
\mathcal{N}\coloneqq\{\mathcal{N}^{(s)}\colon T_{1}\to B\}_{s\in\mathcal{S}},
where B\equiv WB^{\prime} consists of a classical hypothesis register W and a
quantum system B^{\prime}, and the register T_{2} cannot be accessed by the algorithm. Then,
the action on the full encoded state is \mathrm{id}_{T_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{(s)}. Denoting \sigma_{s}^{\mathcal{N}}\coloneqq(\mathrm{id}_{T_{2}}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{(s)})(\rho_{s}), the joint classical-quantum state after the Data Processor acts is 
 

 
 
 \sigma^{\mathcal{N}}\coloneqq\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}p_{S}(s)|s\rangle\!\langle s|\otimes\sigma_{s}^{\mathcal{N}}. 
 
 (8.2) 
 
 
 
 In  [ 55 , Section VI.B] , stability for a quantum model with classical data is quantified by Holevo information. Here, we consider the stability notion defined for a general quantum learning system in  [ 21 , Definition 1] specialized for a fixed data distribution p_{S} (note that   [ 21 , Definition 1] has a maximization over p_{S}). 
 
 
 Definition 15 (Stability) . 
 
 For \gamma\geq 0, a quantum learning algorithm \mathcal{N} is
\gamma-stable with respect to p_{S} and the collection \left(\rho_{s}\right)_{s\in\mathcal{S}} of states if 
 

 
 
 I(ST_{2};WB^{\prime})_{\sigma^{\mathcal{N}}}\leq\gamma, 
 
 (8.3) 
 
 where \sigma^{\mathcal{N}} is given by ( 8.2 ). 
 
 
 
 In prior work on the quantum setting, stability is primarily considered in differentially private quantum models  [ 55 , 21 , 54 ] .
Next, we discuss the stability of the quantum learning model where \mathcal{N}^{(s)}=\mathcal{M} for all s\in\mathcal{S}; i.e., \mathcal{N}_{(s)} is equal to the same quantum channel \mathcal{M}. This is relevant to the scenario where the learning model is fixed or pre-trained. Also, note that the setting \mathcal{N}^{(s)}\neq\mathcal{N}^{(s^{\prime})} for s\neq s^{\prime} is the general setting; we leave the analysis of this more general scenario for future work. 
 
 
 Proposition 25 . 
 
 Let \mathcal{M} be a quantum channel, and let \mathcal{N} be a quantum learning algorithm such that \mathcal{N}^{(s)}=\mathcal{M} for all s\in\mathcal{S}.
The algorithm \mathcal{N} is \gamma^{\prime}-stable if 
 

 
 
 \eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{M})\ I(ST_{2};T_{1})\leq\gamma^{\prime}. 
 
 (8.4) 
 
 Furthermore, it is \gamma^{\prime}-stable if 
 

 
 
 (1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{M}))\ I(ST_{2};T_{1})\leq\gamma^{\prime}. 
 
 (8.5) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Consider the following: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle I(ST_{2}:WB^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle=D(\sigma^{\mathcal{N}}_{ST_{2}WB^{\prime}}\|\sigma^{\mathcal{N}}_{ST_{2}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{N}}_{WB^{\prime}}) 
 
 (8.6) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{s}p_{S}(s)D(\sigma^{\mathcal{N},s}_{T_{2}WB^{\prime}}\|\sigma^{\mathcal{N},s}_{T_{2}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{N}}_{WB^{\prime}}) 
 
 (8.7) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\sum_{s}p_{S}(s)\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{M})D(\sigma^{\mathcal{N},s}_{T_{2}T1}\|\sigma^{\mathcal{N},s}_{T_{2}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{N}}_{T1}) 
 
 (8.8) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{M})\sum_{s}p_{S}(s)D(\sigma^{\mathcal{N},s}_{T_{2}T1}\|\sigma^{\mathcal{N},s}_{T_{2}}\otimes\sigma^{\mathcal{N}}_{T1}) 
 
 (8.9) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{M})I(ST_{2}:T_{1}), 
 
 (8.10) 
 
 where the first inequality follows from the application of ( 2.9 ) with the system choice being R\equiv T_{2} and A\equiv T_{1} along with \mathcal{N}_{A\to B}\coloneq\mathcal{M}_{T_{1}\to WB^{\prime}}.
With that, we arrive at the first statement. 
 
 
 Next, by the use of
\eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{M})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{M})\leq 1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{M}), which follow from Corollary   3 and ( 3.28 ), we conclude the second statement as well.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 9 . 
 
 Proposition   25 provides an approach to understanding how the encoding procedure and the design of the learning model impact the stability of the trained learning algorithm. The contraction coefficients cover the impact of the noisy learning model, while the mutual information terms capture the dependencies and correlations in the dataset and the encoding protocol. 
 
 
 If \mathcal{N}=\mathcal{P}_{d}\circ\mathcal{U}_{d}\circ\cdots\mathcal{U}_{1}\circ\mathcal{P}_{1}, where each \mathcal{P}_{i} is a quantum channel and each \mathcal{U}_{i} is a unitary channel,
then \mathcal{N} is \prod_{i=1}^{d}(1-\alpha_{+}(\mathcal{P}_{i}))I(ST_{2};T_{1})-stable, in such a way that the terms in front of the mutual information term I(ST_{2};T_{1}) decay more with increasing d. 
 
 
 
 Remark 10 (Connection to generalization error) . 
 
 We see that the stability defined in  Definition   15 has a direct connection to the generalization error from  [ 21 , Theorem 1] . Generalization error quantifies how well a model provides inference for fresh input data. Thus, generalization error in the setup we consider in  Proposition   25 scales as \sqrt{\gamma^{\prime}} with 
 

 
 
 \eta^{p}_{D}(\mathcal{M})I(ST_{2}:T_{1})\leq\gamma^{\prime}, 
 
 (8.11) 
 
 as a consequence of  [ 21 , Theorem 1] . 
 
 
 
 
 8.2 Information decay of layered quantum circuits 

 
 In training and using noisy quantum circuits for inference, it is important to understand how the output information could be utilized to infer information about the inputs or how outputs of different inputs can be distinguished.
In  [ 30 ] , the existence of noise-induced barren-plateaus and the sample complexity of error mitigation were discussed for composite noisy channels of the form: 
 

 
 
 \mathcal{M}_{d}=\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes(\mathcal{N}_{d}\circ\mathcal{U}_{d}\circ\cdots\mathcal{N}_{1}\circ\mathcal{U}_{1}), 
 
 (8.12) 
 
 where the subsystem R is noiseless.
This way when we input states \rho_{RA},\sigma_{RA}, we get \operatorname{Tr}_{A}[\mathcal{M}_{d}(\rho)]=\operatorname{Tr}_{A}[\mathcal{M}_{d}(\sigma)] whenever \rho_{R}=\sigma_{R}. However, this may not always be the case with quantum circuits. For instance, there may be noisy circuits (channels) that are of the form: 
 

 
 
 \mathcal{M}_{d}=(\mathrm{id}_{R}\otimes(\mathcal{N}_{d}\circ\mathcal{U}_{d}\circ\cdots\mathcal{N}_{1}\circ\mathcal{U}_{1}))\circ\mathcal{E}, 
 
 (8.13) 
 
 where \mathcal{E} represents a noisy encoding channel or a noisy pre-trained quantum circuit that involves decoherence, randomness, correlations, etc. In these cases we may have \rho_{R}^{\mathcal{E}}\coloneq\operatorname{Tr}_{A}[\mathcal{E}(\rho)]\neq\operatorname{Tr}_{A}[\mathcal{E}(\sigma)]\eqcolon\sigma_{R}^{\mathcal{E}}, which makes the previous analysis in  [ 30 , Section 8] not applicable. In such scenarios, the tools developed in this work will provide useful insights on the impact of the channel \mathcal{E}. 
 
 
 Consider the following: 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle T(\mathcal{M}_{d}(\rho_{RA}),\mathcal{M}_{d}(\sigma_{RA})) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}_{d})\left(T(\mathcal{M}_{d-1}(\rho_{RA}),\mathcal{M}_{d-1}(\sigma_{RA}))-T(\rho_{R}^{\mathcal{E}},\sigma_{R}^{\mathcal{E}})\right)+T(\rho_{R}^{\mathcal{E}},\sigma_{R}^{\mathcal{E}}) 
 
 (8.14) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=1}^{d}\eta^{\Delta}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}_{i})\left(T(\mathcal{E}(\rho_{RA}),\mathcal{E}(\sigma_{RA}))-T(\rho_{R}^{\mathcal{E}},\sigma_{R}^{\mathcal{E}})\right)+T(\rho_{R}^{\mathcal{E}},\sigma_{R}^{\mathcal{E}}) 
 
 (8.15) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{d}\eta^{\Delta}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}_{i})\right)T(\mathcal{E}(\rho_{RA}),\mathcal{E}(\sigma_{RA}))+\left(1-\prod_{i=1}^{d}\eta^{\Delta}_{\operatorname{Tr}}(\mathcal{N}_{i})\right)T(\rho_{R}^{\mathcal{E}},\sigma_{R}^{\mathcal{E}}). 
 
 (8.16) 
 
 If T(\rho_{R}^{\mathcal{E}},\sigma_{R}^{\mathcal{E}}) is also quite small, then similar conclusions as in  [ 30 , Section 8] can be drawn. Under that setting, an exponential number of data samples are required for error mitigation, and noisy barren plateaus also exist in the considered noisy circuit setup (see Section 8.1 and Section 8.2 in  [ 30 ] ). More generally, this would give us some insights on how we can decide on choosing circuit depth, encoder based on noisy channels to minimize information decay. 
 
 
 
 8.3 Information scrambling in quantum circuits with prior correlations 

 
 Quantum information scrambling refers to the delocalization of initially localized information across other subsystems or over the entire quantum circuit.
An important distinction is that scrambling does not necessarily destroy information. For an isolated system undergoing unitary evolution, the information remains globally recoverable, even though it may become inaccessible to observers restricted to small subsystems. This observation means that applying a contraction coefficient directly to the full unitary circuit does not provide any useful insight regarding scrambling. That is because, for every unitary channel \mathcal{U} and every divergence that satisfies unitary invaraince, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{U})=\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{U})=1. 
 
 (8.17) 
 
 With that, we investigate scrambling through reduced channels describing the information accessible to restricted observers, which may be of relevance for distributed quantum circuits. Approaches along these lines have been studied for example in  [ 24 ] . 
 
 
 Let us consider the following setting: Consider a quantum state \rho_{RAC}. System A is passed through the quantum circuit (channel) denoted by \mathcal{E}_{A\to B_{1}\cdots B_{n}}. System C is inaccessible at the output of the quantum circuit, while system R corresponds to prior correlations within subsystems. We want to study the information accessible to a collection of B_{i} about C given access to R. With that in mind we define the following: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{S} 
 \displaystyle\coloneq\operatorname{Tr}_{S^{c}}\circ\mathcal{E}, 
 
 (8.18) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\Gamma_{m} 
 \displaystyle\coloneq\sup_{S\in\mathcal{R}_{m}}\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{S}), 
 
 (8.19) 
 
 where \mathcal{R}_{m}\coloneq\{S\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}:|S|=m\} for m\leq n.
With that, we arrive at the following for all S\in\mathcal{R}_{m}: 
 

 
 
 I(C:\cup_{i\in S}B_{i}|R)\leq\Gamma_{m}\ I(C:A|R). 
 
 (8.20) 
 
 by application of the conditional contraction coefficient. 
 
 
 To differentiate the information loss and information scrambling across subsystems we could use the assistance of the two quantities \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{E}) and \Gamma_{m}. Note that 
 

 
 
 \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{N}_{S})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{E}) 
 
 (8.21) 
 
 by the concatenation property in ( 2.23 ) since \mathcal{N}_{S}=\operatorname{Tr}_{S^{c}}\circ\mathcal{E} and \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\cdot)\leq 1.
If \Gamma_{m} is small and \eta^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{E}) is closer to one, then information survives globally for some input states but is hidden from small regions corresponding to \mathcal{R}_{m}, showcasing a regime where scrambling occurs. If both of them are small, then the circuit has destroyed the information rather than delocalizing it.
Furthermore, if \Gamma_{m} is small and \check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{E}) is closer to one, the information survives globally for all input states but is hidden from all small regions corresponding to \mathcal{R}_{m}. However, it is not always the case that \check{\eta}^{\Delta}_{D}(\mathcal{E}) is closer to one. 
 
 
 
 
 9 Conclusion 

 
 Our paper provided an in-depth investigation of contraction of quantum divergences in settings with reference systems. Such problems naturally appear in network and multi-user settings. While many of the quantities considered here reduce to the usual contraction coefficient in the classical setting, quantum references exhibit a rich structure that shows a clear departure from the classical setting. 
 
 
 We also reported progress in the study of SDPI constants. Interestingly, while relative-entropy SDPI constants, and more generally many f-divergence SDPI constants, tensorize classically, the commonly considered classical-quantum SDPI constants do not tensorize in general. In contrast, fully quantum mutual information and conditional mutual information SDPI constants recover the tensorization property. This suggests that tensorization in the quantum setting depends crucially on how quantum side information is incorporated, and that fully quantum formulations can possess favorable compositional properties absent from their classical-quantum counterparts (with related tensor-stable constructions appearing, e.g., in  [ 27 , 28 ] ). 
 
 
 Finally, we presented several applications. Although our list is extensive, it is certainly not exhaustive, and we expect our framework to find use in many places where one cares about information decay in multi-user quantum settings.
