More broadly, these results point towards a compositional theory of information loss in quantum networks, in which contraction properties of individual noisy components can be used to control the propagation and lifetime of information in larger quantum systems. A central question going forward is therefore to understand precisely when such local-to-global principles hold in the presence of quantum side information, in particular also in the setting of SDPI constants. 
 
 
 To end, we briefly state some open problems: 
 
 • 
 
 When considering the hockey-stick divergence, can the optimization in the contraction coefficient be restricted to orthogonal states, similar to  Proposition   8 ? This would directly imply that the associated contraction coefficient is monotone in \gamma, which in turn would have applications similar to the results in  [ 38 ] . 
 
 • 
 
 Can we find a concrete example such that \eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})<\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}) for some faithful divergence \mathbb{D}? A particularly interesting case would be that of the relative entropy. Although it would be natural to conjecture that such an example exists, the unbounded reference systems make it difficult to confirm. 
 
 • 
 
 Although most basic questions about tensorization now appear to be resolved, it would still be interesting to characterize more precisely when quantities such as the relative entropy SDPI tensorize or fail to tensorize, and which structural properties of the divergence and the allowed side information determine this behavior. 
 
 • 
 
 We did not touch on non-linear strong data-processing inequalities with reference systems (e.g., as studied in  [ 53 ] ). This could be a natural future direction.

 Appendix A Contraction coefficients with classical reference systems 

 
 In this appendix, we begin by defining various contraction coefficients that involve classical reference systems only, and after that, we prove that the resulting contraction coefficients are actually equal to the standard one. As such, there is no need to consider complete or conditional contraction coefficients when the reference system is classical. 
 
 
 Definition 16 (Generalized contraction coefficient with classical reference) . 
 
 For a quantum channel \mathcal{N} and a generalized divergence \mathbb{D}, we define the complete contraction coefficient with classical reference 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D},\operatorname{CQ}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{R}=\sigma_{R},|R|,\\
\rho_{RA},\sigma_{RA}\in\operatorname{CQ}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})}, 
 
 (A.1) 
 
 where CQ denotes the set of classical–quantum states.
We also define the conditional contraction coefficient, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D},\operatorname{CQ}}(\mathcal{N})\coloneqq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|R|,\,\rho_{RA}\neq\sigma_{RA},\\
\rho_{RA},\sigma_{RA}\in\operatorname{CQ}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}. 
 
 (A.2) 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma 13 . 
 
 Let \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA} be classical-quantum states of the following form: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{RA} 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{r}p(r)|r\rangle\!\langle r|_{R}\otimes\rho^{r}_{A}, 
 
 (A.3) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\sigma_{RA} 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{r}q(r)|r\rangle\!\langle r|_{R}\otimes\sigma^{r}_{A}, 
 
 (A.4) 
 
 where p(r) and q(r) are probability distributions and \left(\rho^{r}_{A}\right)_{r} and \left(\sigma^{r}_{A}\right)_{r} are tuples of quantum states.
Let \mathbb{D} be a divergence with the extended direct-sum property, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})=\sum_{r}g(p(r),q(r))\mathbb{D}(\rho_{A}^{r}\|\sigma_{A}^{r})+\mathbb{D}(p\|q), 
 
 (A.5) 
 
 where g(\cdot,\cdot) is a positive function. Then
the following equalities hold: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{\mathbb{D},\operatorname{CQ}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D},\operatorname{CQ}}(\mathcal{N})=\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D},\operatorname{CQ}}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (A.6) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The result follows from applying the extended direct-sum property and the fact that \mathbb{D}(p\|q)=\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}): 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{r}g(p(r),q(r))\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho^{r}_{A})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma^{r}_{A}))+\mathbb{D}(p\|q)-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R}) 
 
 (A.7) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{r}g(p(r),q(r))\mathbb{D}(\mathcal{N}(\rho^{r}_{A})\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma^{r}_{A})) 
 
 (A.8) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\leq\sum_{r}g(p(r),q(r))\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\mathbb{D}(\rho^{r}_{A}\|\sigma^{r}_{A}) 
 
 (A.9) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\left[\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})\right]. 
 
 (A.10) 
 
 Thus, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{D}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\rho_{RA})\|(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathcal{N})(\sigma_{RA}))-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}{\mathbb{D}(\rho_{RA}\|\sigma_{RA})-\mathbb{D}(\rho_{R}\|\sigma_{R})}\leq\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}). 
 
 (A.11) 
 
 Since this inequality holds for all classical-quantum states \rho_{RA} and \sigma_{RA}, we conclude that \eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D},\operatorname{CQ}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N}). We conclude the proof after combining this inequality with \eta_{\mathbb{D}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{c}_{\mathbb{D},\operatorname{CQ}}(\mathcal{N})\leq\eta^{\Delta}_{\mathbb{D},\operatorname{CQ}}(\mathcal{N}), which are direct consequences of definitions.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Note that the extended direct sum property required in  Lemma   13 is satisfied by Umegaki and Belavkin–Staszewski quantum relative, with g(p(r),q(r))=p(r), and many quantum \alpha-Hellinger divergences, with g(p(r),q(r))=p(r)^{\alpha}q(r)^{1-\alpha}, see for example  [ 44 ] .
However, it does not include other common divergences, such as the trace distance or the hockey stick divergences. 
 
 
 
 Appendix B Limit property of relative entropy and generalized divergences 

 
 Lemma 14 . 
 
 Let \rho and \sigma be full-rank states, define 
 

 
 
 \rho(\lambda)\coloneqq\frac{\sigma-\lambda\rho}{1-\lambda}, 
 
 (B.1) 
 
 where \lambda\in\left[0,1\right] is chosen such that \rho(\lambda)
is a full-rank state. Then 
 

 
 
 \left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}D(\rho(\lambda)\|\sigma)\right|_{\lambda=0}=0. 
 
 (B.2) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Using the facts that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\operatorname{Tr}[f(A(x))] 
 \displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[f^{\prime}(A(x))\frac{\partial}{\partial x}A(x)\right], 
 
 (B.3) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(x\ln x\right) 
 \displaystyle=\ln x+1, 
 
 (B.4) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\rho(\lambda) 
 \displaystyle=\frac{\sigma-\rho}{\left(1-\lambda\right)^{2}}, 
 
 (B.5) 
 
 consider that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}D(\rho(\lambda)\|\sigma) 
 \displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\operatorname{Tr}[\rho(\lambda)\ln\rho(\lambda)]-\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\operatorname{Tr}[\rho(\lambda)\ln\sigma] 
 
 (B.6) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[\left(\ln\rho(\lambda)+I\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\rho(\lambda)\right]-\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\rho(\lambda)\right)\ln\sigma\right] 
 
 (B.7) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[\left(\ln\rho(\lambda)+I\right)\left(\frac{\sigma-\rho}{\left(1-\lambda\right)^{2}}\right)\right]-\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[\left(\frac{\sigma-\rho}{\left(1-\lambda\right)^{2}}\right)\ln\sigma\right] 
 
 (B.8) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(1-\lambda\right)^{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\!\left[\left(\ln\rho(\lambda)-\ln\sigma\right)\left(\sigma-\rho\right)\right]. 
 
 (B.9) 
 
 After observing that \lim_{\lambda\to 0}\rho(\lambda)=\sigma, we
conclude the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 In the main text, two different manifestations of the above condition appear. Here we clarify that one does imply the other, even for general divergences. 
 
 
 Lemma 15 . 
 
 Let \rho and \sigma be full-rank states and suppose that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\mathbb{D}(\lambda\tau+(1-\lambda)\sigma\|\sigma)\right|_{\lambda=0^{+}}=0 
 
 (B.10) 
 
 for every state \tau.
Then, for 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho(\lambda)\coloneqq\frac{\sigma-\lambda\rho}{1-\lambda}, 
 
 (B.11) 
 
 we have 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\mathbb{D}(\rho(\lambda)\|\sigma)\right|_{\lambda=0^{+}}=0. 
 
 (B.12) 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since \sigma is full rank, there exists \varepsilon>0 sufficiently
small such that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\tau\coloneqq(1+\varepsilon)\sigma-\varepsilon\rho 
 
 (B.13) 
 
 is a quantum state. Then 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho(\lambda) 
 \displaystyle=\sigma+\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}(\sigma-\rho) 
 
 (B.14) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\sigma+\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon(1-\lambda)}(\tau-\sigma) 
 
 (B.15) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=(1-\mu(\lambda))\sigma+\mu(\lambda)\tau, 
 
 (B.16) 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mu(\lambda)\coloneqq\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon(1-\lambda)}. 
 
 (B.17) 
 
 For all sufficiently small \lambda\geq 0, also \mu(\lambda)\geq 0,
with \mu(0)=0 and \mu^{\prime}(0)=1/\varepsilon. Hence, by the chain rule, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\mathbb{D}(\rho(\lambda)\|\sigma)\right|_{\lambda=0^{+}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}\mathbb{D}((1-\mu)\sigma+\mu\tau\|\sigma)\right|_{\mu=0^{+}}=0, 
 
 (B.18) 
 
 where the final equality follows from the assumed vanishing first
derivative property.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix C Failure of tensorization for the stabilized complete SDPI 

 
 In this section, we prove that the stabilized complete SDPI constant does not tensorize. 
 
 
 Proposition 26 . 
 
 There exist a quantum channel \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B and a state
\sigma_{RA} such that 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}^{c,\mathrm{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\frac{1}{2}<\eta_{D}^{c,\mathrm{stab}}\!\left(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes 2},\sigma_{RA}^{\otimes 2}\right). 
 
 (C.1) 
 
 Consequently, the stabilized complete relative-entropy SDPI constant
does not tensorize in general. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let R be a qubit and let A have orthonormal basis
\{|x\rangle\}_{x=0}^{3}. Define 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{0} 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq|+\rangle\!\langle+|, 
 \displaystyle\omega_{1} 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq|-\rangle\!\langle-|, 
 \displaystyle\omega_{2} 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq|0\rangle\!\langle 0|, 
 \displaystyle\omega_{3} 
 \displaystyle\coloneqq|1\rangle\!\langle 1|, 
 
 (C.2) 
 
 where |\pm\rangle=(|0\rangle\pm|1\rangle)/\sqrt{2}. The crucial relation is 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}=I_{R}. 
 
 (C.3) 
 
 Set 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\xi_{x}\coloneqq\omega_{x}\otimes|x\rangle\!\langle x|_{A},\qquad\sigma_{RA}\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}\sum_{x=0}^{3}\xi_{x}, 
 
 (C.4) 
 
 so that \sigma_{R}=I_{R}/2. Let \mathcal{N}\colon A\to B record whether the input is equal to 2: 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\rho)\coloneqq\operatorname{Tr}[|2\rangle\!\langle 2|\rho]\ |1\rangle\!\langle 1|_{B}+\operatorname{Tr}[(I-|2\rangle\!\langle 2|)\rho]\ |0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{B}. 
 
 (C.5) 
 
 We use the following standard identity for states that are block
diagonal in the same classical basis: 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle D\!\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}\rho_{i}\otimes|i\rangle\!\langle i|\middle\|\sum_{i}q_{i}\sigma_{i}\otimes|i\rangle\!\langle i|\right)=D(p\|q)+\sum_{i}p_{i}D(\rho_{i}\|\sigma_{i}). 
 
 (C.6) 
 
 
 
 
 One copy. 

 
 Fix an arbitrary stabilizing system S and state \tau_{S}. Since
\mathcal{N} depends only on the diagonal entries of A, dephasing A
leaves the numerator of the complete-SDPI ratio unchanged and cannot
increase its denominator. Hence it suffices to consider a dephased
state. 
 
 
 The x-th block of the fixed second state
\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA} is
\frac{1}{4}\tau_{S}\otimes\omega_{x}. Finiteness of the relative entropy
therefore requires the corresponding SR-block of the first state to
be supported on 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\operatorname{supp}(\tau_{S})\otimes\operatorname{supp}(\omega_{x}). 
 
 (C.7) 
 
 Since \omega_{x} is pure, its support on R is one-dimensional.
Thus the SR-block is necessarily a product state, and we may write 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{SRA}=\sum_{x=0}^{3}p_{x}\rho_{S}^{x}\otimes\xi_{x}, 
 
 (C.8) 
 
 where (p_{x})_{x} is a probability distribution and the \rho_{S}^{x} are
normalized states.
The equal-reference-marginal condition is 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\sum_{x=0}^{3}p_{x}\rho_{S}^{x}\otimes\omega_{x}=\tau_{S}\otimes\frac{I_{R}}{2}. 
 
 (C.9) 
 
 Writing the four states in the Pauli basis, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{0} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(I_{R}+X_{R}), 
 \displaystyle\omega_{1} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(I_{R}-X_{R}), 
 
 (C.10) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{2} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(I_{R}+Z_{R}), 
 \displaystyle\omega_{3} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(I_{R}-Z_{R}), 
 
 (C.11) 
 
 the left-hand side of
( C.9 ) becomes 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{x}p_{x}\rho_{S}^{x}\right)\otimes I_{R}+\frac{1}{2}(p_{0}\rho_{S}^{0}-p_{1}\rho_{S}^{1})\otimes X_{R} 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}(p_{2}\rho_{S}^{2}-p_{3}\rho_{S}^{3})\otimes Z_{R}. 
 
 (C.12) 
 
 Here X_{R} and Z_{R} are the usual Pauli matrices acting on R.
Since I_{R}, X_{R}, and Z_{R} are linearly independent, their
operator-valued coefficients on S must agree separately with those
on the right-hand side of
( C.9 ). Hence 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle p_{0}\rho_{S}^{0} 
 \displaystyle=p_{1}\rho_{S}^{1}, 
 \displaystyle p_{2}\rho_{S}^{2} 
 \displaystyle=p_{3}\rho_{S}^{3}, 
 \displaystyle\sum_{x}p_{x}\rho_{S}^{x} 
 \displaystyle=\tau_{S}. 
 
 (C.13) 
 
 Taking traces, we can therefore write, for some \alpha\in[0,1], 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{SRA} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2}\rho_{S}^{+}\otimes(\xi_{0}+\xi_{1})+\frac{1-\alpha}{2}\rho_{S}^{-}\otimes(\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}), 
 
 (C.14) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\tau_{S} 
 \displaystyle=\alpha\rho_{S}^{+}+(1-\alpha)\rho_{S}^{-}. 
 
 (C.15) 
 
 Applying ( C.6 ) to the four
orthogonal values of A gives 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle D(\rho_{SRA}\|\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA})=\alpha D(\rho_{S}^{+}\|\tau_{S})+(1-\alpha)D(\rho_{S}^{-}\|\tau_{S})+D\!\left((\alpha,1-\alpha)\middle\|\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\right). 
 
 (C.16) 
 
 Using ( C.3 ), the two output
states can be written as 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\rho_{SRA}) 
 \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2}\rho_{S}^{+}\otimes\omega_{2}\otimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{B}+\frac{1-\alpha}{2}\rho_{S}^{-}\otimes\omega_{2}\otimes|1\rangle\!\langle 1|_{B} 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\tau_{S}\otimes\omega_{3}\otimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{B}, 
 
 (C.17) 
 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\tau_{S}\otimes\omega_{2}\otimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{B}+\frac{1}{4}\tau_{S}\otimes\omega_{2}\otimes|1\rangle\!\langle 1|_{B} 
 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\tau_{S}\otimes\omega_{3}\otimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{B}. 
 
 (C.18) 
 
 The three terms in each expression have mutually orthogonal supports.
Applying ( C.6 ) to these three
orthogonal sectors gives 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D\!\left(\mathcal{N}(\rho_{SRA})\middle\|\mathcal{N}(\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA})\right)=\frac{1}{2}D(\rho_{SRA}\|\tau_{S}\otimes\sigma_{RA}). 
 
 (C.19) 
 
 The value 1/2 is attained by 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho_{SRA}=\frac{1}{2}\tau_{S}\otimes(\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}). 
 
 (C.20) 
 
 Since S and \tau_{S} were arbitrary, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}^{\mathrm{C,stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA})=\frac{1}{2}. 
 
 (C.21) 
 
 
 
 
 Two copies. 

 
 For 0<\varepsilon<1/2, consider the state 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho^{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\sigma_{RA}^{\otimes 2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{16}\Big[(\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}-\xi_{3})\otimes\xi_{2}+\xi_{2}\otimes(\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}-\xi_{3})\Big]. 
 
 (C.22) 
 
 The reference marginal is unchanged because 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\omega_{0}+\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}-\omega_{3}=0. 
 
 (C.23) 
 
 Hence 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\rho^{\varepsilon}_{R_{1}R_{2}}=\sigma_{R_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{R_{2}}, 
 
 (C.24) 
 
 so \rho^{\varepsilon} is admissible for the two-copy complete SDPI.
The state \sigma_{RA}^{\otimes 2} corresponds to the uniform
distribution over the 16 classical label pairs (x,y). The
perturbation in ( C.22 ) changes this
distribution by the relative amounts 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle+\varepsilon 
 \displaystyle\quad\text{for }(0,2),(1,2),(2,0),(2,1), 
 
 (C.25) 
 
 
 \displaystyle-\varepsilon 
 \displaystyle\quad\text{for }(3,2),(2,3), 
 
 (C.26) 
 
 
 \displaystyle-2\varepsilon 
 \displaystyle\quad\text{for }(2,2), 
 
 (C.27) 
 
 and leaves all other probabilities unchanged. This also makes
positivity clear for 0<\varepsilon<1/2.
Since the conditional quantum state
\omega_{x}\otimes\omega_{y} associated with each label pair is the same
in \rho^{\varepsilon} and \sigma_{RA}^{\otimes 2}, their relative
entropy is simply the classical relative entropy of these label
distributions. We use the elementary expansion 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D\!\left(\big(q_{i}(1+\varepsilon a_{i})\big)_{i}\middle\|(q_{i})_{i}\right)=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\sum_{i}q_{i}a_{i}^{2}+O(\varepsilon^{3}), 
 
 (C.28) 
 
 valid when \sum_{i}q_{i}a_{i}=0. Since every label pair has probability
1/16 under the uniform distribution, we obtain 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D(\rho^{\varepsilon}\|\sigma_{RA}^{\otimes 2}) 
 \displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{32}\left(4\cdot 1^{2}+2\cdot(-1)^{2}+(-2)^{2}\right)+O(\varepsilon^{3}) 
 
 (C.29) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\frac{5}{16}\varepsilon^{2}+O(\varepsilon^{3}). 
 
 (C.30) 
 
 
 
 At the output, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\xi_{0}+\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}-\xi_{3})=\omega_{2}\otimes\left(|0\rangle\!\langle 0|-|1\rangle\!\langle 1|\right)_{B}, 
 
 (C.31) 
 
 while 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\xi_{2})=\omega_{2}\otimes|1\rangle\!\langle 1|_{B}. 
 
 (C.32) 
 
 Therefore, 
 

 
 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\otimes 2}(\rho^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})^{\otimes 2}=\frac{\varepsilon}{16}\omega_{2}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\left(|01\rangle\!\langle 01|+|10\rangle\!\langle 10|-2|11\rangle\!\langle 11|\right)_{B_{1}B_{2}}. 
 
 (C.33) 
 
 Furthermore, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA}) 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\omega_{2}\otimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{B}+\frac{1}{4}\omega_{2}\otimes|1\rangle\!\langle 1|_{B}+\frac{1}{2}\omega_{3}\otimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{B}. 
 
 (C.34) 
 
 Thus the three mutually orthogonal states appearing in
( C.33 ) each have probability 1/16
under the unperturbed two-copy output. Their relative perturbations are
+\varepsilon,+\varepsilon,-2\varepsilon, respectively. Applying
( C.28 ) again gives 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle D\!\left(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes 2}(\rho^{\varepsilon})\middle\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})^{\otimes 2}\right) 
 \displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{32}\left(1^{2}+1^{2}+(-2)^{2}\right)+O(\varepsilon^{3}) 
 
 (C.35) 
 
 
 
 \displaystyle=\frac{3}{16}\varepsilon^{2}+O(\varepsilon^{3}). 
 
 (C.36) 
 
 Consequently, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\frac{D\!\left(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes 2}(\rho^{\varepsilon})\middle\|\mathcal{N}(\sigma_{RA})^{\otimes 2}\right)}{D(\rho^{\varepsilon}\|\sigma_{RA}^{\otimes 2})}=\frac{3}{5}>\frac{1}{2}. 
 
 (C.37) 
 
 Hence, for all sufficiently small \varepsilon>0, 
 

 
 
 \displaystyle\eta_{D}^{\mathrm{C,stab}}\!\left(\mathcal{N}^{\otimes 2},\sigma_{RA}^{\otimes 2}\right)>\frac{1}{2}=\eta_{D}^{c,\mathrm{stab}}(\mathcal{N},\sigma_{RA}), 
 
 (C.38) 
 
 which completes the proof.
∎

 Instructions for reporting errors 
 We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
 support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
 methods listed below: 
 
 Click the "Report Issue" ( 
 
 ) button, located in the page header. 
 
 Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. 
 Our team has already identified the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
 may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
 should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
 all. 
 Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion , and welcome developer contributions .
